# AVC 150 Multimedia Storytelling · AI storytelling build (exploring)

Status: full course draft 5 built by the synthetic panels (23 Oct 2026), waiting on Michelle’s review and sign-off. Review after the October break.

## The plan

- New GCC section of AVC150.
- Added to the Digital Media Arts and Animation programs as the option in place of the journalism course in that slot now (confirm which code).
- First offered **Spring 2028**.

## The idea

Rebuild AVC 150 so the story is told through an AI the students make. Students collect the raw material of a story (interviews, photos, audio, writing, objects), then build a bot that tells it, or a character inside it that people can talk to. Could be a small language model, a chatbot, a Gemini Gem, or something else. Format still open.

**Whose story:** a story that is personal to the student but not necessarily about them: a family member’s, a community member’s, or a place or object they have a connection to. Same as Kurzweil telling her grandfather’s story. Consent from whoever’s story it is becomes part of the assignment.

Where the idea came from: Amy Kurzweil’s graphic memoir *Artificial: A Love Story* (Catapult, 2023). Her father builds a chatbot of her grandfather Fred from his archive of letters, music and writing, and she works through the family story by talking with it and drawing it. It’s the whole course in one book: collect the archive, build the teller, then make a multimedia story about what the bot gets right and wrong. https://amykurzweil.com/

## What the course bank says

- **AVC150 Multimedia Storytelling**, 3 credits, no prerequisites. Gen ed: AHM and HU.
- Official description: students find and develop their personal stories, using digital technology to create, edit, produce and archive a multimedia story.
- Current course record runs 2026 Fall to current (Governing Board approved 27 Feb 2024).
- It’s a choice course in the district’s Animation and Time-Based Media AAS, the Animation CCL, the Time-Based Media CCL, and the Applied Storytelling certificate (Estrella Mountain, South Mountain).
- Lec + lab, 6 periods, occupational subject type.

### Official competencies (MCCCD)

1. Present an overview of storytelling, including elements of the story, the power of the story and a variety of ways to present a story. (I)
2. Explain the impact of voice, place and culture in storytelling. (I)
3. Describe the importance of effectively blending language, image and power in the creation and production of a personal story. (I, II, III)
4. Identify, describe and apply the basic principles, methods and techniques used to craft a personal story. (II)
5. Identify, develop and present the elements of the personal story for peer review. (II)
6. Edit and finalize the story in preparation for the creation of a digital story. (II)
7. Use basic design principles to lay out and produce a storyboard for the personal story. (III)
8. Use computer hardware and software, tools, commands and procedures to manipulate graphics, video and sound in producing and editing the digital story. (III)
9. Edit and produce a short digital personal story. (III)
10. Archive the story on relevant technology. (III)
11. Present the digital story for peer review. (III)

The district note says faculty keep authority over pedagogy, methods, sequencing and assessment.

### Official outline (MCCCD)

- **I. Introduction to Storytelling:** components of the story; significance of the story (voice, place, culture); blending language, image and power.
- **II. Crafting a Personal Story:** creating a journal (people, places, events, memories); writing for self-discovery; identifying elements of the story (text, voice, images); peer editing; finalizing the storyline.
- **III. Creating the Digital Story:** storyboarding (methods, design principles, layout, production); scanning and preparing images; sound and digital video; editing and producing the digital story (graphics, visuals, sound, timing); archiving a digital story; presenting a digital story.

### How the AI idea fits the competencies

- **The bot is the archive (competency 10).** “Archive the story on relevant technology” is the opening: a Gemini Notebook grounded in the student’s collected material is an interactive archive. This is the strongest hook and needs no curriculum change.
- **The bot can’t replace the digital story (competencies 8 and 9).** Students still edit and produce a short digital story with graphics, video and sound. The bot comes after, or alongside, as the archive and an interactive way to present it (competency 11).
- **The journal becomes the archive (outline II.A).** People, places, events and memories are exactly what students collect and upload.
- **Whose story fits “personal” (competencies 3 to 9, outline II.B).** Personal means personal to the student, not only about them. Telling a grandparent’s or community member’s story still leads to self-discovery, which is the arc of Kurzweil’s book.

## Open questions, most important first

1. Which journalism course does AVC150 replace, and what deadline does the program change need to hit to be in the catalog for Spring 2028?
2. ~~Do the competencies leave room for the bot?~~ Answered 23 Oct: yes, as the archive (competency 10), but every section still produces a short digital story (competency 9).
3. Dual enrollment: a no-prereq gen ed course will pull under-18 students. Our own Gemini under-18 study shows what Gemini blocks for those accounts. Plan for it.

## Weak points to solve before building

- **A model trained from scratch is the weakest version for this course.** A tiny model (nanoGPT-style) trained on one student’s pile of material copies style, not content: repetition, made-up words, no memory of the facts. It also turns a no-prereq humanities course into a Python and GPU course.
- **The competencies still rule.** “Create, edit, produce and archive a multimedia story” has to be met. The bot works best as the container for the story, not a replacement for it.
- **Personal stories plus AI tools = privacy.** Students would upload family interviews and photos. Use district-approved tools (Gemini student accounts) and teach consent for anyone recorded. Character.ai is not FERPA compliant; skip it.
- **Grading a bot is hard.** Grade what students author (archive, persona design, script, test log), not how clever the bot sounds.
- **Another new course build.** The UX course is already parked until January.

## Ways to do it, lowest lift first

| Option | What students make | Tools | Pros | Cons |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **1. Story archive bot** | Collect the archive, upload it as the bot’s knowledge, write the narrator’s instructions so it tells the story from the material | Gemini Notebook (formerly NotebookLM) (Gemini student accounts, Google Classroom can assign Gems) | Low tech, fits “archive a multimedia story,” the collecting is the storytelling | Bot can wander from the source; under-18 limits |
| **2. Character interview** | Write a character bible; the bot plays the character; classmates interview it | Gemini Notebook, or a Gemini skill once available | Strong on character and worldbuilding | Mostly text; needs a rubric for the interview |
| **3. Bot as narrator or game master** | Branching story with rules; the bot runs it, the reader chooses | Gemini Notebook or a Gemini skill, or Twine with AI help | Teaches story structure and branching | Easy to drift into game design |
| **4. Talk-to-the-story, video version** | Record short video or audio answers to 30 to 50 questions; the bot picks the right clip for each question | Premiere/Audition for the clips, a Gemini Notebook or small web page as the router | Most multimedia; the student’s own voice; the AI routes, it doesn’t write. The Dimensions in Testimony model, scaled down | Most production time; the router needs building |
| **5. Train-a-tiny-model lab** | One week: train a small model on a chosen text and see what it learns | nanoGPT-style notebook, Google Colab | Demystifies AI; strong reflection piece | Not a storytelling tool; keep it to one lab |

**Leaning:** 1 and 4 as the spine (collect, then build the teller), 2 as a mid-semester project, 5 as one lab. Not decided.

## Who else is doing something like this

- **Amy Kurzweil, *Artificial: A Love Story* (Catapult, 2023).** Graphic memoir about her father building an AI chatbot of her grandfather from his archive of writing. The origin of this course idea and a strong anchor text for options 1 and 2. A New Yorker and Kirkus best book of 2023. She also has a Teaching page and a blog post, “Learning to Dance with AI.” https://amykurzweil.com/ · NPR interview: https://www.npr.org/transcripts/g-s1-44420
- **USC Shoah Foundation, Dimensions in Testimony.** Survivors answered up to about 2,000 questions on camera; language technology matches a visitor’s question to the best recorded answer. The model for option 4. https://sfi.usc.edu/dit
- **Erasmus University Rotterdam, “Create Your Own ChatGPT”** (João Gonçalves and Sarah Young). Undergrads train a language model from scratch on texts they choose, then write a reflective report. The model for option 5. https://wacclearinghouse.org/repository/collections/continuing-experiments/august-2024/ai-literacy/create-your-own-chatgpt/
- **Harvard, Nicole Mills, “Paris in Virtual Reality.”** Second-semester French students wrote character profiles that became prompts for AI characters, then interrogated them in a murder mystery. Close to option 2. https://exploringaipedagogy.hcommons.org/2023/11/29/ai-as-character/
- **Iowa State, ENGL 222X AI and Writing.** Final projects included an interactive AI storyteller app, D&D campaigns and world bibles. https://abramanders.substack.com/p/generative-ai-creative-projects
- **Centre for Digital Media (Vancouver), Master of Digital Media.** Student team built AI game characters that drive the story, with Inworld AI as industry partner. https://thecdm.ca/news/inworldai-industry-project-a-high
- **WashU Center for Teaching and Learning.** Chatbot Blueprint and how-to for building Gemini Gems for a course. Useful for writing the assignment. https://ctl.wustl.edu/creating-gemini-gems-chatbots-to-support-teaching/
- **Interactive Digital Narrative syllabus collection** (pre-AI and AI narrative courses). https://idnsyllabi.com/
- **Google Classroom** can assign Gems and Gemini notebooks to students, if the admin turns them on. https://support.google.com/edu/classroom/answer/16534159

Didn’t find a community college multimedia storytelling course doing exactly this. That could be the opening, but it was a quick search, not an exhaustive one.

## Next steps (when it comes off Parked)

- [ ] Find the curriculum deadline for adding AVC150 to the DMA and Animation programs for Spring 2028.
- [x] Pull the official AVC150 competencies and outline from the course bank (done 23 Oct).
- [ ] Run it through the `research-first` skill, then `build-a-course`.
- [ ] Prototype option 1 with one story of your own before writing any assignment.

# AVC150 Multimedia Storytelling · Canvas Import Guide (Spring 2028 draft)

Package: `AVC150_Spring2028_DRAFT.imscc` (built from COURSE_V5.md)

## 1. Import into a sandbox

1. Open your Canvas sandbox course (not a live section).
2. Go to **Settings › Import Course Content**.
3. Content Type: **Common Cartridge 1.x Package**. Choose the .imscc file.
4. Select **All content**. Leave “Adjust events and due dates” unchecked, because the Spring 2028 dates are already set.
5. Click **Import** and wait for the status to say Completed. Check the Issues link if one shows.
6. In **Settings › Course Details**, confirm the time zone is Arizona and the dates run Sat Jan 15 to Mon May 15, 2028.

## 2. What you get

| Content | Count |
|---|---|
| Modules | 17: Start Here: Getting Started, Week 1 to Week 15, Finals Week |
| Pages | 69, including 17 unpublished “Instructor Notes” pages (one per module) |
| Assignments | 38: 12 practice and checkpoint items (incl. the Syllabus and AI-Use Agreement), 10 milestones, Project 1, the Final Project, and 14 critique records |
| Discussions | 11: D1 to D10 (graded, 20 points each) and an ungraded Course Questions board |
| Rubrics | 8: Practice Checklist (10), Shared Discussion (20), Critique Record (10), Milestone (100), Script (100), Rough Cut (100), Project 1 (100), Final Project (100) |

Assignment groups, weighted by percentage (weighting is on):

| Group | Weight | Items |
|---|---|---|
| Practice and Checkpoints | 10% | P1 to P6, five checkpoints, Syllabus and AI-Use Agreement (10 points each, complete or incomplete) |
| Discussions and Critique | 15% | D1 to D10 (20 points each) and 14 critique records (10 points each) |
| Milestones | 20% | M1 to M10 (100 points each) |
| Project 1 | 20% | Project 1 (100 points) |
| Final Project | 35% | Final Project (100 points) |

**Critique records.** The source grades critique comments at the 14 non-discussion critiqued deadlines in the Discussions group. Each has its own “Critique Record” assignment with no student submission; you grade it from the comments the student includes in the parent item’s Canvas submission. With 200 discussion points and 140 critique points, the split is about 59/41 against the source’s 60/40.

**D7, D8 and D9** are workshop discussions whose post is the milestone. The shared discussion rubric is attached to all ten discussions; on D7 to D9 the page tells students the Post row is scored in proportion to the other three rows (the source’s “rescaled to 100”).

**Surveys.** The mid-semester and course surveys are pages with a placeholder link, not quizzes. Build them as anonymous Canvas surveys or Google Forms and paste the link in.

## 3. Everything is unpublished

Every module, page, assignment and discussion imports **unpublished** so you can review before release. Publish module by module. Leave the 17 “Instructor Notes · ...” pages unpublished for good; they hold competency codes, recording plans, RSI cadence, seat time and pitfalls.

## 4. Dates and Canvas settings

Spring 2028 dates are in the cartridge (Due = the deadline, Available until = the hard close, all at noon Arizona time). Page text uses day names only, never dates.

**Turn on the Late Policy** (a course setting, not in the cartridge): Gradebook › Settings › Late Policies › Automatically apply deduction to late submissions, **10% per day**, lowest possible grade as you prefer. The window is one day, so the deduction applies once. Set the Missing Submission policy to your standing practice.

### Holiday and break adjustments

Students have nothing to do on closed days: no posting time, due date or hard close falls on MLK Day (Mon Jan 17), Presidents’ Day (Mon Feb 21) or spring break (Mar 13 to 19).

| Week | Change | Why | Hours |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | No change. Course opens after the holiday Monday; the single Thursday deadline stands | MLK Day | P1 and D1 stay light (1.0 hour together) |
| 5 | **P4 Visual Field Journal** runs a day early: post Fri Feb 18, due Sat Feb 19, closes Sun Feb 20 | The usual Monday close (and its late window) would land on Presidents’ Day | P4 loses one working day; its 1.5 hours fit between the P3 close (Fri) and Sat noon, and the location shoot can happen any day from Monday of Week 5. The page text and overview say so |
| 5 to 6 | Interview window unchanged (Tue Feb 15 to Wed Feb 23), but the M2 page tells students to avoid Monday of Week 6 | Presidents’ Day | None |
| 8 | **Project 1** runs a day early: post Fri Mar 10, due Sat Mar 11, closes Sun Mar 12 | A Monday close would land on the first day of spring break | Project 1 (5.0 outside hours) loses one day. Students can start in Week 7 as the source already plans (0.5 hours), and the D6 close Friday frees the end of the week. Feedback still lands Thursday of Week 9 (Mar 23), before M5 |
| Break | Nothing due, posted or open Mar 13 to 19; nothing due Sun Mar 19 or Mon Mar 20 | Spring break | None |
| 9 | First deadline after the break is the **Beat Board Checkpoint**, Thu Mar 23 (post Wed Mar 22). M5 stays Sun Mar 26 | Rule: nothing before Thursday of Week 9 | The Beat Board (1.5 hours) has Monday to Wednesday noon instead of the usual four days; it is a paper edit plus five to eight cards, so this is workable. Week 9 keeps both deadlines because M5 depends on the checkpoint critique and on Project 1 feedback |

Both moved items are labeled in their page text, the week overview, the How Deadlines Work page and the calendar table. Feedback-before-next-milestone order is unchanged. No week has more than two due dates; finals week has one.

**Consent documents pending legal review.** The Project Information Sheet, Narrator Agreement, Narrator Release (11 items) and Narrator Review Form are drafts in the Start Here module under a “Consent Documents (draft, pending legal review)” subheader. Send them to district counsel before publishing, along with the Arizona recording-law note on the AI in This Course page and the question of a GCC tribal-liaison contact.

**ILO attachment.** The Final Project rubric names one ILO per criterion in the criterion title (Creative thinking, Visual communication, Oral communication, Information literacy, Career and workforce readiness; Accessibility has none). After you confirm GCC’s ILO names and codes, attach the outcomes to the rubric criteria in Canvas (Outcomes › Find, then edit the rubric) and remove the text tags if you prefer. If GCC limits ILOs per assignment, keep VC, I and W.

## 6. Quick review checklist

- Spot-check one week end to end in Student View (overview, watch and read, practice, discussion, milestone).
- Confirm the rubric shows under each assignment and discussion.
- Confirm Gradebook shows five weighted groups summing to 100%.
- Turn on the Late Policy.
- Publish Start Here first, then each week as it is ready. Keep Instructor Notes unpublished.

# Course build, draft 5 (synthetic panel plus design and media panel, 23 Oct 2026)

**Tool update, 23 Oct 2026 (Michelle):** Maricopa students get Gemini in January 2027, but Google is retiring Gems (Workspace for Education accounts lose them in June 2027, before this course runs). The Teller is built in **Gemini Notebook** (formerly NotebookLM), which holds the archive as sources, including audio. **Gemini skills** (reusable instructions, up to 100 MB of text, PDF and image files, no audio or video) can carry the Teller’s voice and guardrails once skills reach school accounts and sharing works; confirm both before Spring 2028.

Built by the course-builder panel: a PhD instructional technologist, a four-voice writing faculty panel (composition, journalism, English literature, creative writing), a four-voice design and media panel (graphic design, animation and motion, film and video editing, audio) and a synthetic student review. Draft 3 makes it a visual storytelling studio that still builds professional writing, and uses Michelle’s noon deadline rhythm with a 10 percent late window. **Draft only. Not delivery-ready until Michelle reviews and signs off.** The human-review packet comes first, then the full course.

# AVC150 Multimedia Storytelling · Review Packet for Michelle

**Adobe access, partly resolved 23 Oct 2026 (Michelle):** online students DO get Adobe Creative Cloud, so the Adobe path (Photoshop, Illustrator, Premiere, After Effects, Audition) is the course default. **Still unconfirmed: Firefly and generative credits** (Michelle is not sure GCC has Firefly). Until confirmed, Gemini image generation (once student Gemini is live in January 2027) is the default AI image tool for moodboards and style frames, and Firefly, Generative Fill and Firefly sound and music are optional where available. Ask GCC IT whether the student license includes Firefly credits. The free and Chromebook paths stay as backups.

Original review packet from draft 2. Where it conflicts with Section 8 of the course below (tool questions, Discord, Teller voice), Section 8 wins. Everything below needs a human decision or check before the course is built in Canvas. Most important first.

## C. Build log

**Standards met in COURSE_V2**

| Standard | How it is met |
|---|---|
| GCC seat-hour table | 40 direct + 80 outside = 120, every week exactly 8.0, every task estimated (Section 2) |
| Backward design and alignment | All 11 board competencies mapped to modules, objectives (measurable verbs) and assessments; codes kept in instructor notes |
| Authentic assessment | Two projects and ten milestones at Apply level or higher; no exams; held-out Teller questions, narrator clip, version history and recorded walkthrough resist AI substitution |
| RSI | Weekly announcements, ten facilitated discussions, five Studio Sessions, feedback schedule tied to the next due item, mid-semester check-in, rough cut video feedback (6.1) |
| Pacing | Stated turnaround; every milestone due after feedback on the one before; workshop drafts labeled as the milestone and due Day 5; nothing due on Day 1; never more than two due dates per day (1.2, 1.3) |
| UDL | Narrator menu, choice of form and tool path, written or drawn journal pages, audio or video discussion posts, templates, glossary |
| WCAG 2.1 AA | Captions on all video (Level A), plus description of visual information in narration or a descriptive transcript (AA); accessible page structure |
| OSCQR | Getting Started module, syllabus, communication cadence, netiquette, accessibility statement, technology requirements, clear due dates |
| Consent and safety | Two-stage consent (agreement to record, then release after the interview), classmates-viewing item, immigration and legal status rule, grief and minors rules, withdrawal steps, end-of-semester Teller plan |
| House rules | Formal student-facing text; instructor notes box at the top of each module; purple title box then Objectives; one discussion format and rubric with topic titles; “Week N” only; curly quotes; no em dashes; none of the words on her banned list |

**Accuracy fixes applied from the SME critique:** Lambert’s Seven Steps from the free *Cookbook* (not the older seven elements or the purchased book); McCloud Ch. 6 “Show and Tell”; Fredbot taught as retrieval of real sentences; StoryCenter Story Circle used for pitch peer review (Harmon named if mentioned); kishōtenketsu framed as juxtaposition; narration 250 to 375 words for 2 to 3 minutes; “ca” or “undated” dates; phone scan acceptable; loudness in LUFS; captions plus description for AA; MP4 described as a viewing master; fair use as a narrow exception; C5 tags retagged with a pitch peer review added.

**Sources:** 55 linked sources in Appendix A of COURSE_V2: 17 Verified, 37 Live, 1 Not verified (Freesound, not assigned). All 22 draft-1 placeholders are replaced, except those that need you: *Artificial* page blocks, McCloud e-reserve, an Arizona place source, model scripts, model case studies and GCC support pages.

# AVC150 Multimedia Storytelling · Course Design, Version 5

**Course:** AVC150 Multimedia Storytelling, Glendale Community College, Maricopa County Community College District
**Format:** 3 credits, Lec+Lab, 6 periods / 6 load hours, occupational. Fully online, asynchronous, Canvas, 15 weeks. First offered Spring 2028.
**Gen ed:** Arts and Humanities [AHM]; Humanities, Arts and Design [HU]
**Prepared for:** Michelle Blomberg, course owner and human SME reviewer
**Prepared by:** Design and media faculty panel (visual communication and graphic design; animation and motion design; audio and podcast production; film and video editing), revising Version 2 (COURSE_V2.md), which was built with a writing-focused panel. Version 4 was a tool-stack pass on Version 3. Version 5 applies Michelle’s answers to the review questions (23 October 2026).
**Status:** Draft for human review. Everything Michelle must confirm is listed in REVIEW_PACKET.md and in Section 8. Links marked [unverified] did not load for the panel’s checker; items marked [instructor to create] are templates or recordings that do not exist yet. Tools marked CONFIRM need a human check on district and student availability.

## Version 4 change log

### What Version 4 does

The district pays for Google Gemini and Adobe Creative Cloud, so Version 4 makes those two the default at every stage. Online GCC students get Creative Cloud at home (confirmed by Michelle), and students get Gemini in January 2027 (confirmed), a year before the first offering. Gemini Gems retire for education accounts, so the Teller stays in Gemini Notebook, with Gemini skills as an optional add-on. Firefly and Generative Fill appear only as “if your account has generative credits,” with Gemini image generation, or a non-generative Photoshop tool, as the equivalent. Third-party free tools move to the Chromebook and phone backup path only. Nothing else in the course changed except the two Teller decisions below.

### Tool-stack changes

| Area | Version 3 | Version 4 | Hours impact |
|---|---|---|---|
| Tool paths | Three paths: Adobe; free desktop (GIMP, Krita, Audacity, Resolve); Chromebook and phone | Two paths. **Default:** Adobe Creative Cloud and Gemini on a Windows or Mac computer. **Backup:** Chromebook or phone, using Adobe Express, Photoshop on the web and Premiere on a phone (CONFIRM for education accounts), Google Vids, and the Gemini app; third-party free tools (Canva free tier, Photopea, Clipchamp, Audacity, DaVinci Resolve) only when neither Adobe nor Google tools run on the device | 0 (estimates were already for first-time users) |
| Image generation | Firefly first, Gemini as the free alternative | Gemini image generation first (available to Education users of all ages since December 2025; under-18 accounts refine images by follow-up prompts only and cannot edit or generate from uploaded images). Firefly only if the account has generative credits | 0 |
| Image repair, extension and layers | Generative Fill and Expand; Photopea | Photoshop Content-Aware Fill, Spot Healing Brush and Select Subject (no generative credits) by default; Generative Fill and Expand if the account has credits; Gemini image editing for students 18 and over | 0 |
| Writing tools | Gemini app and Google Docs | Gemini in Docs, Sheets, Slides and Gmail (Help me write, Help me visualize) for the script, shot list, metadata, treatment references and narrator emails, where the account has them (18 and over, edition to CONFIRM); the Gemini app otherwise. Canvas in the Gemini app for drafting loglines and pitches (CONFIRM for under-18) | 0 |
| Research | None | Gemini Deep Research, optional, for background on the story’s place and period (Week 3) and as leads for the fact check (Week 10); every claim checked against a cited source (CONFIRM for students) | 0 (inside existing reading and fact-check time) |
| Interview rehearsal | Typed Gemini role-play | Gemini Live, spoken, where the account has it (CONFIRM); typed role-play otherwise | 0 |
| Gemini Notebook | The Teller | The Teller, plus Audio and Video Overviews of the student’s own notebook as a study aid before the paper edit (never used in the film or shared) | 0 (optional, inside the Week 9 paper edit) |
| Moodboard, treatment, storyboard, key art, case study | Adobe Express, Google Slides, Google Sites | Adobe Express, InDesign, Illustrator and Photoshop templates; case-study page in Adobe Portfolio (part of Creative Cloud), with Google Sites as backup | 0 |
| Recording and audio | Phone; Audition or Audacity; Adobe Podcast | Phone voice memo or Audition for recording; Audition and Premiere Pro (Enhance Speech, Essential Sound, Remix, loudness metering) for cleanup and mixing; Adobe Podcast Enhance Speech (browser) and Audacity on the backup path only | 0 |
| Editing and motion | Premiere, Resolve, Adobe Express, Clipchamp | Premiere Pro (speech-to-text, text-based editing, Auto Reframe, captions; Generative Extend only with credits) and After Effects; Adobe Express or Google Vids (basic) on the backup path | 0 |
| Generated video | Gemini or Firefly Video, by approval | Veo only if the district turns it on for students (not listed for education users as of early 2026; CONFIRM), and Firefly Video only with credits; motion tests and animatic previs only, never footage of real people | 0 |
| Captions | Premiere, YouTube Studio | Premiere Pro captions by default; YouTube Studio on the backup path | 0 |
| Firefly credits | “CONFIRM Adobe licensing” | The specific question: is the student license Creative Cloud Pro Plus for Education (which Adobe lists with standard Firefly features and 1,000 premium generative credits per user per month), and are Firefly generative features on for students? | 0 |

**Hours:** no net change. Premiere Pro’s text-based editing and speech-to-text save some time in Weeks 6, 12 and 13 compared with the free editors, and that is offset by the first-time learning curve of moving every student into the full Adobe applications. The week-by-week budget in Section 2 stands.

| Someone who has died | Archivist voice required in graded work; first person an open question | The student chooses the archivist voice **or** first person; first person requires written consent from the family member or estate holder |

## Version 3 change log

### What Version 3 does, in one paragraph

Version 2 read like a writing class. Version 3 makes it a visual storytelling studio that still builds writing and communication skills, because those are skills Digital Media Arts and Animation majors need and often lack. Writing was not stripped out. Writing for its own sake (free-floating analysis paragraphs, prose journals, reflective-writing readings) was replaced with the writing visual professionals do on the job: loglines, pitches, a visual treatment, a two-column audio/visual script, storyboard annotations, shot lists, captions, alt text, on-screen type, a tagline, a client-style email to the narrator, written critique, a maker’s statement and case-study copy. Every one of those pieces feeds a visual artifact. Students choose one of three visual forms; audio stays in a supporting role (clean narration, interview audio, sound design and a music bed). The AI Teller moves from Gemini Gems, which education accounts lose in June 2027, to **Gemini Notebook** (formerly NotebookLM), with Gemini skills as an optional add-on once school accounts have them. The calendar follows Michelle’s AVC183 rhythm: two noon deadlines a week (Thursday and Sunday), posting to Discord for critique at noon the day before, three critique comments due with the work, a 24-hour late window at a 10% deduction, and nothing due at midnight. Workload stays at 120 hours: every addition replaced something of equal or larger size.

### Story forms (replaces “photo documentary, motion comic, animation or live action, all equivalent”)

| Form | What the finished piece is | Typical tools |
|---|---|---|
| **A. Animation or motion comic** | Drawn, painted, cut-out or motion-graphic frames with limited animation (parallax, puppet or cut-out moves, animated type) | Photoshop, Illustrator, Fresco, After Effects, Premiere Pro; Gemini image generation for studies (backup: Adobe Express animation) |
| **B. Illustrated or photo documentary** | Archival photographs, the student’s own photography or illustration, with designed type and camera moves on stills | Photoshop, Lightroom, Illustrator, Premiere Pro (backup: Adobe Express, Photoshop on the web) |
| **C. Live-action or archival video** | The student’s own footage (b-roll, place, hands, objects) cut with archival photographs or public-domain film | Phone camera, Premiere Pro, After Effects for titles (backup: Premiere on a phone, Adobe Express, Google Vids) |

Every story uses both audio and visual: narration and sound with designed picture. There is no audio-only path (Michelle, 23 Oct 2026). All forms are graded on the same rubrics. Section 3.4 states what each visual criterion means in each form.

### Deadline rhythm and seat-time changes

| Change | Version 2 | Version 3 | Hours impact |
|---|---|---|---|
| Deadline rhythm | Day 3 to Day 7 due dates, up to two per day | Michelle’s two-cycle rhythm. **Cycle B:** post Wednesday at noon, due Thursday at noon, hard close Friday at noon. **Cycle A:** post Saturday at noon, due Sunday at noon, hard close Monday at noon. Week 1 has one deadline (Thursday at noon, close Friday at noon); two cycles a week from Week 2. Nothing is due at midnight. In Canvas, Due = the deadline and Available until = the hard close; work submitted between them loses 10% | 0 |
| Critique | 10 discussions with two replies each (about 10.75 direct hours including posts) | 24 critiqued deadlines (10 discussions, 5 practices, 5 checkpoints, Project 1 and the critiqued milestones). Students post to Discord and comment on at least three classmates; the comments and the work are submitted together in Canvas. Items with private material (consent, release, transcript, Teller logs, surveys, final archive) have no critique | Critique 0.5 per critiqued deadline = 12.0 direct, plus 1.0 extra for the Week 15 showcase; discussion post creation moved to outside work (about 3.0) |
| Checkpoints | None | Five critiqued checkpoints (Beat Board, Storyboard, Key Art, Motion Test, Titles and Captions) split the large production milestones into two deadlines each. They are parts of the milestone work, not new work | 0 outside; their critique is counted above |
| Lectures, demos and Studio Sessions | 5 Studio Sessions of 0.5 to 1.0; lectures mostly 1.0 | Lectures trimmed to 0.5 to 0.75 in Weeks 5, 8, 10, 12 and 14 and Studio Sessions to 0.5 to 0.75 in Weeks 5, 10, 12 and 14, because live peer critique now carries part of the teaching; a 0.5 setup Studio Session added in Week 1 | about −3.0 direct |
| Outside trims to absorb discussion posts | | P2 −0.25, Week 2 stories −0.25, Week 3 readings −0.25, M2 −0.25, CAI reading −0.25, M4 −0.25, Project 1 −1.25, Week 8 model viewing moved into the lecture; M3 +0.5 (a more realistic interview estimate) | about −2.5 outside, with the remaining 0.5 absorbed by the Week 5 and Week 6 rebalancing shown in 2.1 |

**Net hours:** every change above was offset inside its week. Total stays 40 direct + 80 outside = 120. Section 2.1 shows the week-by-week reconciliation.

### Writing that stays as writing, because the competencies require it

The interview guide (Week 4), the narration script (Weeks 9 and 10, now in A/V format), the fact-check table (Week 10), the Teller instructions and guardrails (Weeks 7, 8 and 15), the maker’s statement and case-study copy (Weeks 10 and 15), and written peer critique (every discussion). These carry the outcomes on crafting, editing and finalizing a story and presenting it for peer review.

### What changed from draft 1 (carried forward from Version 2)

| Area | Draft 1 | Version 2 (kept in Version 3) |
|---|---|---|
| Workload | 13 practices, 15 discussions, full AI statement on every milestone; student review estimated 149 to 175 real hours | 6 practices, 10 discussions, three-field log per milestone, full statement on the two projects only; every task re-estimated conservatively to 40 + 80 = 120 |
| Pacing | Feedback promised “within 7 days,” arriving after the next item was due; hidden Day 4 drafts | Stated turnaround (2 to 3 days on milestones, 4 to 5 on projects); every milestone due after feedback on the one before; workshop drafts are the milestone itself, labeled as such. Version 3 keeps the turnaround rule and replaces the day-number scheme with Michelle’s two-cycle noon rhythm (above) |
| Consent | All signed forms in Week 4, before the interview | Narrator agreement to be recorded in Week 4; full release after the interview; classmates-viewing item; immigration-status rule; grief and minors rules; narrator menu including self-interview; end-of-semester Teller plan |
| Teller | First person allowed with consent, including for the dead | Third-person archivist voice by default; first person only for a living narrator with written consent; never public; first person for someone who has died is an open decision for Michelle |
| AI framing | Mostly “catch the AI being wrong” | AI as a collaborator that saves real time, with one sustained critique lens drawn from *Artificial* |
| Access | Premiere-centered; 600 dpi scans; Google Doc fallback for under-18 students | Three supported tool paths including Chromebook and phone; phone scan acceptable; an instructor-hosted Teller; everyone shares Tellers by screen recording |
| Content accuracy | Old “seven elements,” wrong McCloud chapter, Fredbot as a generator, “-12 dB,” “captions meet AA” | Lambert’s Seven Steps; McCloud Ch. 6; Fredbot as retrieval; loudness in LUFS; captions plus description of visual information |
| Student view | Competency codes and jargon | Plain-language outcomes; glossary; first-use definitions; codes kept in instructor notes |

### Design concept

Students choose a story that is personal to them but not necessarily about them: a grandparent’s move to Arizona, a coach’s first season, a family recipe, a shop on a corner that is gone. With consent from the person whose story it is (the **narrator**), they record an interview and gather an **archive** of photographs, documents, sound, reference images and visual journal pages. They build an AI **Teller** in Gemini Notebook, grounded in that archive, so the archive can be questioned and searched. Amy Kurzweil’s graphic memoir *Artificial: A Love Story* (Catapult, 2023) is the anchor text: her father builds “Fredbot” from her grandfather’s letters and lecture notes, and she tells the family story by talking with it and drawing it. That is the model for this course: a visual artist who writes, and who uses an AI as one tool among many.

Students then do what no Teller can do for them. They pitch the story with a moodboard, design style frames, write a visual treatment and a two-column script, storyboard and cut an animatic, design key art and titles, and produce a 2 to 4 minute digital story in one of three visual forms. Audio supports the picture: clean narration, the narrator’s real voice, sound design and a music bed. The semester ends with **The Living Archive**, a portfolio piece made of the finished story, its key art, the preserved archive, a screen-recorded demonstration of the Teller and a designed case-study page.

AI is used at every stage as a creative collaborator, built on the two platforms the district pays for. Google Gemini supplies the Gemini app, image generation for moodboards and style exploration, Gemini Notebook for the Teller, Deep Research for background, and Gemini in Docs, Sheets, Slides and Gmail for the writing. Adobe Creative Cloud supplies production: Photoshop, Illustrator, InDesign, Premiere Pro, After Effects, Audition and Adobe Express, with AI-assisted transcription, editing, audio cleanup and captioning, and Firefly and Generative Fill where the account has generative credits. The course keeps one honest critique running through it, borrowed from Kurzweil: when a machine speaks about a person, or draws one, what does it get right, what does it miss, and who decides?

### Design principles applied

- Backward design: the 11 board-approved competencies (C1 to C11) are the course outcomes. Every objective, activity and assessment traces to them in the instructor notes; students see the outcomes in plain language.
- One big project, scaffolded by 10 milestones; one mid-semester project (The Teller and the Micro-Story) whose style frames set the look of the final.
- Visual first, audio supporting. Every week produces something a viewer can look at.
- Writing as a professional skill: every writing task is the kind a designer, animator or editor writes at work, and every one feeds a visual artifact.
- Authentic assessment at Apply level or higher. No exams.
- Grade what the student authors: choices, curation, writing, design, editing, testing, judgment. Never grade how clever the Teller sounds or how polished a generated image is on its own.
- OSCQR, Regular and Substantive Interaction (RSI), UDL and WCAG 2.1 AA designed in from the start.
- Workload honesty: every task is estimated for a student new to the tool, and the course was cut until the estimates fit 120 hours.

Outline alignment: Unit I (Introduction to Storytelling) = Weeks 1 to 4. Unit II (Crafting a Personal Story) = Weeks 5 to 10. Unit III (Creating the Digital Story) = Weeks 11 to 15. The archive (C10) starts in Week 6 because that is when the material exists. The district note gives faculty authority over this sequencing.

**Visual artifact trail (what goes in the portfolio):** moodboard (Week 3) → visual field journal (Week 5) → still-life archive photograph (Week 6) → style frames and motion micro-story (Week 8) → beat board (Week 9) → visual treatment (Week 10) → storyboard and animatic (Week 11) → key art and title card (Week 12) → motion test and rough cut (Week 13) → final cut with title sequence (Week 14) → designed case-study page (Week 15).

### 1.2 Deadline rhythm and calendar

**How the rhythm works.** Each module opens on Monday at noon (Week 1 opens on the first Monday of the term). Every week from Week 2 on has two deadlines, never more:

| Cycle | Post for critique (critiqued items only) | Deadline (Canvas “Due”) | Hard close (Canvas “Available until”) |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Cycle B** (midweek) | Wednesday at noon, in the assignment’s Discord channel | Thursday at noon: the work and your comments on at least three classmates’ posts, submitted together in Canvas | Friday at noon. Work submitted between the deadline and the hard close loses 10%; nothing is accepted after it |
| **Cycle A** (weekend) | Saturday at noon, in the assignment’s Discord channel | Sunday at noon: the work and your comments, submitted together in Canvas | Monday at noon. Same rule |

Week 1 has a single deadline: post by Wednesday at noon, due Thursday at noon, hard close Friday at noon. Nothing in this course is ever due at midnight. Items without critique have only the deadline and the 24-hour late window (10% deduction) to the hard close. Work posted for critique after the posting time may receive less peer feedback, because classmates comment on the work that is there when they sit down to critique. You still owe your own three comments by the deadline. Between deadlines students have three to four days to make the work (Sunday noon to Thursday noon is four days; Thursday noon to Sunday noon is three).

**Returning on the late-window day** (stated in the same words on the How Deadlines Work page and in the critique protocol): “Your required critique is three comments by the deadline. Some classmates will post during the late window, after most feedback has been given. If you can, return on the late-window day (Monday for the weekend cycle, Friday for the midweek cycle) and leave a comment on any work posted late. This is not graded and earns no points; it is what a good member of an online creative community does. At some point in the semester you may be the one waiting for feedback, and it is worth more to a late project than the points it lost.” This is encouraged and visible (it is pinned in every Discord channel) but it is not a deadline, carries no points and adds no third deadline to any week.

**Canvas settings:** Due = the deadline; Available until = the hard close; Late Policy on, deducting 10% per day late (the window is one day, so the deduction is 10% once); Missing Submission policy per Michelle’s standing practice.

**Calendar (each cell is one deadline; at most two per week)**

| Week | Thursday at noon (post Wednesday at noon; hard close Friday at noon) | Sunday at noon (post Saturday at noon; hard close Monday at noon) |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | P1 and D1 · Syllabus and AI-Use Agreement (no critique) | None |
| 2 | D2 | P2 |
| 3 | D3 | M1 |
| 4 | D4 | M2 |
| 5 | P3 | P4 |
| 6 | M3 | D5 |
| 7 | M4 (no critique) | P5 (no critique) |
| 8 | D6 · Mid-semester survey (no critique) | Project 1 |
| 9 | Beat Board Checkpoint | M5 (D7) |
| 10 | P6 | M6 |
| 11 | Storyboard Checkpoint | M7 (D8) |
| 12 | Key Art Checkpoint | M8 (no critique) |
| 13 | Motion Test Checkpoint | M9 (D9) |
| 14 | Titles and Captions Checkpoint | M10 (no critique) |
| 15 | D10 · Course survey (no critique) | None |
| Finals week | **Wednesday at noon:** Final Project (no critique; hard close Thursday of finals week at noon) | None |

A survey shares a deadline time with another item; it is not a third deadline. Week 15 is the last instructional week (Michelle, 23 Oct 2026); the Final Project’s only deadline falls in finals week, so no week has more than two.

**The interview window** sits between deadlines: it opens Tuesday of Week 5 at noon (after M2 feedback) and runs through Wednesday of Week 6, so it includes a full weekend. M3 is due Thursday of Week 6.

## 2. Seat-time reconciliation

**Rule applied:** 3 credits x 40 = 120 student hours. Online delivery of a 3-credit, 6-load Lec+Lab course = 40 hours direct instruction + 80 hours outside work. Target 2.67 direct + 5.33 outside per week.

**Direct instruction:** recorded lectures and demonstrations, guided labs, Studio Sessions (live, recorded and required to watch), instructor-facilitated peer critique in Discord (reading classmates’ posts and writing critique comments), and reviewing individualized instructor video feedback. **Outside work:** reading and viewing, practice, checkpoints, milestone and project work, making discussion posts, surveys.

**How the estimates were made:** each task is estimated for a student using the tool for the first time, on the default Adobe and Gemini path (with the Chromebook backup checked for the heaviest tasks), following the minimum viable spec stated on the assignment. Reading is estimated at about 25 pages of prose per hour and about 60 pages of graphic memoir per hour. A critique round (viewing at least three classmates’ posts and writing three comments of 40 to 80 words) is estimated at 0.5 hours; the Week 15 showcase round, with full films and case-study pages, at 1.5. Logging AI use is budgeted inside each milestone (about 10 minutes). Visual tasks are estimated at the minimum viable spec, not at portfolio polish: a style frame at 15 to 25 minutes, a moodboard at about an hour including AI exploration, key art at about an hour from existing assets.

**Reconciliation:** five weeks at 3.0 direct (Weeks 1, 7, 11, 13, 15) and ten at 2.5 = 40.0. Five weeks at 5.0 outside and ten at 5.5 = 80.0. 40 + 80 = 120, matching the GCC Art and Humanities seat-hour table. No week exceeds 8.0 hours. Critique accounts for 13.0 of the 40 direct hours (24 rounds at 0.5, with the showcase at 1.5); lectures, demonstrations, labs and Studio Sessions account for 24.5; reviewing instructor feedback and the welcome and closing videos for 2.5.

### 2.2 What was cut to make the estimate honest

Carried from Version 2:

| Cut or change | Hours returned (approximate, per student) |
|---|---|
| 15 discussions to 10; posts shortened (now a visual with 100 to 200 words, or 2 minutes of audio or video) | 6 |
| 13 practices to 6 (sound portrait, image pairings, structure map, read-aloud, thumbnails, restoration and sound bed folded into milestones, checkpoints or discussions) | 8 |
| Full AI-use statement on 12 items to a three-field log on milestones and a full statement on the two projects | 4 |
| Interview capped at 20 to 30 minutes; only a 10-minute excerpt fully corrected | 4 |
| Journal from five written entries to three visual pages with short captions | 1.5 |
| Narration 300 to 550 words to 250 to 375 (about 450 maximum with interview clips for a 4-minute piece) | 1 |
| Teller tests from 30 to 20 (P5’s ten count toward Project 1) plus 5 in the final | 2 |
| Final reflection essay replaced by a 3 to 5 minute recorded walkthrough | 1.5 |
| Archive preservation moved from Week 14 to Week 12; Teller v2 moved into the Final; case-study page started in Week 10 | spreads 5 hours out of Weeks 13 to 15 |
| Templates provided (editing project with title, caption and credit tracks; storyboard; metadata sheet; case-study page; consent documents) | 4 |

Added in Version 3:

| Cut or change | Hours returned (approximate, per student) |
|---|---|
| Critique only on items with shareable work; consent, release, transcript, Teller logs, the asset pack, the final archive and surveys carry no critique | 2.5 (five fewer critique rounds) |
| Lectures and Studio Sessions shortened where live peer critique now carries the teaching | 3.0 direct |
| Project 1 style frames are reused as the final piece’s look, so Week 11 does not restart visual development | 1 to 2 (not counted in the budget; a safety margin) |
| Templates added: moodboard board, A/V script, beat board, treatment section of the case-study page, key art layout (2:3 and 4:5 with safe areas), title and credit templates, narrator email templates, a Canvas critique record template | 2 (already reflected in the estimates) |
| Two writing-chapter readings (Kahn, Giles) and one audio review (NPR Book of the Day) swapped for visual and pitching readings of equal or shorter length | 0.25 |

### 2.3 Weeks still at risk

| Week | Risk | Mitigation built in |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Week 1’s single deadline comes on Thursday of the first week | P1 and the D1 post are light (1.0 together); the hard close is Friday at noon; readings may continue into the weekend |
| 3 | Moodboards that become a weekend of image generation | Capped at 12 to 20 images and one hour; the moodboard is graded on the choices and captions, not on image count |
| 5 to 6 | Scheduling the interview around a narrator’s life | Interview day set in M2; the window runs Tuesday of Week 5 at noon to Wednesday of Week 6 and includes a weekend; if the narrator cancels, the student tells the instructor and M3 may be submitted by Monday of Week 7 at noon with no penalty; self-interview always available |
| 6 | Scanning family items that live at a relative’s house | Ten items minimum, any mix of scanned, photographed or born-digital; scanning may continue into Week 12 |
| 8 | Project 1 is estimated at 5.5 hours, tighter than Version 2, and animation students may overbuild style frames | Three to five frames at style-frame finish, not animation; limited motion only (a pan, zoom, parallax move or one animated element); edit from a template; the 24-hour late window (10% deduction) is a safety valve, not a plan |
| 11 | Animators overbuilding the animatic | The animatic is a timing test: still frames on a timeline with scratch narration; nothing more is graded |
| 12 to 13 | Key art plus assembly in one week; editing for students new to video, especially on Chromebook | Key art is built from existing style frames and archive images in a provided template and checked at Thursday’s checkpoint; assembly edit happens in Week 12 so Week 13 only adds motion, sound and timing; Form B (photo or illustrated) is the recommended scope for first-time editors; editing template provided |
| 15 | Case-study page for students new to web pages | Page and treatment set up from a template in Week 10; the story and key art are added in Week 14; Week 15 adds only the copy, maker’s statement, walkthrough and Teller section |
| All | Weekend deadlines for students who work weekends | Every Sunday deadline has a Monday-at-noon hard close (10% deduction); work may be submitted early |
| All | Chromebook students translating demos | Every demo has a short Chromebook-path clip (Adobe Express, Photoshop on the web, Google Vids); every AI image step also accepts drawn, photographed or collaged work, graded the same |

The panel cannot verify these estimates. The first section offered should collect a two-minute weekly time log (optional, anonymous Canvas survey) so Michelle can recalibrate with real data. Visual tasks and critique rounds are where students most often overspend; the log should ask about them by name.

## 3. Assessment plan

### 3.1 Assignment groups and weights

| Assignment group | Items | Weight | Grading approach |
|---|---|---|---|
| Practice and Checkpoints | P1 to P6, the five checkpoints, and the Syllabus and AI-Use Agreement check | 10% | Complete/incomplete against a short checklist, with feedback |
| Discussions and Critique | D1 to D10, and the critique comments given at every other critiqued deadline | 15% | D1 to D10: shared discussion rubric (3.7), 60% of the group. Critique comments at the other 14 critiqued deadlines: critique record rubric (3.7b), 40% of the group |
| Milestones | M1 to M10 | 20% | Milestone rubric (3.5), Script rubric (3.6) for M5 and M6, or Rough Cut rubric (3.6b) for M9 |
| Project 1: The Teller and the Micro-Story | 1 | 20% | Analytic rubric (3.3) |
| Final Project: The Living Archive | 1 | 35% | Analytic rubric with ILO tags (3.4) |
| **Total** | | **100%** | |

**Weights** (Michelle, 23 Oct 2026): confirmed as shown. **Revision:** any milestone may be resubmitted once within one week of feedback (Michelle, 23 Oct 2026). **Late work:** every item has a 24-hour window after its deadline in which work is accepted with a 10% deduction; nothing is accepted after the hard close. Exceptions (illness, emergencies, a narrator’s cancellation) go through the instructor, following Michelle’s standing policy (to confirm).

**One Canvas record per critiqued deadline:** the work (file or link), a link or screenshot of the Discord post, the feedback received (screenshot or a short summary), and links or screenshots of the three comments the student wrote. A template is provided [instructor to create]. Private material (consent forms, releases, recordings, transcripts, Teller links) is submitted only in Canvas and never posted to Discord.

**Writing and visual craft in every grade:** each milestone, project and discussion rubric has at least one visual or production craft criterion and one writing and communication criterion. Writing is graded for clarity, audience, concision and mechanics; visual craft for composition, typography, color, timing, sound mix and finish appropriate to the stage.

### 3.3 Project 1: The Teller and the Micro-Story (Week 8, 20%)

**Competencies (instructor view):** C2, C3, C4, C8, C10

> **Deadlines** · Post the micro-story for critique: Saturday of Week 8 at noon (Discord) · Due: Sunday of Week 8 at noon (Canvas: the full submission plus your comments on three classmates’ micro-stories) · Hard close: Monday of Week 9 at noon (10% deduction between the deadline and the hard close; nothing accepted after). The Teller link, logs and design form go in Canvas only.

**Scenario:** A community oral-history project in the Phoenix area is piloting AI “Tellers” that let visitors search a family or community archive by asking questions. Contributors have been asked to submit a working prototype built from their own collection, demonstrated by screen recording, together with a 45 to 60 second motion micro-story drawn from one item in it, so the project team can see and hear the real person behind the archive. The style frames you design for the micro-story also become the visual direction for your final piece.

**Steps (student-facing):**
1. Load at least 12 archive items into your Teller in Gemini Notebook: your transcript excerpt, at least five captioned images with their metadata, your visual field journal pages, and any documents your narrator approved for AI upload. Continue from your Week 7 prototype.
2. Complete the Teller instruction scaffold in your own words (who the Teller is, whose archive it holds, its voice, what it must never do, what it says when the archive has no answer). Place the instructions in the notebook’s custom instruction setting, or load them as a source titled “Teller Instructions” if that setting is not available. You may ask Gemini to help draft and tighten the instructions; record that in your log.
3. Choose the Teller’s voice. In the **archivist voice**, the Teller speaks about your narrator in the third person and quotes the archive (for example, “In the Week 5 interview, Rosa says…”). In the **first-person voice**, the Teller speaks as the person, still quoting the archive. First person requires written consent: from a living narrator on release item 6, or, for someone who has died, from the family member or estate holder on release item 7. Either voice is graded the same. In every case the Teller is never public; it is shown only by screen recording.
4. Test the Teller with 20 questions in total. The 10 questions from your Week 7 prototype count. Include at least 5 questions the archive cannot answer. Rate each answer accurate, partly accurate, or invented. Change your instructions or sources to fix at least three problems, then ask those questions again.
5. Choose one item from the archive (a photograph, an object, a recording, a place) and write a micro-story script of 120 to 150 words in the two-column A/V template: narration and sound on the left, the picture on the right. Include 5 to 15 seconds of your narrator’s recorded voice, with the timestamp from your transcript, and at least one sensory detail from your visual field journal. If you interviewed yourself, use a clip of your own interview.
6. Design three to five **style frames** in your chosen form. A style frame is a finished still that shows exactly how the final piece will look: composition, color, texture, type and light. At least one frame includes on-screen type (a title or a caption). You may use Gemini image generation (or Firefly, if your account has generative credits) to explore composition, palette and texture; the final frames are made mostly from the archive and your own drawing, photography or footage, and any generated element is labeled in your log.
7. Record your narration and edit a 45 to 60 second sequence: your style frames with limited motion (a pan, zoom, parallax move or one animated element), your narration, the narrator clip and the ambient sound you recorded in Week 5. Use Enhance Speech or a similar tool to clean the audio if needed. Add captions. Export as MP4.
8. Complete the full AI-use statement (4.3).

**Submit:**
- A 2 to 3 minute screen recording of you asking your Teller at least five questions, plus the notebook share link shared only with the instructor (CONFIRM named-person sharing).
- The half-page Teller Design Form: final instructions; sources loaded; voice decision and the release item that permits it; three guardrails, each pointing to the logged problem that caused it.
- The 20-question test log.
- The A/V micro-story script, with the narrator clip’s source timestamp.
- The style frames as PNG files.
- The micro-story MP4 with captions.
- The AI-use statement.

**Held-out questions:** during grading, the instructor asks your Teller three questions you have not seen and notes how it responds.

**Minimum viable spec:** 12 sources loaded, 20 questions logged, 3 fixes retested, one design form, an A/V script of 120 to 150 words, three style frames, a 45 to 60 second captioned MP4 with a narrator clip.

**Rubric: Project 1**

| Criterion (weight) | Exemplary (100%) | Proficient (85%) | Developing (70%) | Beginning (50%) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Archive curation (10%) | Sources chosen with clear purpose, captioned and described, and together give the Teller enough to answer real questions | Sources relevant and described; minor gaps | Sources loaded with little description | Fewer than 12 sources, unlabeled, or used without release |
| Teller design and instruction writing (15%) | Instructions are clear, specific and concise; they define role, voice, scope and the “no answer” response; each guardrail points to a logged problem; the chosen voice (archivist or first person) is backed by the written consent it requires | Instructions complete and clear; guardrails present and mostly tied to problems | Instructions generic or wordy; guardrails not tied to testing | Instructions missing or unadapted |
| Testing and correction (15%) | 20 questions logged with honest ratings; causes of failures explained; three fixes retested; held-out questions handled within the archive | 20 questions logged; fixes retested | Testing incomplete or ratings uncritical | Little or no testing |
| Micro-story writing (20%) | One item, one moment and a clear turn; written for the ear; every line earns its time; A/V columns tell an editor exactly what to show; voice, place and culture specific to this person; mechanics clean | Focused script with clear voice; A/V format used; minor slack or errors | Summary rather than a moment; picture column vague; noticeable errors | Unfocused, prose instead of a script, or no narrator clip |
| Visual craft: style frames and sequence (20%) | Frames share a deliberate look; composition, hierarchy, color and type are controlled and serve the story; type is legible with sufficient contrast; motion is purposeful | Consistent look with solid composition and readable type; minor issues | Frames inconsistent; composition or type weak; motion arbitrary | Fewer than three frames, or frames unrelated to the story |
| Sound and timing (10%) | Clean narration and consistent levels; ambient sound placed with intent; picture changes follow the voice; captions accurate | Clear audio with minor level or timing issues; captions present | Noticeable noise or uneven levels; captions uncorrected | Unusable audio or no captions |
| Consent and AI-use statement (10%) | Release items match every use; statement specific about where AI helped, what it got wrong and what was kept | Release and statement complete | Partial | Missing release coverage or undisclosed AI use |

The Teller is not graded on how fluent or convincing it sounds. It is graded on what the student authored: curation, instructions, testing and correction. Generated images are not graded on their own polish; style frames are graded on the student’s design decisions.

### 3.4 Final Project: The Living Archive (Week 15, 35%)

**Competencies (instructor view):** C2, C3, C4, C6, C7, C8, C9, C10, C11

> **Deadlines** · No critique post (the showcase is D10, Thursday of Week 15) · Due: Wednesday of finals week at noon (Canvas) · Hard close: Thursday of finals week at noon (10% deduction between the deadline and the hard close; nothing accepted after).

**Scenario:** You are preparing a portfolio entry for a studio, museum, agency, newsroom or media employer that wants to see how you take a real story from raw material to a finished, responsibly produced piece. Your Living Archive shows the finished digital story and its key art, the preserved archive, a demonstration of the Teller that lets others search it, and a designed case study that explains your decisions, including your decisions about AI.

**Components:**
1. **The digital story** (submitted as M10, Week 14): 2 to 4 minutes in your chosen form (animation or motion comic; illustrated or photo documentary; live-action or archival video), with narration and sound. Limited motion is the recommended scope; a fully animated piece is not expected. A designed title sequence and end credits. Narration in your voice, your narrator’s recorded voice, or both; no synthetic voices. Accurate captions, and key visual information (who is in a photograph, a date stamp, on-screen text) spoken in the narration or included in a descriptive transcript. Most of the screen time comes from the archive or from your own drawing, photography or footage; every generated or extended image or clip is labeled in the asset log, and any generated image that could pass for a photograph or real footage is styled as illustration or captioned on screen.
2. **Key art** (drafted at the Key Art Checkpoint, finished in M8): one poster in 2:3 or 4:5 with the title, a tagline and a credit block, used on the case-study page and in the showcase.
3. **Teller v2:** load the final script and storyboard; revise the instructions using Project 1 feedback; ask 5 new questions and log them; write a Teller critique (150 to 250 words, illustrated with two annotated screenshots of the Teller’s answers, or included in the walkthrough) naming at least two places where the Teller misrepresents your narrator or the story, and why. Complete the end-of-semester plan from the release (delete, keep until a stated week, or transfer to the narrator).
4. **The archive package** (built in M8): organized folder, metadata sheet, preservation copies, release and narrator review record, licensing log, and evidence of a 3-2-1 backup. The archive is the preserved files and records; the Teller is one way to search it.
5. **The case-study page** (started in P6): unlisted by default; a designed page that includes the treatment, the story, the key art, three storyboard frames beside the matching final frames, a 60 to 90 second screen recording of the Teller (never a live link), a 100 to 150 word maker’s statement, the AI-use statement and the walkthrough. Case-study copy totals about 300 words beyond the statements; every image has a caption and alt text.
6. **The recorded walkthrough:** 3 to 5 minutes in which you screen-share your archive folder, your Teller test log and your edit timeline and explain three decisions in your own voice. A student who cannot record may book a 10-minute live conversation with the instructor instead.
7. **Showcase and peer review (D10):** a showcase post (key art, the story as the release allows, and the maker’s statement) and three written peer reviews.

**What each visual criterion means in each form**

| Criterion | A. Animation or motion comic | B. Illustrated or photo documentary | C. Live-action or archival video |
|---|---|---|---|
| Visual design | Character and environment design, panel or frame composition, consistent style from the style frames | Image selection and sequencing, cropping, restoration labeled, type over images | Shot design, framing and coverage, b-roll that adds information, archival integration |
| Motion, editing and timing | Limited animation and parallax timed to the voice; holds and easing | Camera moves on stills with purpose; cut rhythm follows the narration | Cutting rhythm, J and L cuts, continuity, color match |
| Sound | Narration, narrator clip, room tone and sound design mixed under the picture (music optional) | Same | Same, plus location sound handled cleanly |

**Scaffolding**

| Milestone or checkpoint | Week, deadline | Feeds |
|---|---|---|
| M1 Pitch Package | 3, Sunday | Story choice, form, moodboard, narrator, risk plan |
| M2 Interview Guide and Narrator Agreement | 4, Sunday | Interview |
| P4 Visual Field Journal | 5, Sunday | Sensory details, place references, Teller sources |
| M3 The Interview | 6, Thursday | Archive, Teller, script, micro-story clip |
| M4 Release, Transcript and Archive Inventory | 7, Thursday | Teller, archive package |
| P5 Teller Prototype | 7, Sunday | Project 1 |
| Project 1 | 8, Sunday | Style frames, Teller v2, case-study page, narration skills |
| Beat Board Checkpoint | 9, Thursday | Script structure |
| M5 A/V Script Draft | 9, Sunday | Final script |
| P6 Visual Treatment and Case-Study Page Setup | 10, Thursday | Storyboard look, case study |
| M6 Final Script, Fact Check and Scratch Narration | 10, Sunday | Storyboard, timing |
| Storyboard Checkpoint | 11, Thursday | Storyboard |
| M7 Storyboard, Shot List and Animatic | 11, Sunday | Assembly, case-study frames |

**Rubric: Final Project** (one ILO per criterion; codes to confirm with GCC)

| Criterion (weight) | Exemplary (100%) | Proficient (85%) | Developing (70%) | Beginning (50%) | ILO |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Story craft and script (15%) | A clear question and a turn: the audience understands something differently by the end; voice, place and culture are specific to this person and community; the script is concise and written for the ear; every moment earns its time | Coherent structure with a point; minor slack | Story present but summarizes or meanders | No discernible structure | TE Creative thinking |
| Visual design: composition, typography and color (20%) | Composition, hierarchy, type and color follow design principles and serve the story; a consistent look carried from the style frames; final frames show development from the storyboard; titles and key art are legible and well set; imagery rule met | Visuals support the story with solid design choices; type readable | Visuals repeat the words literally; layout, type or color issues | Visuals unrelated or poorly prepared | VC Visual communication |
| Motion, editing and timing (10%) | Motion and cuts follow the voice; holds, moves and transitions are purposeful; color is consistent across shots | Timing mostly supports the story; minor rough edits | Pacing drags or rushes; motion arbitrary | Timing undermines the story | TE Creative thinking |
| Sound and narration (10%) | Clear, expressive narration; the narrator’s voice placed with purpose; room tone and sound design support the picture (music optional); loudness on target | Clear narration and appropriate sound; minor level issues | Levels, sound design or music (if used) distract | Sound unusable | OC Oral communication |
| Archive and Teller v2, including the critique (10%) | Archive organized, described, preserved and backed up; Teller v2 revised from feedback and retested; critique names two specific misrepresentations with annotated evidence and explains why | Archive organized; Teller updated; critique present | Archive partly organized; critique general | Archive or Teller missing | I Information literacy |
| Rights, consent, narrator review and AI disclosure (10%) | Every asset owned, licensed or released and logged; narrator review documented and honored; AI use disclosed in credits and statement with clear judgment | All present and complete | Gaps in a log or the disclosure | Unlicensed assets, no narrator review, or undisclosed AI use | I Information literacy |
| Accessibility (5%) | Accurate captions; key visual information spoken or in a descriptive transcript; on-screen type meets contrast; alt text on every case-study image | Captions accurate; description or alt text partial | Captions uncorrected | No captions | (none; WCAG requirement) |
| Case study and written communication (10%) | A designed, professional page for an employer audience; copy is clear, concise and free of errors; the maker’s statement states intent and decisions; captions and alt text are specific; the walkthrough explains three decisions with evidence | Complete page, statement and walkthrough; minor errors | Page thin, copy wordy or unclear, or walkthrough general | Missing | W Career and workforce readiness |
| Showcase and written peer critique (10%) | Showcase post frames the work with key art and the maker’s statement; three specific, kind, actionable written reviews using the protocol and design vocabulary | Showcase and three reviews complete | Reviews general or fewer than three | None | OC Oral communication |

### 3.5 Milestone rubric (M1 to M4, M7, M8, M10)

| Criterion (weight) | Complete (100%) | Partial (60%) | Not yet (0 to 30%) |
|---|---|---|---|

| Visual or production craft (20%) | Choices appropriate to the stage are deliberate: composition, type, color and finish (M1, M7, M8, M10); recording and file quality (M3, M4); clear layout of documents (M2) | Some deliberate choices; uneven craft | Little evidence of craft decisions |
| Writing and communication (20%) | The writing in this milestone (pitch, logline, email, captions, alt text, annotations, metadata, credits) is clear, aimed at its audience, concise and free of errors | Clear with some wordiness or errors | Unclear, off-audience, or many errors |
| Development (10%) | Shows choices and revision from prior feedback | Some development | No visible development |
| AI-use log entry (10%) | Three-field entry complete (or “No AI used this milestone”) | Partial | Missing |

### 3.6 Script rubric (M5, M6)

| Criterion (weight) | Strong (100%) | Developing (70%) | Not yet (40%) |
|---|---|---|---|
| The moment and the turn (25%) | Centers one moment; something changes by the end | Moment present; turn unclear | Summary of events |
| Voice (25%) | Sounds like the student and honors the narrator’s own words (at least two timestamped quotes, unedited by AI) | Some generic or smoothed phrasing | Generic voice; quotes missing or altered |
| The cut (20%) | Every line earns its time; within length | Some slack or over length | Well over length or unfocused |
| Writing for the screen (20%) | A/V format used correctly; the picture column names specific images, shots and on-screen type; words and pictures do different work rather than repeating each other; mechanics clean | Format used; picture column general; minor errors | Prose essay, or picture column missing |
| Completeness, version history and log (10%) | Version history link, beat board (M5) or fact-check table (M6), checklist and log complete | Partial | Missing |

### 3.6b Rough Cut rubric (M9)

| Criterion (weight) | Strong (100%) | Developing (70%) | Not yet (40%) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Structure and the turn (25%) | The story’s shape works on screen; the turn lands | Shape present; turn unclear | Structure does not yet work |
| Picture and timing (25%) | Composition follows the storyboard or improves on it; motion and cuts follow the voice | Mostly timed to the voice; some arbitrary moves | Picture and voice unrelated |
| Sound mix (20%) | Narration clear over room tone and sound design (and music, if used); levels consistent | Minor level issues | Voice buried or clipped |
| Captions and on-screen text (15%) | Draft captions present; on-screen type legible and in safe areas | Captions present; some type issues | Missing |
| Narrator email, completeness and log (15%) | Professional review email sent with the form and a clear deadline; checklist and log complete | Partial | Missing |

### 3.7 Shared discussion format and rubric (D1 to D10)

Every discussion page has the same parts: **Prompt**, **Deadlines** (the standard block: post time, deadline and hard close), **Your post** (one visual from your own work or the week’s material, such as an image, frame, storyboard page or clip, with 100 to 200 words of writing, or a 2-minute audio or video post with captions or a transcript, posted in the discussion’s Discord channel by noon on the posting day; in workshop weeks the post is the milestone), **Critique** (written comments of 40 to 80 words on at least three classmates’ posts, by noon on the deadline day), **Canvas record** (the post, the feedback received and the three comments, submitted together by the deadline), and **Instructor role**. Each prompt asks for material from your own project, so no two posts can be the same.

| Criterion (weight) | Exemplary (100%) | Proficient (85%) | Developing (70%) | Beginning (50%) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Post: substance and visual evidence (40%) | Answers every part of the prompt; the visual is specific to the student’s project and the writing points to what is in it | Answers the prompt with a relevant visual and some specifics | Answers part of the prompt; visual generic or missing | Off-topic or minimal |
| Critique: written comments (35%) | Three comments that extend, question or apply a peer’s idea with specifics, using the critique protocol and naming the frame, timecode or line | Three relevant comments | Fewer than three, or agreement only | No comments |
| Design and story vocabulary (10%) | Uses course terms (composition, hierarchy, contrast, beat, turn, timing) precisely | Uses some course terms | Few course terms | None |
| Clarity and mechanics (15%) | Clear, concise, within length, free of errors | Clear with minor errors | Wordy or unclear in places | Unclear |

In workshop weeks (D7, D8, D9) the post is graded as the milestone, and the discussion grade covers the critique, vocabulary, and clarity rows only, rescaled to 100.

### 3.7b Critique record rubric (critique at the other critiqued deadlines)

Applies to the comments a student writes at P1 to P4, P6, the five checkpoints, M1, M2, M3, M6 and Project 1 (14 deadlines; P1 shares its critique with D1). The work itself is graded by its own rubric.

| Criterion (weight) | Complete (100%) | Partial (60%) | Not yet (0 to 30%) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Three comments given (40%) | At least three classmates, by the deadline | Two | One or none |
| Protocol and specificity (40%) | Each comment uses I noticed, I wondered, What if, and names a frame, timecode or line | Protocol used loosely or without specifics | Praise or agreement only |
| Clarity and tone (20%) | Clear, concise (40 to 80 words), about the work, never the person | Mostly clear | Unclear or off-tone |

**Critique space:** Discord, as in AVC183, with one channel per assignment and a pinned post repeating the critique protocol and the posting time. Canvas discussions are the fallback for any student who cannot use Discord (approved; screen names allowed for credit work and for dual-enrollment students under 18; see Section 8).

### 3.8 Critique protocol

Feedback has three parts: **I noticed** (a specific description of what is on the screen or page), **I wondered** (a question about a choice), **What if** (one concrete suggestion). Feedback addresses the work, never the person, and treats every story as belonging to someone real. Written replies name what they are looking at (a frame number, a timecode, a line of the script) so the maker can find it.

**Required critique:** at least three comments of 40 to 80 words by the deadline, at every critiqued assignment.

**Returning on the late-window day:** Your required critique is three comments by the deadline. Some classmates will post during the late window, after most feedback has been given. If you can, return on the late-window day (Monday for the weekend cycle, Friday for the midweek cycle) and leave a comment on any work posted late. This is not graded and earns no points; it is what a good member of an online creative community does. At some point in the semester you may be the one waiting for feedback, and it is worth more to a late project than the points it lost.
