## 4. AI integration plan

### 4.1 AI as a creative collaborator: where it saves real time

These are the uses the Watch videos name out loud. In each one, the student authors the decision and the final artifact. Gemini and Adobe are the defaults. “If credits” means the student’s Creative Cloud account has Firefly generative credits (see 4.2). Availability notes come from Google’s and Adobe’s own pages, checked 23 October 2026; anything not confirmed there is marked CONFIRM.

| Use | Week | Tool (default first) | What it does for the student | What the student still authors |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Story starters, loglines and pitch drafts | 2 to 3 | Gemini app; Canvas in the Gemini app for drafting | Turns three names or places into 15 story possibilities; holds drafts side by side | The choice of story, the logline and pitch wording |
| Quick visual studies and moodboard images | 2 to 3 | Gemini image generation; Firefly Text to Image if credits | Rough studies for palette, light and texture in minutes | Which images belong on the board and the caption that says why |
| Background on place and period (optional) | 3, 10 | Gemini Deep Research (CONFIRM for students; Education Plus allows a few reports a month) | A cited background report on a neighborhood, business or era | Which claims hold up against the cited sources, and what belongs in the story |
| Narrator emails | 4, 13 | Gemini in Gmail, Help me write (CONFIRM edition); Gemini app otherwise | Tightens a draft email | The message, in the student’s own voice |
| Interview rehearsal | 5 | Gemini Live, spoken (CONFIRM for students); typed role-play in the Gemini app otherwise | A safe practice run for nervous interviewers | The guide, the follow-ups, the listening |
| Transcription first draft | 6 | Premiere Pro speech-to-text; Gemini Notebook with the audio as a source | Hours of typing; correct only the passages you will use | The corrected excerpt and the judgment about what was misheard |
| Metadata, alt text and shot lists | 6, 11 | Gemini in Sheets (CONFIRM edition); the Gemini app with a pasted list otherwise | Drafts descriptions, alt text and shot-list rows | Checked dates, names, rights status, final alt text and every shot |
| The Teller | 7 to 8, 15 | Gemini Notebook (available to Education users of all ages since August 2025); Gemini skills optional once school accounts have them | Makes the whole archive searchable in plain language, with citations | Instructions, voice choice, guardrails, tests, fixes, critique |
| Study aid on your own archive (optional) | 9 | Gemini Notebook Audio Overview or Video Overview | Hear or see the archive summarized before the paper edit | Nothing from an overview goes into the film or is shared |
| Style exploration | 7 to 8 | Gemini image generation; Firefly Style Reference and Structure Reference if credits | Several looks for the same composition in minutes | The chosen look, the final style frames built from archive and own work |
| Paper edit by search | 9 | The Teller | Lists every passage about one moment, with timestamps | Which passages to use and in what order |
| Structure options | 9 | Gemini app | Three alternate orders of the student’s own beats in seconds | The chosen structure and the reason |
| Script tightening | 9 to 10 | Gemini in Docs (CONFIRM edition); Gemini app otherwise | Suggests cuts and tighter lines | Every change; quoted words are never edited by AI |
| Fact check | 10 | The Teller; Deep Research reports as leads only | Finds where the interview and the documents disagree | The fact-check table and what to say on screen |
| Read-back | 10 | Text-to-speech (Gemini app read-aloud or the device’s screen reader) | Hearing where a script drags before recording | Every cut |
| Treatment layout | 10 | InDesign or Adobe Express; Gemini in Slides, Help me visualize, for reference images (CONFIRM edition) | A clean layout and quick reference images | The look, the palette, the type pairing, the words |
| Animatic frames | 11 | Gemini image generation; Firefly if credits | Quick alternate compositions to test timing | The storyboard and the composition decisions |
| Image repair and extension | 12 | Photoshop Content-Aware Fill and Spot Healing Brush (no credits); Generative Fill and Generative Expand if credits; Gemini image editing | Fixes scratches and fills a 16:9 frame | What is restored versus invented, labeled |
| Key art exploration | 12 | Illustrator, Photoshop or InDesign; Gemini image generation for background variations; Firefly if credits | Layout and background variations for the poster | Type, hierarchy, tagline, final composition |
| Layer separation for parallax | 12 | Photoshop Select Subject and Content-Aware Fill; Generative Fill if credits | Separates foreground from background and fills the gap | Which layers move and how far |
| Assembly and rough cut | 12 to 13 | Premiere Pro text-based editing, Auto Reframe and Scene Edit Detection; Generative Extend only if credits | Cuts from the transcript; reframes for vertical | Every edit decision; extended frames labeled |
| Motion | 13 | After Effects (Form A); Premiere Pro (Forms B and C) | Parallax, camera moves on stills, animated type | Every move and its timing |
| Previs motion test (optional, by approval) | 11, 13 | Veo, only if the district turns it on for students (not listed for education users as of early 2026; CONFIRM); Firefly Video if credits | A short abstract or environmental test, such as weather, texture or light | Never footage of real, identifiable people or staged events; labeled; used in the final only with approval |
| Audio rescue | 8, 13 | Premiere Pro and Audition Enhance Speech; Adobe Podcast Enhance Speech (browser) on the backup path | Saves a noisy kitchen recording | Recording choices and the mix |
| Sound design; optional music | 13 | Audition and Premiere Pro (Essential Sound; Remix to fit a licensed track if music is used); Firefly sound effects and soundtrack if credits | Fills gaps in sound design; fits any music to the cut | Which sounds (and any music), where they sit, the mix; any music is logged |
| Caption draft | 13 to 14 | Premiere Pro captions; YouTube Studio on the backup path | Most of the typing | Corrected captions and description of visuals |

**The critique lens (one sustained thread, not a trap):** students meet it in Week 2 (reading *Artificial*), test it in Week 4 (the AI image versus the real family photograph) and Week 7 (Fredbot selects real sentences; Gemini Notebook cites sources but its answers still paraphrase and can overreach where the archive has gaps), and write it in the Final (the Teller critique). Everywhere else AI is a working tool.

**Firefly and generative credits.** Generative Fill, Generative Expand, Generative Extend and Firefly draw on generative credits. Whether students have them depends on the license. Adobe lists Creative Cloud Pro Plus for Education with standard Firefly features and 1,000 premium generative credits per user per month (Adobe higher-education plan page, checked 23 October 2026). Until Michelle confirms the license, every assignment names a no-credit path: Gemini image generation, or a non-generative Photoshop tool (Content-Aware Fill, Spot Healing Brush, Select Subject).

### 4.3 AI-use records

**Three-field log (every milestone; about 10 minutes).** A Google Sheet template [instructor to create], one row per milestone:

| Tool | What I used it for | What I kept, changed or rejected |
|---|---|---|
| | | |

If no AI was used, the row says “No AI used this milestone.” That is a complete entry. Example row: “Gemini in Sheets · drafted descriptions for 10 archive items · kept 7, corrected 3 dates against the photo backs.” Generated images kept in any artifact are listed by file name.

**Full AI-use statement (Project 1 and the Final only).** A completed sample statement is posted in Getting Started [instructor to create].

1. **Log summary:** the rows from your log for this project.
2. **Where AI helped or got it wrong, and how you could tell:** one or two specific examples (for example, “Gemini image generation gave me four palettes for the kitchen frame in two minutes; all four made the light too warm for a winter morning, so I color-matched to the archive photo in Photoshop instead.” or “Premiere Pro’s speech-to-text wrote ‘Glendale’ as ‘Glenn Dale’ every time, which I caught against the audio.”).
3. **What is mine:** two or three sentences naming the writing, drawing, photography, design, recording and editing you did.
4. **Credit line:** the line that appears in your credits or case study (example: “Narration written and recorded by [name]. Illustrations by [name]. Interview transcript drafted with Adobe Premiere Pro speech-to-text and corrected by the author. Moodboard studies made with Google Gemini. Two background extensions made with Adobe Photoshop Generative Expand and labeled.”).

### 4.4 Consent, privacy and safety

**Narrator menu (no reason required for any choice):**
1. A family member.
2. A community member the student already knows: a coach, employer, mentor, librarian, faith leader or neighbor.
3. **A self-interview:** a classmate or Gemini asks the questions from the student’s own guide; the student is the narrator, and the story is about a place, an object or the student’s own memory. Counts fully for M3; consent items then cover only the student.
4. A GCC-arranged volunteer narrator, if a pool exists (CONFIRM).
5. Last resort: a public oral history from an openly licensed or public-domain collection the student has a personal connection to, plus a self-interview about that connection (license confirmed by the instructor).

No student has to choose a story that exposes their family, and no one has to explain why they chose a different one.

**Consent sequence (following Oral History Association practice):**

| When | Document | How it may be given |
|---|---|---|
| Week 3, after M1 is approved | Informal ask; the student explains the project briefly | Conversation |
| Week 4, M2 | **Project Information Sheet** sent with the student’s project email and the OHA “For Participants” page; **Narrator Agreement to Be Recorded** | E-signature (Google Form), a photo of a signed page, or a text or email reply; the narrator also says yes on the recording slate in Week 5 |
| Week 5 or 6, after the interview (M4) | **Release** with separate yes/no items (below) | E-signature or signed page; guardian signature if the narrator is under 18 |
| Week 13 to 14 | **Narrator review** of the rough cut | Narrator watches; student records the response and any requested changes in the review form |
| Week 15 | End-of-semester Teller plan confirmed | Release item 10 |

**Upload rule:** nothing from the interview goes into any AI tool until the release is signed with item 4 checked. A student who is waiting transcribes a 10-minute excerpt by hand or with a local tool.

**Release items** (each yes or no):
1. My recording and transcript may be used in the student’s class project.
2. My materials and the student’s Teller may be shared with the instructor and classmates inside the course for peer review, including in the course’s private Discord server. (If no: the student shares only a screen recording with private details cut, or uses an alternate discussion prompt.)
3. My photographs and documents may be scanned and used (list attached).
4. My words, recordings, photographs and documents may be uploaded to the AI tools named on the information sheet, in the student’s district Google account (and, for image tools, the student’s district Adobe account).
5. A Teller may answer questions about me in the third person, quoting my words.
6. A Teller may speak as me in the first person, quoting my recorded words (living narrator; default no). The Teller is never public in any voice; it may be shown only by screen recording, as item 9 allows.
7. For a story about someone who has died: a Teller may speak as [name] in the first person, quoting the archive. Signed by the family member or estate holder: name, relationship, signature (default no). The archivist voice needs no extra consent.
8. The finished story and its key art may be shown: class only / unlisted link (default) / public. Name used: full name / first name only / no name.
9. A short screen recording of the Teller may appear on the student’s case-study page. The Teller itself is never public.
10. At the end of the semester the Teller is: deleted (default) / kept by the student until a stated week / transferred to me. The archive is copied to me and to the student’s personal storage: yes / no.
11. Restrictions: topics, people not to be named, material not to be shared, or a date before which something may not be public.

There is no item for synthesizing the narrator’s voice or likeness; that is not permitted in this course. That includes AI images, animation or video generated to resemble the narrator or any real, identifiable person. Students may draw or animate a narrator by hand, or stylize their own photographs of a narrator who agreed to item 3, because those are authored, not generated.

**Withdrawal:** the narrator may withdraw at any time. The student then removes the material from the Drive folder, Gemini Notebook sources, any image tool library, any discussion post and any posted video, and tells the instructor. Anything already seen by classmates cannot be unseen; the information sheet says so.

**Sensitive information:** students do not ask about immigration or legal status, legal cases, health details or criminal history. If a narrator raises them, the student offers to stop recording, and cuts those passages from every transcript before any AI upload. These details never go into the Teller or any public version, even with consent. Students do not upload identification numbers, financial, medical or immigration documents to any tool.

**Third parties:** people mentioned but not interviewed are not named or shown recognizably in public versions without a reason.

**Cultural protocols:** some stories belong to a community. Students ask the narrator what may be shared and with whom, record the answer, and do not upload restricted or sacred material to any AI tool or use it as a style reference. In Arizona, tribal nations hold sovereignty over cultural knowledge and imagery; students talk with the instructor before using such material (CONFIRM whether GCC has guidance or a liaison).

**Grief:** content notes appear in Getting Started and Weeks 1, 2 and 7 (grief, death, migration, AI recreations of people who have died), with links to GCC Counseling and the Dean of Students [links to confirm]. A student choosing a death within about the past year checks in with the instructor first; this is a conversation, not a gate. For someone who has died, the student chooses the archivist voice or first person; first person requires written consent from the family member or estate holder (release item 7). A Teller about someone who has died is never public in either voice and is shown only by screen recording. In D6, Tellers about someone who has died are shown only to classmates who opt in.

**Minors:**
- *Minor as narrator:* a guardian signs; the minor agrees on the recording; first name only or none in any shared version; no Teller outside the course; no recognizable images of the minor in public versions without guardian consent.
- *Minor as student:* interviews only adults the student already knows, or by phone or video call with a guardian aware; never alone with an unfamiliar adult. Guardian consent for any public posting.

**Recording law:** Arizona is a one-party consent state (A.R.S. § 13-3005; [Digital Media Law Project summary](https://www.dmlp.org/legal-guide/arizona-recording-law)). This course requires everyone recorded to agree anyway. Some states require all-party consent for calls; students interviewing across state lines follow the stricter rule. Students filming b-roll in public places avoid recognizable bystanders or ask permission.

### 4.5 Account access

- Every Maricopa student’s Gemini account has the same features (Michelle, 23 Oct 2026), so the course has one path for everyone. P1 still asks which features open, so the instructor can catch a setup problem early.
- If a feature is ever blocked for a student, the instructor can host that student’s Teller in a course account for the affected weeks (CONFIRM that a course account may be used this way). This is a contingency, not a standing path.
- Everyone shows their Teller by screen recording (D6, Project 1, the Final), which is also the publication rule.
- The SME panel has not read *Artificial* for mature content. Michelle should confirm it is suitable for dual-enrollment students or name an alternate reading path.

*Footnote (Google’s documentation, for reference only):* Google lists limits for Workspace for Education users under 18: they cannot edit uploaded images or generate from them in the Gemini app, and they do not get the AI features in Google Vids. Gemini in Docs, Sheets, Slides and Gmail is reported as 18 and over. Michelle reports no restricted accounts at Maricopa, so the course is not designed around these limits.

### 4.6 What to grade and what not to grade

| Grade this (student-authored) | Do not grade this |
|---|---|
| The story choice, logline and pitch | How human, witty or moving the Teller sounds |
| Moodboard selection and captions | The polish of AI-generated images, video or music on their own |
| Interview guide, follow-ups, listening and the narrator email | How much or how little AI a student used, when disclosed and within the rules |
| Archive selection, description, alt text and organization | Polish that comes from a tool preset or template |
| Teller instructions, guardrails, tests, fixes and critique | Drawing skill as such, apart from design decisions |
| Style frames, treatment, storyboard composition and design decisions | |
| Script writing in A/V format and revision (version history) | |
| Key art, titles and on-screen type | |
| Edit decisions: timing, pacing, motion, sound | |
| Licensing, consent, narrator review, disclosure | |
| Case-study copy, maker’s statement and the recorded walkthrough | |

## 5. Module-by-module content

Page conventions (order confirmed (Michelle, 23 Oct 2026)): every page opens with a GCC red title box that contains the page title, then Objectives, then Overview; module pages continue with Watch, Read/View, Practice, Assessment and Discussion. The heading “Objectives” and the objective list follow directly beneath it. The instructor notes box sits at the top of each module, is hidden from students in Canvas, and is the only place competency codes appear. Everything after the instructor notes box is student-facing. Glossary terms are defined at first use on each page and linked to the course glossary. Every resource, tool or site a page refers to is a working link.

**Standard deadline block.** Every assignment and discussion carries the same block, in this order: *Post for critique* (critiqued items only: day, noon, Discord channel) · *Due* (day and noon; what goes in Canvas) · *Hard close* (day and noon; 10% deduction between the deadline and the hard close; nothing accepted after). In Canvas, Due = the deadline, Available until = the hard close, and the Late Policy deducts 10% once. Each module opens on Monday at noon.

---

### Week 1 · Getting Started: What Is a Story?

> **Instructor notes (not student-facing)**
> **Competencies:** C1 (overview of storytelling); C8 (tools, setup).
> **Recording plan:** Welcome and course tour (6 to 8 min), including a direct statement about AI: other classes may treat AI as cheating; here, disclosed use inside the rules is expected, and the log protects the student. State plainly that this is a visual storytelling course that builds the writing visual professionals do at work, and walk through How Deadlines Work (two noon deadlines a week, posting for critique the day before, the 24-hour late window with a 10% deduction). Lecture 1.1, three segments of 12 to 15 min. Setup demo, four segments of about 15 min: installing Creative Cloud at home (Photoshop, Illustrator, InDesign, Premiere Pro, After Effects, Audition, Adobe Express) and checking for generative credits; Gemini, Gemini Notebook and Gemini in Docs, Sheets, Slides and Gmail with the district account; the Chromebook and phone backup path; include a two-minute tour of the three story forms with one example each, and joining the course Discord server. Setup FAQ video (about 15 min) answering the most common setup problems [instructor to create].
> **RSI cadence:** Monday-at-noon welcome announcement (captioned video). Comment in the D1 Discord channel on every post by Thursday at noon. Message anyone without P1 on Thursday afternoon, reminding them of the Friday-at-noon hard close. Friday summary announcement.
> **Seat time:** Direct 3.0 (welcome 0.5, lecture 0.75, setup demo 1.0, setup FAQ video 0.25, D1 critique 0.5). Outside 5.0 (Getting Started pages including How Deadlines Work, glossary, syllabus check and AI-Use Agreement 1.5; *Artificial* block 1 1.5; *Cookbook* Ch. 1 0.5; stories and NPR interview 0.5; P1 with the D1 post 1.0).
> **Common pitfalls:** personal Google accounts instead of district accounts; students who cannot join Discord (route them to the Canvas discussion fallback without comment); students thinking “personal story” means their most painful one; alt text that repeats the caption. Week 1’s deadline arrives on the fourth day of term; keep P1 light. *Artificial* page blocks must be set by Michelle with the book in hand.

[GCC red title box: Week 1 · Getting Started: What Is a Story?]

**Objectives**
By the end of this module, you will be able to:
1. Set up your district Google account, recording method, editing tools and Discord access for the semester.
2. Define a story and distinguish it from a topic.
3. Describe the semester project, the three story forms, the deadline rhythm, and how AI will be used in the course.

**Overview**
This module introduces the course, its tools and its project. It addresses the course outcome on understanding storytelling: what a story is, why stories matter, and the many ways a story can be presented. Across the semester you will choose a story that is personal to you, though not necessarily about you; record an interview with the person whose story it is (your **narrator**); gather an **archive** (the organized collection of recordings, images, documents and notes behind a story); build an AI **Teller** that can answer questions from that archive; and produce a short digital story for your portfolio in one of three forms: animation or motion comic, illustrated or photo documentary, or live-action or archival video. Along the way you will practice the writing that designers, animators and editors do at work: pitches, scripts, captions, alt text and case-study copy. Read the How Deadlines Work page before anything else: this week has one deadline, Thursday at noon.

*Content note:* this course includes stories about family, migration, death and grief, and an anchor text about an AI built from the writing of someone who has died. Support is available through [GCC Counseling](https://www.gccaz.edu/) [link to confirm] and the [Dean of Students](https://www.gccaz.edu/) [link to confirm]. You may always choose a different story.

**Watch**
- Welcome and Course Tour: the Living Archive project, the three story forms, how deadlines and critique work, how AI is used in this course, how each module is organized, and where to find help.
- Lecture 1.1, What Is a Story? Segment 1: a story versus a topic (“my grandmother” is a topic; “the summer she taught me to drive her truck” is a story). Segment 2: who tells, who listens, and why stories last. Segment 3: forms of presenting a story (spoken, written, drawn, audio, video, animation, interactive and AI-assisted) and an introduction to *Artificial: A Love Story*.
- Setup Demonstration: installing Adobe Creative Cloud and choosing your tool path; joining the course Discord server; recording clean audio on a phone; taking a well-composed phone photograph; writing a caption and alt text; signing in to Gemini and Gemini Notebook with your district account; Canvas notification settings.
- Setup FAQ video: answers to the most common setup problems.

**Read/View**
- How Deadlines Work (Getting Started).
- Amy Kurzweil, *Artificial: A Love Story* (Catapult, 2023), block 1 [pages to be set by the instructor]. Book page: [amykurzweil.com/artificial-a-love-story](https://amykurzweil.com/artificial-a-love-story/).
- Joe Lambert et al., [*Digital Storytelling Cookbook*](https://www.storycenter.org/s/cookbook.pdf), Chapter 1, “Stories in Our Lives,” pages 1 to 8 (free; CC BY-NC-SA 2.5).
- Three StoryCenter stories: [“Fences” by Emily Love](https://www.storycenter.org/stories/v/fences-by-emily-love), [“Remaking El Rancho” by Dulce Alonso](https://www.storycenter.org/stories/v/remaking-el-rancho-by-dulce-alonso), [“This Is My Home” by Bill Tall Bull](https://www.storycenter.org/stories/v/this-is-my-home-by-bill-tall-bull).
- NPR, [“Using AI, cartoonist Amy Kurzweil connects with deceased grandfather in *Artificial*”](https://www.npr.org/2023/10/19/1205898776/new-yorker-cartoonist-amy-kurzweils-graphic-memoir-artificial-a-love-story) (about 5 minutes, with transcript).

**Practice**
*P1 Setup Check*
*Deadlines:* Post for critique (with D1): Wednesday of Week 1 at noon, in Discord #d1-introductions · Due: Thursday of Week 1 at noon (Canvas: the items below plus your D1 critique record) · Hard close: Friday of Week 1 at noon (10% deduction between the deadline and the hard close; nothing accepted after).
1. Install Adobe Creative Cloud on your computer (your GCC account includes it at home), or, if you work on a Chromebook or phone, choose the backup path, using the Software and Recording Setup Guide, and name the story form you are leaning toward. You may change your form until M1.
2. Join the course Discord server (or tell the instructor if you need the Canvas discussion option).
3. Sign in to Gemini and Gemini Notebook with your district Google account. Report which features open, including image generation, Gemini Live, Deep Research, and Gemini in Docs and Gmail. In Creative Cloud, report whether Generative Fill and Firefly are available to you. Whatever opens, this report is complete; it helps the instructor support you.
4. Record a 30-second audio clip in the quietest space available to you (a closet full of clothes or a parked car works well), introducing yourself and naming one place that matters to you.
5. Photograph or draw that place. Write a one-sentence caption and a one-sentence alt text (alt text describes what is in the image for someone who cannot see it; a caption tells the viewer what it means). This image is also your D1 post.
6. Submit your tool path, form preference, feature report, the audio file, and the image with its caption and alt text.

*Syllabus and AI-Use Agreement*
*Deadlines:* No critique · Due: Thursday of Week 1 at noon (Canvas) · Hard close: Friday of Week 1 at noon (10% deduction between the deadline and the hard close; nothing accepted after).
1. Read the syllabus, the How Deadlines Work page, the AI in This Course page and the glossary.
2. Complete the syllabus check.
3. Sign the AI-Use Agreement, which confirms that you will keep a short AI-use log and disclose AI use in your projects.

**Assessment**
No milestone this week.

**Discussion · D1 Introductions: A Story Someone Told You**
*Prompt:* Post your P1 image of a place that matters to you. Introduce yourself: your name, your program, and one thing you hope to make this semester. Then share a story someone once told you that you still remember. What made it stay with you: the voice, the place, a detail, or the way it was told?
*Deadlines:* Post for critique: Wednesday of Week 1 at noon, Discord #d1-introductions · Due: Thursday of Week 1 at noon (Canvas record, submitted with P1) · Hard close: Friday of Week 1 at noon (10% deduction; nothing accepted after).
*Your post:* the image with 100 to 200 words, or a 2-minute audio or video post with captions or a transcript.
*Critique:* comments on at least three classmates’ posts by Thursday at noon, each naming one thing that made the story memorable and one thing the image shows.
*Instructor role:* comments on every post by Thursday at noon; posts a Friday summary naming the story elements that appeared across the class.

### Week 2 · The Power of Story: The Seven Steps and the Anchor Text

> **Instructor notes (not student-facing)**
> **Competencies:** C1; C3 (language, image and power).
> **Recording plan:** Lecture 2.1, four segments of about 15 min: story elements and the shape of change; Lambert’s Seven Steps from the *Cookbook* (one slide notes the older “seven elements” as history); forms and platforms, with one clip each from an animated, a photo-based and a live-action story; *Artificial* as a model of drawing, documents and a bot each doing different work. Demo (about 30 min): taking clean screenshots and annotating frames (shot type, what the image does); Gemini as a brainstorming partner for story starters, using the student’s own notes; writing a logline; making a quick AI study or thumbnail for a logline.
> **RSI cadence:** Monday-at-noon announcement with the content note repeated and the two deadlines named. Instructor posts two follow-up questions in the D2 channel by Wednesday evening. P2 feedback within 3 days of the Monday hard close.
> **Seat time:** Direct 2.5 (lecture 1.0, demo 0.5, critique for D2 and P2 1.0). Outside 5.5 (*Artificial* block 2 1.5; *Cookbook* Ch. 2 0.75; two stories and Ira Glass Part 1 0.5; Pixar in a Box “Visual Language” 0.5; D2 post 0.5; P2 1.75).
> **Common pitfalls:** analysis of *Artificial* that relies on AI summaries (AI cannot see the drawings in a print book); D2 requires a photo of a specific panel with a page number. Annotations that only describe (“a man walks”) without saying what the image does. Loglines that summarize a life. Confirm any scene referenced in a prompt against the book before publishing.

[GCC red title box: Week 2 · The Power of Story: The Seven Steps and the Anchor Text]

**Objectives**
By the end of this module, you will be able to:
1. Identify the elements of a story (character, desire, conflict, change, setting and point of view) in a published digital story.
2. Analyze a short digital story frame by frame, using Lambert’s Seven Steps and basic shot vocabulary.
3. Compare the ways words, drawings, documents and an AI each carry a story in *Artificial*.
4. Write a logline that names a person, a moment and what is at stake.

**Overview**
This module continues the course outcome on understanding storytelling and introduces the outcome on blending language, image and power. You will learn the method this course builds on, Joe Lambert’s Seven Steps, begin reading *Artificial* closely, and practice two kinds of professional writing: the frame annotation and the **logline** (a one-sentence description of a story that makes someone want to see it). This week has two deadlines: D2 on Thursday at noon and P2 on Sunday at noon.

**Watch**
- Lecture 2.1, The Power of Story. Segment 1: story elements and the shape of change. Segment 2: Lambert’s Seven Steps (Owning Your Insights, Owning Your Emotions, Finding the Moment, Seeing Your Story, Hearing Your Story, Assembling Your Story, Sharing Your Story). Segment 3: forms and platforms. Segment 4: what drawing, documents and a bot each contribute in *Artificial*.
- Demonstration: annotating frames; generating story starters with Gemini; writing a logline and making a quick visual study for it.

**Read/View**
- *Artificial*, block 2 [pages to be set by the instructor]. Read it before Wednesday; D2 asks for a specific panel.
- [*Digital Storytelling Cookbook*](https://www.storycenter.org/s/cookbook.pdf), Chapter 2, “Seven Steps of Digital Storytelling,” pages 9 to 24.
- Two stories in different visual forms: StoryCenter, [“Distance” by Marianna Corona](https://www.storycenter.org/stories/v/distance-by-marianna-corona) (photo-based), and StoryCorps, [“Q & A”](https://storycorps.org/animation/q-and-a/) (animated). Optional: StoryCenter podcast, [“My Grandmother’s Teaspoons”](https://www.storycenter.org/podcast-blog//season-one-episode-three-my-grandmothers-teaspoons), first 10 minutes, to hear what sound alone carries.
- [Ira Glass on Storytelling](https://www.openculture.com/2009/10/ira_glass_on_the_art_of_story_telling.html), Part 1 (about 5 minutes).
- Pixar in a Box, [Storytelling unit](https://www.khanacademy.org/computing/pixar/storytelling), “Visual Language” lesson (free; Khan Academy and Pixar; about 30 minutes).

**Practice**
*P2 Frame Breakdown and Loglines*
*Deadlines:* Post for critique: Saturday of Week 2 at noon, Discord #p2-frames-and-loglines · Due: Sunday of Week 2 at noon (Canvas: your work plus your critique record) · Hard close: Monday of Week 3 at noon (10% deduction between the deadline and the hard close; nothing accepted after).
1. Choose “Distance” or “Q & A.”
2. Take six screenshots that show how the story moves from beginning to turn to end.
3. Annotate each screenshot in one or two sentences: the shot or image type (close-up, wide, archival photograph, drawn panel), what the image does that the words do not, and, for at least two frames, which of the Seven Steps it shows. Use the frame breakdown template.
4. Write down three people, places or objects connected to your life. Ask Gemini to suggest 15 possible stories from them (suggested prompt: “Here are three people and places from my life. Suggest 15 possible short stories, each centered on one moment, not a whole life. Ask me one question about each.”).
5. Choose your three strongest starters and write a logline for each (one sentence, 25 words or fewer): whose story it is, the moment it centers on, and what is at stake.
6. Make one quick visual study for each logline: a thumbnail sketch, a phone photograph, or an AI-generated image that shows no real, identifiable person.
7. Post the annotated frames and the three loglines with their studies. Comment on at least three classmates’ posts by Sunday at noon.
8. Add a row to your AI-use log and submit the Canvas record.

**Assessment**
No milestone this week.

**Discussion · D2 Reading *Artificial*: Who Gets to Tell a Family Story?**
*Prompt:* Post a photo of one panel or page from *Artificial* (with the page number) where words and drawing together show something neither could show alone. Describe what each does. Then answer: in this book, who is telling the story, and who decides what the grandfather would have said?
*Deadlines:* Post for critique: Wednesday of Week 2 at noon, Discord #d2-artificial · Due: Thursday of Week 2 at noon (Canvas record) · Hard close: Friday of Week 2 at noon (10% deduction; nothing accepted after).
*Your post:* the photo with 100 to 200 words, or a 2-minute audio or video post with captions.
*Critique:* comments on at least three classmates’ posts by Thursday at noon.
*Instructor role:* two follow-up questions in the channel by Wednesday evening; Friday summary connecting posts to language, image and power.

### Week 3 · Voice, Place and Culture

> **Instructor notes (not student-facing)**
> **Competencies:** C2 (voice, place, culture); C5 (presenting story elements for peer review, through the D3 Story Circle).
> **Recording plan:** Lecture 3.1, four segments: voice (whose voice, the gift of a real voice); place as a character, including how color, light and texture carry place on screen; culture and language, including keeping a narrator’s own words, dialect and Spanish, and avoiding visual stereotypes in generated images; the danger of a single story. Demo (about 30 min): building a moodboard from the board template in Adobe Express or InDesign, generating palette, light and texture studies with Gemini image generation (Firefly if the account has credits), with a short Chromebook clip in Adobe Express; an optional Deep Research report on the story’s place or period, and how to check its sources; captioning a moodboard; tightening a pitch with Gemini without changing it into someone else’s story; recording ambient sound on a phone.
> **RSI cadence:** Monday-at-noon announcement. Instructor comments on every D3 pitch in the channel by Thursday at noon with one sentence (approve the scale, or narrow it), so students can use it before M1. **M1 feedback by Tuesday of Week 4 at noon**: approve or redirect, including the chosen form (steer first-time editors toward Form B). Private follow-up on any proposal involving a minor, a recent death, a community-held story or sensitive material.
> **Seat time:** Direct 2.5 (lecture 1.0, demo 0.5, critique for D3 and M1 1.0). Outside 5.5 (*Artificial* block 3 1.5; StudioBinder and Pixar in a Box “Pitching” 0.5; Adichie and two StoryCorps animations 0.5; D3 post 0.25; M1 2.75).
> **Common pitfalls:** stories too large (a whole life); narrators not yet asked; moodboards of 60 images with no point of view; generated images that lean on cultural stereotypes (address in feedback, gently and specifically). M1’s people list and risk plan go in Canvas only, not in Discord. No Arizona-specific place reading has been found; add one if located (see REVIEW_PACKET.md).

[GCC red title box: Week 3 · Voice, Place and Culture]

**Objectives**
By the end of this module, you will be able to:
1. Explain how voice, place and culture shape the meaning of a story, in words and in images.
2. Present a story idea to peers for feedback and respond to theirs in writing.
3. Propose a story for the semester project in a pitch package: logline, pitch, form, moodboard, narrator and consent plan.

**Overview**
This module addresses the course outcome on the impact of voice, place and culture in storytelling, and begins peer review of story elements. You will listen closely to how real people talk about real places, share your idea in a Story Circle (a StoryCenter workshop practice in which each person shares a story idea and hears what listeners want to know), and propose the story you will develop for the rest of the semester. A **moodboard** is a curated set of images that shows the look and feeling of a piece before it is made: its palette, light, texture and type. This week has two deadlines: D3 on Thursday at noon and M1 on Sunday at noon.

**Watch**
- Lecture 3.1, Voice, Place and Culture. Segment 1: voice, literal and figurative. Segment 2: place as a character. Segment 3: culture and language in the record and on screen. Segment 4: the danger of a single story.
- Demonstration: building and captioning a moodboard with AI studies; tightening a pitch with Gemini; recording ambient sound with a phone.

**Read/View**
- *Artificial*, block 3 [pages to be set by the instructor].
- StudioBinder, [“What Is a Mood Board”](https://www.studiobinder.com/blog/what-is-a-mood-board/).
- Pixar in a Box, [Storytelling unit](https://www.khanacademy.org/computing/pixar/storytelling), “Pitching” lesson.
- Chimamanda Ngozi Adichie, [“The Danger of a Single Story”](https://www.ted.com/talks/chimamanda_ngozi_adichie_the_danger_of_a_single_story) (TED, about 18 minutes).
- StoryCorps animations: [“Mi Abuela Panchita”](https://storycorps.org/animation/mi-abuela-panchita/) and [“Facundo the Great”](https://storycorps.org/animation/facundo-the-great/). Notice how the animators use color and drawn place to carry a voice.

**Practice**
No separate practice this week. The Story Circle (D3) is your practice.

**Assessment**
*M1 Pitch Package*
*Deadlines:* Post for critique (logline, pitch and moodboard only): Saturday of Week 3 at noon, Discord #m1-pitch · Due: Sunday of Week 3 at noon (Canvas: the full package plus your critique record) · Hard close: Monday of Week 4 at noon (10% deduction between the deadline and the hard close; nothing accepted after).
1. Choose your narrator from the narrator menu on the AI in This Course page (a family member, a community member you know, yourself through a self-interview, or another listed option). You do not need to give a reason for your choice.
2. Have a short, informal conversation with your narrator about the project. If your narrator is yourself, write a short paragraph about the place, object or memory you will explore.
3. Write a logline (25 words or fewer) and a pitch of 100 to 120 words: whose story this is, the one moment or turning point it will center on, and why it matters to you. Revise using at least one comment from the Story Circle.
4. Choose your story form (animation or motion comic; illustrated or photo documentary; live-action or archival video).
5. Build a moodboard of 12 to 20 images using the board template: your own photographs, archive images you already have, licensed images and AI studies. AI studies may explore palette, light, texture and setting; they may not show real, identifiable people. Label every generated image “AI study.”
6. Write a 50 to 80 word caption for the moodboard naming the palette, the type direction (for example, “hand-lettered titles” or “a plain sans serif”), the light and the feeling you are after.
7. In Canvas only: list every person whose voice, image or story will appear, and name one risk (access, time, sensitive material) and your plan for it.
8. Post the logline, pitch and moodboard. Comment on at least three classmates’ packages by Sunday at noon.
9. Set up your AI-use log from the template, add a row and submit the Canvas record.

**Discussion · D3 Story Circle: Whose Story, Which Moment?**
*Prompt:* Post your logline and one image from your moodboard. In your comments, use the critique protocol (I noticed, I wondered, What if) to tell classmates what you most want to know and whether the story fits in 2 to 4 minutes.
*Deadlines:* Post for critique: Wednesday of Week 3 at noon, Discord #d3-story-circle · Due: Thursday of Week 3 at noon (Canvas record) · Hard close: Friday of Week 3 at noon (10% deduction; nothing accepted after).
*Your post:* the logline and image with up to 100 words of context, or a 1-minute audio or video pitch with the image.
*Critique:* comments on at least three classmates’ pitches by Thursday at noon, so each maker can use them in M1.
*Instructor role:* comments on every pitch by Thursday at noon on scale and focus.

### Week 4 · Language, Image and Power: Consent and the AI Image

> **Instructor notes (not student-facing)**
> **Competencies:** C3; C2.
> **Recording plan:** Lecture 4.1, three segments: McCloud’s word and picture combinations (*Understanding Comics* Ch. 6, “Show and Tell”), with examples of each in animation and documentary; power: whose story it is, who in the family or community is left out, who holds the camera or the stylus, what a generated image claims; consent as part of craft (OHA narrator rights). Demo (about 30 min): generating an image with Gemini (and Firefly if the account has credits), reading Content Credentials (a tamper-evident label that records how an image was made), and writing a caption, alt text and disclosure line; writing a project email to a narrator from the template, with Help me write in Gmail where available; sending the information sheet and collecting an e-signature with a Google Form.
> **RSI cadence:** Monday-at-noon announcement; M1 feedback arrives Tuesday at noon, before any narrator email goes out. **M2 feedback by Tuesday of Week 5 at noon**, confirming the guide is ready and the interview may proceed.
> **Seat time:** Direct 2.5 (lecture 1.0, demo 0.5, critique for D4 and M2 1.0). Outside 5.5 (*Artificial* block 4 1.5; McCloud Ch. 6 0.5; OHA and Smithsonian forms 0.5; Rosner 0.25; D4 post 0.5; M2 2.25).
> **Common pitfalls:** generating an image of a real, identifiable relative (not allowed); guides full of yes/no questions; guides that ask about immigration status, legal or health matters (not allowed); narrator emails that are too casual or too long; posting the narrator agreement to Discord (Canvas only). McCloud Ch. 6 needs library e-reserve.

[GCC red title box: Week 4 · Language, Image and Power: Consent and the AI Image]

**Objectives**
By the end of this module, you will be able to:
1. Describe how language, image and power combine in a story about a real person.
2. Compare an AI-generated image with an archival or original image in what it claims and whose view it represents, and label each accurately.
3. Prepare an interview guide, write a clear project email to your narrator, and obtain your narrator’s agreement to be recorded.

**Overview**
This module addresses the course outcome on blending language, image and power, and continues the outcome on voice, place and culture. It treats consent as part of the craft of telling a real person’s story, and it treats your narrator the way a professional treats a client: with a clear explanation of what you will make and how it will be shown. Wait for your M1 feedback (Tuesday at noon) before you email your narrator. This week has two deadlines: D4 on Thursday at noon and M2 on Sunday at noon.

**Watch**
- Lecture 4.1, Language, Image and Power. Segment 1: how words and pictures combine. Segment 2: whose story it is, and who is left out. Segment 3: consent and the narrator’s rights.
- Demonstration: generating and labeling an AI image; reading Content Credentials; writing alt text and a disclosure line; writing the narrator email; collecting an e-signature.

**Read/View**
- *Artificial*, block 4 [pages to be set by the instructor]. You will have finished the book by the end of this week.
- Scott McCloud, *Understanding Comics*, Chapter 6, “Show and Tell” (library e-reserve [link to confirm]).
- Oral History Association, [“For Participants in Oral History Interviews”](https://oralhistory.org/wp-content/uploads/2025/12/2025-OHA-PrinciplesBP_For-Participants-in-OH-Interviews.pdf). You will give a copy to your narrator.
- Smithsonian, [*Folklife and Oral History Interviewing Guide*](https://folklife.si.edu/the-smithsonian-folklife-and-oral-history-interviewing-guide/smithsonian), sample information and release forms.
- Helen Rosner, [“The Ethics of a Deepfake Anthony Bourdain Voice in ‘Roadrunner’”](https://www.newyorker.com/culture/annals-of-gastronomy/the-ethics-of-a-deepfake-anthony-bourdain-voice), *The New Yorker* (may be metered).
- Optional: Content Authenticity Initiative, [“How it works”](https://contentauthenticity.org/how-it-works).

**Practice**
No separate practice this week. The image pairing for D4 is your practice.

**Assessment**
*M2 Interview Guide and Narrator Agreement*
*Deadlines:* Post for critique (the interview guide only): Saturday of Week 4 at noon, Discord #m2-interview-guide · Due: Sunday of Week 4 at noon (Canvas: the guide, a copy of your email, the agreement, the interview day, and your critique record) · Hard close: Monday of Week 5 at noon (10% deduction between the deadline and the hard close; nothing accepted after).
1. Use StoryCorps [“Great Questions”](https://storycorps.org/participate/great-questions/) and your M1 pitch to ask Gemini for 20 candidate questions. Cut them to 10 to 12 open questions that ask for scenes (“Take me to the day…”), ordered from easy to deeper, with one planned follow-up each. Add “Is there anything I didn’t ask that I should have?” Include two questions that ask your narrator to describe what something looked like (a room, a face, a street), because those answers become pictures.
2. Check the guide against the interview rules: no questions about immigration or legal status, legal cases or health details.
3. After your M1 feedback, write a project email or message to your narrator (150 to 200 words) from the template: what you are making, what you will record, how long it will take, how the finished piece will be shown, and that they may say no to anything. You may use Help me write in Gmail, if your account has it, to tighten the message; it must still sound like you. Send it with the Project Information Sheet and the OHA “For Participants” page.
4. Obtain your narrator’s agreement to be recorded by e-signature, a photo of a signed page, or a text or email reply. (Self-interview: complete the form for yourself.)
5. Schedule the interview for 20 to 30 minutes, in the quietest place available, between Tuesday of Week 5 at noon and Wednesday of Week 6. Record the planned day.
6. Post the guide (no names or private details). Comment on at least three classmates’ guides by Sunday at noon, with at least one comment suggesting a follow-up question.
7. Add a row to your AI-use log and submit the Canvas record.

**Discussion · D4 Image and Power: The AI Image and the Real Family**
*Prompt:* Post two images connected to your story: one of your own photographs or an archival image you have permission to share, and one AI-generated image that does not show a real, identifiable person. Give each a one-line caption and a one-line alt text, and give the AI image a disclosure line naming the tool. What does each image claim? Whose view does it represent? Then respond to the Rosner article: where should a storyteller draw the line between illustration and fabrication?
*Deadlines:* Post for critique: Wednesday of Week 4 at noon, Discord #d4-image-and-power · Due: Thursday of Week 4 at noon (Canvas record) · Hard close: Friday of Week 4 at noon (10% deduction; nothing accepted after).
*Your post:* the two images with 100 to 200 words.
*Critique:* comments on at least three classmates’ posts by Thursday at noon.
*Instructor role:* mid-cycle post on the Archival Producers Alliance guidelines; Friday summary.

### Week 5 · Gathering: The Interview

> **Instructor notes (not student-facing)**
> **Competencies:** C4 (methods and techniques); C8 (recording).
> **Recording plan:** Lecture 5.1, two segments of about 15 min: the interview as listening (scene-seeking questions, follow-ups, interviewing someone about loss); recording technique (room, phone distance, airplane mode, the **slate**, room tone) and shooting reference: photographing the place, hands, objects and light while you are there. Demo (about 30 min): Gemini Live rehearsal, spoken, with typed role-play as the fallback (say plainly that it exists for nervous interviewers); recording a slate and room tone; shooting ten reference photographs or short b-roll clips on a phone (wide, medium, detail); laying out a visual journal page. Studio Session (30 min, live, recorded, required to watch): interview clinic with student questions.
> **RSI cadence:** Monday-at-noon announcement. **M2 feedback Tuesday at noon** opens the interview window (Tuesday of Week 5 at noon to Wednesday of Week 6). Friday check-in message to anyone who has not reported an interview day. M3 is due Thursday of Week 6.
> **Seat time:** Direct 2.5 (lecture 0.5, demo 0.5, Studio Session 0.5, critique for P3 and P4 1.0). Outside 5.5 (readings and model interviews, StudioBinder camera shots 1.0; P3 0.75; P4 1.5; M3 part 1, the interview itself, 2.25).
> **Common pitfalls:** interrupting; skipping room tone; uploading the recording to AI before the release (not allowed); photographing the narrator without asking; journal pages that become essays (the caption limit is the point). **At-risk weeks 5 and 6:** narrator cancellations (see M3).

[GCC red title box: Week 5 · Gathering: The Interview]

**Objectives**
By the end of this module, you will be able to:
1. Apply interview techniques, including scene-seeking questions and follow-ups, to gather a personal story.
2. Record an interview with clean, usable audio and gather visual reference from the story’s places.
3. Use a visual field journal to develop your own connection to the story.

**Overview**
This module begins the unit on crafting a personal story and addresses the course outcome on the principles, methods and techniques used to craft one. The interview is your primary source. While you are there, you will also gather visual reference: the light, objects and places that will become frames later. This week has two deadlines: P3 on Thursday at noon and P4 on Sunday at noon. Your interview window opens Tuesday at noon and runs through Wednesday of Week 6; rehearse (P3) before you interview.

**Watch**
- Lecture 5.1, The Interview as Listening. Segment 1: listening, questions and follow-ups, and interviewing someone about loss. Segment 2: recording technique and shooting reference. A **slate** is a short spoken introduction at the start of a recording (your name, the narrator’s name, the place, the week, and the narrator saying they agree to be recorded). **Room tone** is 30 seconds of the room’s natural silence, used to smooth edits. **B-roll** is supporting footage or photographs (places, hands, objects) that you cut over a voice.
- Demonstration: rehearsing with Gemini; recording a slate and room tone; shooting reference and b-roll; laying out a visual journal page.
- Studio Session: interview clinic.

**Read/View**
- Nate Rott, [“Feign ignorance, demystify the mic and other audio interview tips”](https://www.npr.org/sections/npr-training/2025/05/28/g-s1-64689/feign-ignorance-demystify-the-mic-and-other-audio-interview-tips), NPR Training.
- Dana Lynn Driscoll, [“Introduction to Primary Research”](https://pressbooks.gvsu.edu/writingspaces2/chapter/introduction-to-primary-research-observations-surveys-and-interviews/), *Writing Spaces*, Vol. 2, Chapter 10, interview sections only.
- Global Center for Journalism & Trauma, [“Interviewing in the aftermath of trauma”](https://gcjt.org/resources/interviewing-aftermath-trauma).
- StudioBinder, [“Ultimate Guide to Camera Shots”](https://www.studiobinder.com/blog/ultimate-guide-to-camera-shots/), the sections on wide, medium and close-up shots (for your reference photographs).
- Model interviews: StoryCorps [“Q & A”](https://storycorps.org/animation/q-and-a/) (review) and [“No More Questions!”](https://storycorps.org/animation/no-more-questions/) (a reluctant narrator who opens up).

**Practice**
*P3 Interview Rehearsal*
*Deadlines:* Post for critique (your three changes only): Wednesday of Week 5 at noon, Discord #p3-rehearsal · Due: Thursday of Week 5 at noon (Canvas: the chat share link, your three changes and your critique record) · Hard close: Friday of Week 5 at noon (10% deduction between the deadline and the hard close; nothing accepted after).
1. Write a two-sentence description of a narrator for Gemini to play (for example, “a grandfather who answers briefly and changes the subject when asked about his first job”). Do not use private details about your real narrator.
2. Ask Gemini to play that narrator while you ask questions from your guide for about 10 minutes. Use Gemini Live, if your account has it, so you rehearse out loud; otherwise type the exchange.
3. Write down three changes to your guide or follow-ups, and the one follow-up that worked best.
4. Post the three changes and the best follow-up. Comment on at least three classmates’ posts by Thursday at noon.
5. Submit the Canvas record with the chat share link (Canvas only).

*P4 Visual Field Journal*
*Deadlines:* Post for critique: Saturday of Week 5 at noon, Discord #p4-field-journal · Due: Sunday of Week 5 at noon (Canvas: the three pages plus your critique record) · Hard close: Monday of Week 6 at noon (10% deduction between the deadline and the hard close; nothing accepted after).
1. Make three visual journal pages about your connection to the story: one about a person, one about a place, and one about a memory. Each page is mainly visual: a sketch, a collage, your own reference photographs, or a mix, in the spirit of Lynda Barry’s diary pages. Use the page template or a sketchbook.
2. For the place page, use at least three reference photographs or short b-roll clips you shot on location (or, if you cannot get there, photographs of objects or rooms that stand in for it).
3. Write a sensory caption of 40 to 60 words for each page: a sound, a smell, a texture, a phrase someone always said. Every word should earn its place.
4. You may ask Gemini for journaling prompts, and you may use AI image generation for a background texture, labeled; the pages themselves and their captions are yours. Do not show your narrator in a generated image.
5. Post the three pages (leave out anything private). Comment on at least three classmates’ journals by Sunday at noon.
6. Submit the Canvas record.

**Assessment**
*M3 The Interview* is conducted this week and due Thursday of Week 6 at noon. Instructions are on the Week 6 page and the M3 assignment page; read them before your interview.

**Discussion**
No discussion this week. Use the Course Questions board and the Studio Session.

### Week 6 · Building the Archive

> **Instructor notes (not student-facing)**
> **Competencies:** C4; C10 (archiving); C8 (scanning).
> **Recording plan:** Lecture 6.1, four segments: from interview to archive; what an archive is (the preserved files, descriptions and consent records) and how a Teller differs (one way to search it); file naming and folders; describing items with **metadata** (information about an item: what it is, who made it, when, and who may use it) and writing alt text. Demo (about 30 min): phone scanning; photographing an object as a still life (window light, plain background, three angles); transcription first draft with Premiere Pro speech-to-text and correcting an excerpt; the metadata sheet with Gemini in Sheets (or the Gemini app) drafting descriptions and alt text.
> **RSI cadence:** Monday-at-noon announcement. Instructor posts a model still-life and caption in the D5 channel. **M3 feedback by Sunday of Week 6 at noon.** M4 is due Thursday of Week 7; students start it this week.
> **Seat time:** Direct 2.5 (lecture 1.0, demo 0.5, critique for M3 and D5 1.0). Outside 5.5 (LoC handouts and video, WebAIM alt text 0.75; M3 part 2 0.75; D5 post 0.75; M4 part 1 3.25).
> **Common pitfalls:** uploading before the release; dates invented for undated items; files named IMG_4492; alt text that starts “image of”; posting interview audio to Discord (never; M3’s critique post is reference photographs only). Scanning may continue into Week 12. **Narrator cancellations:** a student who tells the instructor by Wednesday may submit M3 by Monday of Week 7 at noon with no deduction.

[GCC red title box: Week 6 · Building the Archive]

**Objectives**
By the end of this module, you will be able to:
1. Complete and document an interview recording with its reference photographs.
2. Organize an archive with consistent file names, folders and descriptions.
3. Digitize documents and photographs, and photograph objects, with the equipment you have.
4. Correct an AI transcript so that it preserves your narrator’s own words.

**Overview**
This module continues the outcome on crafting a personal story and begins the outcome on archiving the story on relevant technology. Your **archive** is the preserved set of files, descriptions and consent records behind your story. **Alt text** is a short written description of an image for people who use screen readers; good alt text is specific and brief, and it says what matters in the image for this story. This week has two deadlines: M3 on Thursday at noon and D5 on Sunday at noon. You will also start M4, which is due Thursday of Week 7.

**Watch**
- Lecture 6.1, From Interview to Archive. Segment 1: what an archive is. Segment 2: naming and folders. Segment 3: describing items and writing alt text. Segment 4: the archive and the Teller.
- Demonstration: scanning with a phone; photographing an object as a still life; a transcription first draft; the metadata sheet.

**Read/View**
- Library of Congress, Personal Digital Archiving: [“Scanning Your Personal Collections”](https://digitalpreservation.gov/personalarchiving/documents/scanning_collections.pdf), [“Keeping Personal Digital Photographs”](https://digitalpreservation.gov/personalarchiving/documents/PA_photo.pdf), [“Keeping Personal Digital Audio”](https://digitalpreservation.gov/personalarchiving/documents/PA_Audio.pdf), and the video [“Adding Descriptions to Digital Photos”](https://digitalpreservation.gov/multimedia/videos/personalarchiving-photometadata.html).
- WebAIM, [“Alternative Text”](https://webaim.org/techniques/alttext/), the sections on context and on writing effective alt text.

**Practice**
No separate practice this week.

**Assessment**
*M3 The Interview*
*Deadlines:* Post for critique (reference photographs only, never interview audio): Wednesday of Week 6 at noon, Discord #m3-reference · Due: Thursday of Week 6 at noon (Canvas: recording, room tone, ambient sound, shoot log, release status and your critique record) · Hard close: Friday of Week 6 at noon (10% deduction between the deadline and the hard close; nothing accepted after).
1. Conduct your interview (20 to 30 minutes) in the window (Tuesday of Week 5 at noon to Wednesday of Week 6) using your guide. Start with the slate, including your narrator’s spoken agreement.
2. Record 30 seconds of room tone, and one minute of ambient sound from a place in the story if you can get there.
3. Close by asking, “Is there anything you said that you would rather I not use?” Note the answer.
4. Back up the recording in two places the same day.
5. Within 24 hours, write a shoot log of about 100 words using the template: setting, light, sound, anything off the record, concerns, and the reference shots you captured.
6. Offer your narrator the release (it may be signed now or within the next week).
7. Post three reference photographs from the interview location (no people unless they agreed to class sharing). Comment on at least three classmates’ photographs by Thursday at noon, with one comment on light or framing.
8. Add a row to your AI-use log and submit the Canvas record.

If your narrator has to reschedule, tell the instructor by Wednesday of Week 6; you may then submit M3 by Monday of Week 7 at noon with no deduction.

*M4 Release, Transcript and Archive Inventory* (start this week; due Thursday of Week 7 at noon; see the Week 7 page for its deadline block). This week, complete steps 1 to 5: release, transcript draft, the corrected excerpt, the folder, and scanning.

**Discussion · D5 The Archive: One Object, Many Stories**
*Prompt:* Photograph one object from your archive as a still life: deliberate light, a plain background, and framing that shows what matters about it (only if your release allows class sharing; otherwise use an object from your own life). Post the photograph with a 60 to 80 word caption that tells its story, a one-line alt text, and one question for your classmates. In your comments, guess the story behind a classmate’s object from the photograph before reading the caption, then compare, and comment on one lighting or framing choice.
*Deadlines:* Post for critique: Saturday of Week 6 at noon, Discord #d5-one-object · Due: Sunday of Week 6 at noon (Canvas record) · Hard close: Monday of Week 7 at noon (10% deduction; nothing accepted after).
*Your post:* the photograph, caption, alt text and question.
*Critique:* comments on at least three classmates’ posts by Sunday at noon.
*Instructor role:* posts a model still-life and caption; Monday summary on how framing and description change what an object means.

### Week 7 · Building the Teller

> **Instructor notes (not student-facing)**
> **Competencies:** C10; C3.
> **Recording plan:** Lecture 7.1, three segments of about 15 min: what a language model does, in plain terms; **grounding** (an AI answering from a given set of sources) and why grounded tools still invent or overreach when the archive has a gap; Fredbot versus a Gemini Notebook: per Kurzweil’s NPR interview, Fredbot answers with lines drawn from her grandfather’s letters and notes, so its failures are choosing the wrong real sentence; Gemini Notebook cites its sources but paraphrases and summarizes, and can fill gaps with plausible inventions. That is why the Teller must quote and cite. (Confirm the Fredbot details against the book before recording.) Guided lab, five segments of about 15 min with pause points and a written checklist: creating a notebook and loading sources; the instruction scaffold, placed in the notebook’s custom instruction setting or loaded as a “Teller Instructions” source (CONFIRM which the student accounts support); voice and the release; **guardrails** (rules the Teller must follow); testing. Mention that Gemini skills may later add reusable behaviors to the Teller once school accounts have them; nothing graded depends on them. Studio Session (live, recorded, required to watch): Teller troubleshooting, ending with a short introduction to Project 1 style frames (one example per form).
> **Under the Hood option:** a prepared Colab notebook trains a tiny character-level model on a public-domain text; students only run cells. The write-up adds the grounded-tool paragraph. Recorded walkthrough if Colab is unavailable [instructor to create].
> **RSI cadence:** Monday-at-noon announcement with content note. This week has no critique: both items contain private material. **M4 feedback by Sunday of Week 7 at noon; P5 feedback by Tuesday of Week 8 at noon**, before Project 1.
> **Seat time:** Direct 3.0 (lecture 0.75, guided lab 1.25, Studio Session 1.0). Outside 5.0 (Path A or B 0.75; Dimensions in Testimony 0.5; M4 part 2 1.25; P5 2.0; Project 1 start 0.5).
> **Common pitfalls:** one-line instructions; first-person voice without written consent (release item 6 for a living narrator, item 7 for someone who has died); treating fluent answers as accurate; starting style frames before choosing the archive item.

[GCC red title box: Week 7 · Building the Teller]

**Objectives**
By the end of this module, you will be able to:
1. Explain how a grounded AI tool answers questions from a set of sources and why it can still invent information.
2. Complete an archive inventory with metadata and alt text.
3. Build a Teller grounded in your archive, with written instructions and guardrails, and test it.

**Overview**
This module addresses the course outcome on archiving the story on relevant technology and returns to language, image and power. Your **Teller** is a Gemini Notebook (formerly NotebookLM): a notebook that answers only from the sources you load, with citations, guided by instructions you write. It makes your archive searchable in plain language. It does not replace the story you will write, design and produce. Writing the Teller’s instructions is a form of professional writing: every sentence must be clear enough for a machine to follow and specific enough to prevent a known problem. This week has two deadlines, neither with critique: M4 on Thursday at noon and P5 on Sunday at noon.

*Content note:* this week’s optional reading on chatbots built from people who have died discusses grief and a young person’s death. An alternate reading is listed.

**Watch**
- Lecture 7.1, How a Teller Works. Segment 1: language models in plain terms. Segment 2: grounding, and why gaps lead to invention. Segment 3: Fredbot and Gemini Notebook: selecting a real sentence versus paraphrasing and filling gaps.
- Guided Lab (follow along, with the written checklist): creating a notebook and loading sources; completing the instruction scaffold; choosing the voice; writing guardrails; testing.
- Studio Session: Teller troubleshooting, and a short introduction to style frames for Project 1.

**Read/View** (choose one path; both take about 45 minutes)
- *Path A, Reading:* MIT Sloan Teaching & Learning Technologies, [“When AI Gets It Wrong: Addressing AI Hallucinations and Bias”](https://mitsloanedtech.mit.edu/ai/basics/addressing-ai-hallucinations-and-bias/); then **either** Jason Fagone, [“The Jessica Simulation,” Part 1](https://www.sfchronicle.com/projects/2021/jessica-simulation-artificial-intelligence/) (*San Francisco Chronicle*; content note: grief) **or** University of Cambridge, [“Call for safeguards to prevent unwanted ‘hauntings’ by AI chatbots of dead loved ones”](https://www.cam.ac.uk/research/news/call-for-safeguards-to-prevent-unwanted-hauntings-by-ai-chatbots-of-dead-loved-ones).
- *Path B, Under the Hood Lab:* open the course Colab notebook [instructor to create], run each cell in order, and watch a small model learn to imitate a text. Write 100 to 150 words explaining why it produces text that sounds right but is not true, and how a grounded tool is different.
- Everyone: USC Shoah Foundation, [Dimensions in Testimony overview](https://sfi.usc.edu/dit) and [FAQ](https://sfi.usc.edu/dit/faq), and 10 minutes speaking with [Pinchas Gutter on IWitness](https://iwitness.usc.edu/dit/pinchas) (free account may be required). Notice that every answer is the person’s real recorded words.
- Reference: Google, [“Learn about Gemini Notebook”](https://support.google.com/notebooklm/answer/16164461).

**Practice**
*P5 Teller Prototype*
*Deadlines:* No critique (the Teller Exchange in Week 8 is where classmates see it) · Due: Sunday of Week 7 at noon (Canvas) · Hard close: Monday of Week 8 at noon (10% deduction between the deadline and the hard close; nothing accepted after).
1. Create a Gemini Notebook and load at least eight cleared archive items.
2. Warm-up: ask three questions before you write any instructions, and note one surprise.
3. Complete the instruction scaffold in your own words: Who are you? Whose archive do you hold? What voice do you use (the archivist voice, or first person if you have the written consent it requires)? What must you never do? What do you say when the archive does not contain the answer? Place it in the notebook’s instruction setting, or load it as a source titled “Teller Instructions.” You may ask Gemini to tighten your wording.
4. Ask 10 questions: at least six the archive can answer and at least three it cannot.
5. Rate each answer accurate, partly accurate, or invented, in the test log template.
6. Submit a screenshot of your Teller, your warm-up note, your completed scaffold, the log and the notebook share link (shared with the instructor only).

**Assessment**
*M4 Release, Transcript and Archive Inventory*
*Deadlines:* No critique (private archive material) · Due: Thursday of Week 7 at noon (Canvas) · Hard close: Friday of Week 7 at noon (10% deduction between the deadline and the hard close; nothing accepted after).
1. Collect your narrator’s signed release (or your own, for a self-interview). Until item 4 is checked, nothing from the interview goes into any AI tool.
2. Create a transcript first draft with Premiere Pro speech-to-text or Gemini Notebook (or by hand or a local tool if you are still waiting for the release). Cut any passages about immigration or legal status, legal cases or health details, and anything your narrator asked you not to use.
3. Choose the 10 minutes you are most likely to use and correct that excerpt fully against the audio, keeping your narrator’s exact words. Label the rest “machine draft, uncorrected.”
4. Create your archive folder from the course template (01_Interview, 02_Images, 03_Documents, 04_Audio, 05_Journal, 06_Consent, 07_Production).
5. Add at least 10 items, named YYYY-MM-DD_subject_description (use “ca1975” or “undated” when the date is unknown). Scan with a phone scan app at its highest-quality setting, or at 600 dpi on a flatbed if you have one; GCC library scanners are available. Photograph objects as still lifes. Your P4 journal pages and reference photographs count as items.
6. Describe every item in the metadata sheet: title, date (marked as a guess where it is one), creator, description, source, release status. Gemini may draft descriptions; check every name and date.
7. Write alt text (one or two sentences) for the five images you are most likely to use. Gemini may draft it; revise it so it says what matters for your story.
8. Add a row to your AI-use log and submit the release (or its status), the corrected excerpt, the folder link (shared with the instructor only), and the metadata sheet with alt text.

Project 1 begins this week: read the Project 1 assignment page and choose the archive item for your micro-story. Project 1 is due Sunday of Week 8 at noon.

**Discussion**
No discussion this week. Use the Studio Session and the Course Questions board. The Teller Exchange is in Week 8.

### Week 8 · Project 1: The Teller and the Micro-Story

> **Instructor notes (not student-facing)**
> **Competencies:** C2, C3, C4, C8, C10.
> **Recording plan:** Lecture 8.1, two segments of about 15 min, including the model micro-story: the micro-story (one item, one moment, one turn) and the two-column A/V script (words on the left, picture on the right; the picture column does work the words do not); style frames: building three to five finished stills that set the look (composition, palette, type, texture), one example per form, and how to use Gemini image generation (or Firefly Style Reference, if the account has credits) to explore before committing. Demo (about 30 min): recording narration into Audition, with a phone voice memo as the backup; Enhance Speech; laying style frames on a timeline with a pan, zoom or parallax move; mixing a narrator clip and ambient sound; auto-captions. Model micro-story [instructor to create: Michelle records a 45 to 60 second piece from her own family archive with a narrator clip, three style frames and captions; shown inside the lecture].
> **RSI cadence:** Monday-at-noon announcement repeating the minimum viable spec. **P5 feedback Tuesday at noon.** Mid-semester check-in: a short individual video or written note on each student’s progress (reviewing it counts as direct time). **Project 1 feedback by Thursday of Week 9 at noon** (4 days), style-frame and micro-story notes first. Ask three held-out questions of each Teller during grading.
> **Seat time:** Direct 2.5 (lecture 0.5, demo 0.5, mid-semester feedback 0.5, critique for D6 and Project 1 1.0). Outside 5.5 (D6 post 0.25; survey 0.25; Project 1 5.0: Teller tests and fixes 1.0, design form 0.25, A/V micro-script 0.75, style frames 1.25, narration recording 0.5, edit and captions 0.75, AI-use statement 0.25, Teller screen recording 0.25).
> **Common pitfalls:** spending the whole week on the Teller; micro-stories that summarize a life; no narrator clip; animators fully animating the micro-story (limited motion only); style frames that are all generated (the rubric grades authored design decisions). **At-risk week** (Project 1 is estimated tightly).

[GCC red title box: Week 8 · Project 1: The Teller and the Micro-Story]

**Objectives**
By the end of this module, you will be able to:
1. Construct and test a Teller grounded in your archive, and document how you improved it.
2. Write a 120 to 150 word micro-story script in two-column A/V format, centered on one archive item and using your narrator’s recorded voice.
3. Design three to five style frames that establish the look of your story.
4. Produce a captioned 45 to 60 second motion micro-story with clean narration and ambient sound.

**Overview**
This module completes Project 1, the course’s first major project. It draws on the outcomes for voice, place and culture; language, image and power; crafting a personal story; using recording and editing tools; and archiving. A **style frame** is a finished still image that shows exactly how a piece will look. The style frames you make this week become the visual direction for your final story, and both parts of Project 1 become components of your Final Project. This week has two deadlines: D6 on Thursday at noon and Project 1 on Sunday at noon.

**Watch**
- Lecture 8.1, The Micro-Story and the Style Frame, with the instructor’s model micro-story. Segment 1: one item, one moment, one turn, written in A/V format. Segment 2: style frames that set the look.
- Demonstration: recording narration; cleaning audio with Enhance Speech; moving style frames on a timeline; mixing a narrator clip and ambient sound; captions.

**Read/View**
- The Project 1 assignment page and rubric.
- StoryCorps, [“Sundays at Rocco’s”](https://storycorps.org/animation/sundays-at-roccos/), first minute: notice how quickly the drawn place locates you, and what the picture does while the voice talks.
- Optional, for narration: NPR, [“Starting Your Podcast: A Guide for Students”](https://www.npr.org/2018/11/15/662070097/starting-your-podcast-a-guide-for-students), the “Podcast Production” section (scripting, voicing, mixing).

**Practice**
*Mid-semester survey*
*Deadlines:* No critique · Due: Thursday of Week 8 at noon (Canvas survey) · Hard close: Friday of Week 8 at noon. The survey shares the D6 deadline time; it is not a separate deadline.

**Assessment**
*Project 1: The Teller and the Micro-Story*
*Deadlines:* Post for critique (the micro-story MP4 only): Saturday of Week 8 at noon, Discord #project-1-micro-story · Due: Sunday of Week 8 at noon (Canvas: the full submission plus your critique record) · Hard close: Monday of Week 9 at noon (10% deduction between the deadline and the hard close; nothing accepted after).
The full scenario, steps, submission list, minimum viable spec and rubric are on the assignment page (Section 3.3). AI-use expectations: AI may help draft and tighten Teller instructions, draft transcripts, suggest edits to your micro-story draft, explore looks for your style frames and clean audio. The micro-story’s wording, your narration and your final style frames are yours, and your narrator’s clip must be their real recorded voice. Label any generated element. Submit the full AI-use statement. Post only the micro-story (as your release allows); the Teller link, logs and design form go in Canvas only. Comment on at least three classmates’ micro-stories by Sunday at noon, with one comment on the style frames and one on timing.

**Discussion · D6 Teller Exchange: Ask a Classmate’s Teller**
*Prompt:* Post a 2-minute screen recording of your Week 7 Teller answering two questions, and one sentence about whose archive it holds. (If your Teller is about someone who has died, mark the post “opt-in” in its first line; classmates choose whether to view it. If your release does not allow class sharing, post a recording with private details cut, or answer the alternate prompt: describe one fix you made and why, with a before-and-after screenshot.) In your comments, ask a classmate’s Teller two questions; the owner runs them and replies with the answers by Thursday at noon. Report one thing you learned and one place the Teller could not answer well.
*Deadlines:* Post for critique: Wednesday of Week 8 at noon, Discord #d6-teller-exchange · Due: Thursday of Week 8 at noon (Canvas record) · Hard close: Friday of Week 8 at noon (10% deduction; nothing accepted after).
*Your post:* the screen recording and one sentence.
*Critique:* questions for at least three classmates’ Tellers by Wednesday evening, and answers to the questions asked of your Teller by Thursday at noon.
*Instructor role:* asks questions of at least five Tellers in the channel; Friday summary of common fixes.
