### Week 9 · Shaping the Story: Structure and the A/V Script

> **Instructor notes (not student-facing)**
> **Competencies:** C4; C5 (peer review).
> **Recording plan:** Lecture 9.1, four segments: the **dramatic question** (the question the story makes the audience want answered) and the turn; structures (chronological, circular, braided, and kishōtenketsu, a four-part structure of introduction, development, twist and reconciliation that turns on a new fact rather than a fight; if Dan Harmon’s Story Circle is mentioned, name it as his), shown as beat boards; scene and summary, and how a picture can carry summary so the narration does not have to; writing narration for the ear in A/V format (short sentences, one idea per line, the picture column naming shots and on-screen type). Demo (about 30 min): **paper edit by search** (asking the Teller to list every passage about one moment with timestamps; the biggest time saver in the course); building a beat board with thumbnails; asking Gemini for three alternate orders of the student’s own beats.
> **RSI cadence:** Monday-at-noon announcement stating plainly: “Your beat board is due Thursday at noon and your script draft Sunday at noon; post each the day before.” Instructor comments in the Beat Board channel before Friday at noon. **Project 1 feedback Thursday at noon**, before M5. Instructor comments on at least one third of drafts in the D7 channel. **M5 feedback by Wednesday of Week 10 at noon.** Link GCC writing tutoring [link to confirm].
> **Seat time:** Direct 2.5 (lecture 1.0, demo 0.5, critique for the Beat Board Checkpoint and M5 1.0). Outside 5.5 (readings 1.25; Beat Board Checkpoint 1.5: paper edit 0.75, beat board 0.75; M5 2.75: A/V draft 2.5, log and version history 0.25).
> **Common pitfalls:** essays instead of scripts; picture columns that say “photo of grandma” for every line; narration that describes what the picture already shows; AI-rewritten drafts (version history and timestamped quotes guard against this). Model scripts: *Cookbook* Ch. 3 plus Michelle’s Week 8 model, reformatted as A/V [instructor to create]; two transcribed StoryCenter or StoryCorps scripts if permission is confirmed.

[GCC red title box: Week 9 · Shaping the Story: Structure and the A/V Script]

**Objectives**
By the end of this module, you will be able to:
1. Apply a story structure to organize material from your archive, using a beat board.
2. Draft a narration script in two-column A/V format for a 2 to 4 minute digital story.
3. Present your draft for peer review and give structured written feedback to others.

**Overview**
This module addresses the course outcomes on crafting a personal story and presenting its elements for peer review. A **beat** is one unit of change in a story; a **beat board** shows each beat as a small image with one line of text. An **A/V script** puts the audio (narration, interview clips, sound) in the left column and the picture (images, shots, on-screen type) in the right column, so the words and the pictures are written together. This week has two deadlines: the Beat Board Checkpoint on Thursday at noon and M5 on Sunday at noon.

**Watch**
- Lecture 9.1, Shaping the Story. Segment 1: the dramatic question and the turn. Segment 2: structures, including kishōtenketsu, as beat boards. Segment 3: scene, summary and what the picture can carry. Segment 4: writing for the ear in A/V format.
- Demonstration: the paper edit by search; building a beat board; three alternate orders with Gemini.

**Read/View**
- Pixar in a Box, [Storytelling unit](https://www.khanacademy.org/computing/pixar/storytelling), “Story Structure” lesson.
- [*Digital Storytelling Cookbook*](https://www.storycenter.org/s/cookbook.pdf), Chapter 3, “Approaches to Scripting,” pages 25 to 30.
- [Ira Glass on Storytelling](https://www.openculture.com/2009/10/ira_glass_on_the_art_of_story_telling.html), Part 3 (the gap between taste and skill).
- Model A/V script: the instructor’s Week 8 micro-story in A/V format [instructor to create].

**Practice**
*Beat Board Checkpoint*
*Deadlines:* Post for critique: Wednesday of Week 9 at noon, Discord #beat-board · Due: Thursday of Week 9 at noon (Canvas: the beat board, paper edit list and your critique record) · Hard close: Friday of Week 9 at noon (10% deduction between the deadline and the hard close; nothing accepted after).
1. **Paper edit:** ask your Teller to list every passage about your story’s central moment, with timestamps. Listen to each one and choose what you will use. Optional: generate an Audio or Video Overview of your notebook as a study aid before you choose; it is never used in the film or shared.
2. Write your dramatic question in one sentence.
3. Build five to eight beat cards in the template, each with a thumbnail (a quick sketch, an archive image, a Project 1 style frame or an AI study) and one line of text.
4. Ask Gemini for three alternate orders of your beats; choose one and explain why in two or three sentences.
5. Post the dramatic question, the beat board and your reason. Comment on at least three classmates’ boards by Thursday at noon, with one comment on where the turn falls.

**Assessment**
*M5 A/V Script Draft*
*Deadlines:* Post for critique (this is your D7 post): Saturday of Week 9 at noon, Discord #d7-script-workshop · Due: Sunday of Week 9 at noon (Canvas: the script with version history link, the revised beat board and your critique record) · Hard close: Monday of Week 10 at noon (10% deduction between the deadline and the hard close; nothing accepted after).
1. Revise your beat board using checkpoint comments and your Project 1 feedback.
2. Write a narration script of 250 to 375 words for a 2 to 3 minute story (up to about 450 for a 4-minute story with interview clips; clip time counts toward running time, not word count) in the A/V template, in a Google Doc so version history is kept. Gemini in Docs, if your account has it, may suggest cuts and tighter lines; you decide every change. Quote your narrator at least twice, with timestamps; quoted words are never edited by AI.
3. In the picture column, name the image or shot for every line, and mark where on-screen type, interview audio and sounds will appear.
4. Post your revised beat board and draft in D7, and share the version history link with the instructor in Canvas.
5. Add a row to your AI-use log and submit the Canvas record.

**Discussion · D7 Script Workshop**
*Prompt:* Your post is your M5 beat board and A/V draft. In your comments, review classmates’ drafts using the critique protocol, with one comment on the moment or turn and one on how the words and pictures work together (point to a line).
*Deadlines:* the same as M5 (post Saturday of Week 9 at noon; due Sunday at noon; hard close Monday of Week 10 at noon).
*Your post:* this is M5.
*Critique:* comments on at least three classmates’ drafts by Sunday at noon.
*Instructor role:* comments on at least one third of drafts in the channel; every student receives instructor feedback through M5 by Wednesday of Week 10 at noon.

### Week 10 · Editing and Finalizing the Story

> **Instructor notes (not student-facing)**
> **Competencies:** C6 (edit and finalize); C3; C9 (portfolio setup).
> **Recording plan:** Lecture 10.1, two segments of about 15 min: revision, editing and cutting for time; accuracy (a **fact-check table**: every factual claim matched to an archive item; family legend labeled as family legend) and performing narration (pace, breath, emphasis). Demo (about 30 min): read-back with text-to-speech; the Teller as fact-checker (“Where do the interview and the documents disagree?”); building the treatment section of the case-study page from the Adobe Portfolio template (Google Sites as backup), with the layout designed in InDesign or Adobe Express: logline, synopsis, a palette pulled from the style frames, a type pairing, references and a sound approach. Studio Session (30 min, live, recorded, required to watch): narration clinic, including students who are nervous about recording their own voice.
> **RSI cadence:** Monday-at-noon announcement. **M5 feedback by Wednesday at noon**, before M6. P6 feedback within 3 days of the Friday hard close, with one note on the look and one on the writing. **M6 feedback by Wednesday of Week 11 at noon.**
> **Seat time:** Direct 2.5 (lecture 0.5, demo 0.5, Studio Session 0.5, critique for P6 and M6 1.0). Outside 5.5 (StudioBinder treatment article and Art of the Title 0.5; P6 2.0; M6 3.0: revise 1.25, fact check 0.75, read-back and cut 0.25, scratch narration 0.5, revision note and log 0.25).
> **Common pitfalls:** scripts over length; narration read too fast; facts the narrator never said; treatments that retell the whole story instead of selling the look and the moment; more than two typefaces.

[GCC red title box: Week 10 · Editing and Finalizing the Story]

**Objectives**
By the end of this module, you will be able to:
1. Write and design a visual treatment that communicates the look and intent of your story.
2. Revise a script in response to peer and instructor feedback.
3. Verify the facts in your script against your archive.
4. Record a scratch narration at final pace.

**Overview**
This module addresses the course outcome on editing and finalizing the story before it becomes a digital story, and closes the unit on crafting a personal story. You will also set up your portfolio case-study page, starting with a **treatment**: a short, designed document that tells a producer or client what the piece is, what it will look and sound like, and why. This week has two deadlines: P6 on Thursday at noon and M6 on Sunday at noon. Your M5 feedback arrives Wednesday at noon.

**Watch**
- Lecture 10.1, Editing the Story. Segment 1: revision, editing and cutting for time. Segment 2: accuracy, the fact-check table and performing narration.
- Demonstration: read-back; the Teller as fact-checker; building the treatment section of your case-study page.
- Studio Session: narration clinic.

**Read/View**
- StudioBinder, [“What Is a Film Treatment”](https://www.studiobinder.com/blog/what-is-a-film-treatment-definition/).
- Art of the Title, [title sequence archive](https://www.artofthetitle.com/titles/): watch two title sequences of your choice and note one type choice in each that sets the tone.
- [*Digital Storytelling Cookbook*](https://www.storycenter.org/s/cookbook.pdf), Step 6, “Assembling Your Story” (skim).

**Practice**
*P6 Visual Treatment and Case-Study Page Setup*
*Deadlines:* Post for critique (the treatment section): Wednesday of Week 10 at noon, Discord #p6-treatment · Due: Thursday of Week 10 at noon (Canvas: the page link plus your critique record) · Hard close: Friday of Week 10 at noon (10% deduction between the deadline and the hard close; nothing accepted after).
1. Copy the case-study page template [instructor to create] in Adobe Portfolio, which is part of your Creative Cloud account (Google Sites is the backup if Adobe Portfolio is not available to you). Design the treatment layout in InDesign or Adobe Express.
2. Set the page to unlisted unless your release allows public sharing.
3. Build the treatment section: your logline; a synopsis of 60 to 80 words; a look-and-feel panel with a palette of four to six colors taken from your style frames, a type pairing (no more than two typefaces) and four to six reference images including your Project 1 style frames; and two or three sentences on your sound approach (narration, narrator clips, ambient sound, music).
4. Add the Project 1 section: your micro-story video, a 60 to 90 second screen recording of your Teller, and a caption and alt text for each image.
5. Post a screenshot of the treatment section. Comment on at least three classmates’ treatments by Thursday at noon, with one comment on the look and one on the writing.
6. Submit the Canvas record with the page link.

**Assessment**
*M6 Final Script, Fact Check and Scratch Narration*
*Deadlines:* Post for critique (the script and scratch narration): Saturday of Week 10 at noon, Discord #m6-final-script · Due: Sunday of Week 10 at noon (Canvas: everything below plus your critique record) · Hard close: Monday of Week 11 at noon (10% deduction between the deadline and the hard close; nothing accepted after).
1. Revise your A/V script using workshop comments and your M5 feedback. Keep it within length.
2. Ask your Teller where the interview and the documents disagree. If you need background on the place or period, you may run a Gemini Deep Research report and use it only as leads, checking each claim against a cited source and noting it in your log. Complete the fact-check table: each factual claim in the script, the archive item that supports it, and how you will handle anything uncertain (on screen or in narration).
3. Listen to the script with text-to-speech read-back and cut anything that drags.
4. Record a scratch narration (a quick recording used for timing) at final pace and note the running time.
5. Write a three-sentence revision note on your most important changes.
6. Post the script and scratch narration (as your release allows). Comment on at least three classmates’ posts by Sunday at noon, with one comment on pace.
7. Submit the script (with version history link), the fact-check table, the scratch narration and the note. Add a row to your AI-use log.

**Discussion**
No separate discussion this week. Critique happens in the P6 and M6 channels.

### Week 11 · Storyboarding and the Animatic

> **Instructor notes (not student-facing)**
> **Competencies:** C7 (storyboard with design principles); C5.
> **Recording plan:** Lecture 11.1, four segments: storyboard methods by form (panels for animation and motion comic; photo boards for documentary; shot-by-shot boards and shot lists for live action); shot types and motion for still images (pan, zoom, **parallax**: layers moving at different speeds to suggest depth); design principles on screen (hierarchy, contrast, alignment, rule of thirds, color continuity from the style frames, type on screen, title and action safe areas); the **animatic** (still frames timed to scratch narration to test pacing). Demo, four segments: hand thumbnails; the annotated storyboard template (Photoshop or InDesign; Google Slides on the backup path); AI frame alternatives with Gemini image generation (Firefly if the account has credits); building an animatic in Premiere Pro, with Adobe Express or Google Vids on the backup path.
> **RSI cadence:** Monday-at-noon announcement stating: “Your thumbnails and rough board are due Thursday at noon; your storyboard and animatic are due Sunday at noon. Post each the day before.” Instructor comments in the Storyboard channel before Friday at noon and reviews storyboards in D8. **M6 feedback Wednesday at noon. M7 feedback by Wednesday of Week 12 at noon.**
> **Seat time:** Direct 3.0 (lecture 1.0, demo 1.0, critique for the Storyboard Checkpoint and M7 1.0). Outside 5.0 (*Cookbook* Ch. 4, StudioBinder storyboard and shot list 0.5; Storyboard Checkpoint 1.5: thumbnails 0.5, AI alternatives 0.25, rough board 0.75; M7 3.0: annotations and finish 1.0, shot list 0.25, animatic 1.5, log 0.25).
> **Common pitfalls:** illustrating every noun literally; annotations that say “cool shot”; animators overbuilding (the animatic is still frames on a timeline); live-action students without a shot list. Photo-based storyboards stay near the *Cookbook*’s “fewer than twenty images”; animation may use more.

[GCC red title box: Week 11 · Storyboarding and the Animatic]

**Objectives**
By the end of this module, you will be able to:
1. Apply basic design principles to compose frames for a digital story.
2. Produce an annotated storyboard that maps narration, image, sound and timing, and a shot list for anything still to be made.
3. Build an animatic that tests your story’s pacing.

**Overview**
This module begins the unit on creating the digital story and addresses the course outcome on using design principles to lay out and produce a storyboard. Storyboard **annotations** are the short, exact notes under each frame that tell a whole crew what happens: the shot, the action, the line, the sound and the duration. A **shot list** lists every image or clip you still need to make, with how you will make it. This week has two deadlines: the Storyboard Checkpoint on Thursday at noon and M7 on Sunday at noon.

**Watch**
- Lecture 11.1, Storyboarding. Segment 1: methods by form. Segment 2: shots and motion for still images. Segment 3: design principles on screen. Segment 4: the animatic.
- Demonstration: thumbnails; annotated storyboard templates; AI frame alternatives; animatic assembly in Premiere Pro (and on the backup path).

**Read/View**
- [*Digital Storytelling Cookbook*](https://www.storycenter.org/s/cookbook.pdf), Chapter 4, “Storyboarding,” pages 31 to 35.
- StudioBinder, [“What is a Storyboard?”](https://www.studiobinder.com/blog/what-is-a-storyboard/) and [“What Is a Shot List”](https://www.studiobinder.com/blog/what-is-a-shot-list/).
- Pixar in a Box, [Storytelling unit](https://www.khanacademy.org/computing/pixar/storytelling), “Storyboarding” lesson (optional; recommended for Form A).
- Optional: Russell Sharman, [*Moving Pictures*](https://uark.pressbooks.pub/movingpictures/), “Mise-en-scène” and “Cinematography” (CC BY-NC-SA 4.0).
- Optional: [*Graphic Design and Print Production Fundamentals*](https://ecampusontario.pressbooks.pub/graphicdesign/) (CC BY 4.0), the chapter on design elements, principles and compositional techniques [chapter to confirm].
- Storyboard templates: Photoshop, InDesign, printable PDF, and Google Slides for the backup path [instructor to create].

**Practice**
*Storyboard Checkpoint*
*Deadlines:* Post for critique: Wednesday of Week 11 at noon, Discord #storyboard · Due: Thursday of Week 11 at noon (Canvas: the items below plus your critique record) · Hard close: Friday of Week 11 at noon (10% deduction between the deadline and the hard close; nothing accepted after).
1. In 30 minutes, draw 24 small thumbnails for your story by hand or on a tablet.
2. Choose three frames and generate AI alternatives (no real, identifiable people). Note in one sentence each which version serves the story better and why.
3. Lay out a rough board of 12 to 20 frames (animation may use more), carrying the look of your style frames.
4. Post the thumbnails, the three comparisons and the rough board. Comment on at least three classmates’ boards by Thursday at noon, with one comment on composition (name the frame number).

**Assessment**
*M7 Storyboard, Shot List and Animatic*
*Deadlines:* Post for critique (this is your D8 post): Saturday of Week 11 at noon, Discord #d8-storyboard-critique · Due: Sunday of Week 11 at noon (Canvas: storyboard PDF, shot list, animatic and your critique record) · Hard close: Monday of Week 12 at noon (10% deduction between the deadline and the hard close; nothing accepted after).
1. Finish the storyboard using checkpoint comments and your M6 feedback. Annotate every frame in the template format: shot or image type, action or motion, narration line, sound, duration, and image source (archive, your own work, or AI-assisted). Most frames come from the archive or your own work.
2. Write a shot list for every image or clip you still need to draw, photograph or film, with where, when and how you will make it (required for live-action and archival video; as needed for other forms). The shot list template is a Google Sheet; Gemini in Sheets, if your account has it, may help organize it.
3. Build an animatic with your scratch narration in your editing tool.
4. Post the storyboard (PDF), shot list and animatic (MP4 or unlisted link) in D8.
5. Add a row to your AI-use log and submit the Canvas record.

**Discussion · D8 Storyboard Critique**
*Prompt:* Your post is your M7 storyboard, shot list and animatic. In your comments, review classmates’ work using the critique protocol: one comment on composition (name the frame number) and one on timing (name the timecode).
*Deadlines:* the same as M7 (post Saturday of Week 11 at noon; due Sunday at noon; hard close Monday of Week 12 at noon).
*Your post:* this is M7.
*Critique:* comments on at least three classmates’ posts by Sunday at noon.
*Instructor role:* reviews storyboards in the channel; Monday summary on design principles across the class.

### Week 12 · Images, Key Art, Assembly and the Archive Package

> **Instructor notes (not student-facing)**
> **Competencies:** C8; C3; C10.
> **Recording plan:** Lecture 12.1, three segments of about 15 min: resolution, 16:9 and color for video, and restoring versus inventing (Content-Aware Fill and the Spot Healing Brush, which use no generative credits; Generative Fill and Expand where the account has credits; every change labeled); licensing: owned, licensed or released media is the rule; fair use is a narrow exception not to rely on for a portfolio piece; Creative Commons, public-domain archival film (Prelinger, Library of Congress) and generative AI; **key art** (the single image that sells a film: hierarchy, title treatment, tagline, credit block) and the archive package (preservation copies, **3-2-1 backup**: three copies, on two kinds of storage, one kept somewhere else). Demo (about 30 min): preparing and labeling images in Photoshop; layer separation for parallax with Select Subject and Content-Aware Fill (Generative Fill if credits allow); building key art and a title card from the template (2:3 and 4:5 with safe areas); dropping narration and images into the editing template as an **assembly edit** (everything in order, no polish). Studio Session (45 min, live, recorded, required to watch): editing setup for each tool path.
> **RSI cadence:** Monday-at-noon announcement. Instructor comments on every key art post before Friday at noon. **M7 feedback Wednesday at noon. M8 feedback by Wednesday of Week 13 at noon**, including a licensing check.
> **Seat time:** Direct 2.5 (lecture 0.75, demo 0.5, Studio Session 0.75, critique for the Key Art Checkpoint 0.5). Outside 5.5 (readings 0.5; Key Art Checkpoint 1.0; M8 4.0: prepare images 1.25, key art revision 0.25, asset log 0.5, assembly edit 1.25, archive package and backup 0.5, log 0.25).
> **Common pitfalls:** AI “restoration” that changes a face; images taken from search results; archival film used without checking its rights statement; key art with five typefaces or a tagline that summarizes the plot. **At-risk week** for first-time editors; the template carries the structure, and key art is built from existing assets.

[GCC red title box: Week 12 · Images, Key Art, Assembly and the Archive Package]

**Objectives**
By the end of this module, you will be able to:
1. Design key art and a title card with a clear type hierarchy and a tagline.
2. Prepare images for video at the correct size and shape.
3. Distinguish restoring from inventing when using AI image tools, and label each change.
4. Assemble your story in order on an editing timeline and preserve your archive.

**Overview**
This module addresses the course outcomes on using tools to manipulate graphics, on language, image and power, and on archiving the story. **Key art** is the single designed image that represents a film on a poster, a streaming tile or a portfolio page. A **tagline** is the short line on it that makes someone want to watch. This week has two deadlines: the Key Art Checkpoint on Thursday at noon and M8 on Sunday at noon (M8 has no critique).

**Watch**
- Lecture 12.1, Images, Key Art and the Archive. Segment 1: size, shape and color for video; restoring versus inventing. Segment 2: licensing and archival sources. Segment 3: key art, and the archive package and backup.
- Demonstration: preparing and labeling images; layers for motion; key art and the title card; the assembly edit.
- Studio Session: editing setup.

**Read/View**
- Creative Commons, [“About CC Licenses”](https://creativecommons.org/share-your-work/cclicenses/) and [“Understanding CC Licenses and Generative AI”](https://creativecommons.org/2023/08/18/understanding-cc-licenses-and-generative-ai/).
- Archival Producers Alliance, [GenAI Guidelines](https://www.archivalproducersalliance.com/genai-guidelines), the transparency section.
- Reference for archival images and film (use as needed; check each item’s rights statement): [Prelinger Archives](https://archive.org/details/prelinger) on the Internet Archive, Library of Congress [Free to Use and Reuse](https://www.loc.gov/free-to-use/), and [Wikimedia Commons](https://commons.wikimedia.org/).
- Optional: NPPA, [“Code of Ethics for Visual Journalists”](https://nppa.org/resources/code-ethics).

**Practice**
*Key Art Checkpoint*
*Deadlines:* Post for critique: Wednesday of Week 12 at noon, Discord #key-art · Due: Thursday of Week 12 at noon (Canvas: the key art and title card PNGs plus your critique record) · Hard close: Friday of Week 12 at noon (10% deduction between the deadline and the hard close; nothing accepted after).
1. Design key art (2:3 or 4:5) from the Illustrator, Photoshop or InDesign template, built from your style frames and archive images: the title, a tagline of 10 words or fewer, and a credit block (“A film by…,” narrator credit as the release allows, music and image credits to come).
2. Design a matching title card (16:9) for the opening of your film.
3. Check type contrast with the [WebAIM Contrast Checker](https://webaim.org/resources/contrastchecker/).
4. Post both. Comment on at least three classmates’ key art by Thursday at noon, with one comment on type hierarchy and one on the tagline.

**Assessment**
*M8 Asset Pack, Assembly Edit and Archive Package*
*Deadlines:* No critique (private archive material) · Due: Sunday of Week 12 at noon (Canvas) · Hard close: Monday of Week 13 at noon (10% deduction between the deadline and the hard close; nothing accepted after).
1. Prepare every image in your storyboard for 1920 x 1080 (or 1080 x 1920 if your story is vertical and you have a reason).
2. Use an AI-assisted Photoshop tool on at least one image (for example, removing scratches, extending a frame to 16:9, or separating layers for parallax): Generative Fill or Expand if your account has generative credits; otherwise Content-Aware Fill, the Spot Healing Brush or Select Subject, or Gemini image editing. In the asset log, describe each change as restoring or inventing. Any generated image that could pass for a photograph is styled as illustration or captioned on screen.
3. Revise your key art and title card using checkpoint comments.
4. Complete the asset log: asset, source, creator, license or release, AI involvement, Content Credentials.
5. Using the editing template, place your scratch narration, interview clips and images on the timeline in storyboard order.
6. Archive package: save preservation copies (WAV for audio, TIFF or PNG for images, PDF or plain text for documents), keep your editing project and original media, and complete a 3-2-1 backup.
7. Submit the asset folder link, the asset log, the revised key art and title card, a screenshot of the assembled timeline, and a screenshot of your backup locations. Add a row to your AI-use log.

**Discussion**
No separate discussion this week. Critique happens in the Key Art channel.

### Week 13 · Motion, Sound and Timing: The Rough Cut

> **Instructor notes (not student-facing)**
> **Competencies:** C8; C9 (edit and produce); C5.
> **Recording plan:** Lecture 13.1, four segments: pacing and rhythm (the cut follows the breath; holds that let an image land); motion by form (parallax and limited animation; camera moves on stills; cutting live action and archival film, including continuity and **J and L cuts**: sound that starts before or continues after its picture); sound design (music is optional) and loudness targets: final mix about -16 to -14 **LUFS** integrated (a measure of perceived loudness) for web delivery, true peak no higher than -1 dBTP, music, if used, starting 15 to 20 dB under the voice and adjusted by ear (confirm with GCC audio faculty); captions from the start. Demo, four segments: motion and parallax in After Effects and Premiere Pro; sound effects and an optional music bed in Audition and Premiere Pro (licensed libraries, Firefly sound generation if the account has credits, Remix to fit a track to length); Enhance Speech and loudness metering; auto-captions; writing the narrator review email from the template.
> **RSI cadence:** Monday-at-noon announcement stating: “Your motion test is due Thursday at noon; your rough cut is due to your classmates on Saturday at noon and to your narrator by Sunday at noon.” Instructor comments on every motion test before Friday at noon. **M8 feedback Wednesday at noon.** Instructor screens every rough cut and records video feedback by **Wednesday of Week 14 at noon**.
> **Seat time:** Direct 3.0 (lecture 1.0, demo 1.0, critique for the Motion Test Checkpoint and M9 1.0). Outside 5.0 (Motion Test Checkpoint 1.5; M9 3.5: remaining motion 1.25, sound design 0.25, timing pass 1.0, captions, export, post and narrator email 0.75, log 0.25).
> **Common pitfalls:** music, where used, too loud; polishing transitions before the structure works; every still drifting at the same speed; missing media after moving files. **At-risk week.** Rough cut is graded on structure, picture timing and mix, not polish.

[GCC red title box: Week 13 · Motion, Sound and Timing: The Rough Cut]

**Objectives**
By the end of this module, you will be able to:
1. Edit narration, images, motion and sound into a timed sequence.
2. Mix narration, interview audio, room tone and sound design (and music, if you use it) at appropriate levels.
3. Present a rough cut for peer review and request your narrator’s review in a professional email.

**Overview**
This module addresses the course outcomes on using tools to manipulate graphics, video and sound, and on editing and producing a short digital story. Picture leads; sound supports it. Music is optional. A **music bed** is music placed under narration at a low level to set tone; competency on sound is met by narration, interview audio, room tone and sound design. This week has two deadlines: the Motion Test Checkpoint on Thursday at noon and M9 on Sunday at noon.

**Watch**
- Lecture 13.1, Editing for Time. Segment 1: pacing and rhythm. Segment 2: motion and cutting by form. Segment 3: sound design, optional music, and loudness. Segment 4: captions from the start.
- Demonstration: motion; sound effects and, optionally, a music bed; cleaning and measuring audio; auto-captions; the narrator review email.

**Read/View**
- Optional music and sound sources (music is not required): [Free Music Archive](https://freemusicarchive.org/), [ccMixter](https://ccmixter.org/) and [Incompetech](https://incompetech.com/music/royalty-free/) (attribution required). [YouTube Audio Library](https://www.youtube.com/audiolibrary) is inside YouTube Studio (signed in). Record every track’s license in your asset log, and log any AI-generated music in your AI-use log.
- Optional: Russell Sharman, [*Moving Pictures*](https://uark.pressbooks.pub/movingpictures/), “Editing” and “Sound” (CC BY-NC-SA 4.0); [Transom](https://transom.org/), a public-media site with free guides to recording, mixing and sound design.

**Practice**
*Motion Test Checkpoint*
*Deadlines:* Post for critique: Wednesday of Week 13 at noon, Discord #motion-test · Due: Thursday of Week 13 at noon (Canvas: the clip plus your critique record) · Hard close: Friday of Week 13 at noon (10% deduction between the deadline and the hard close; nothing accepted after).
1. Starting from your assembly edit, finish the first 20 to 30 seconds of your story: motion (camera moves, parallax, limited animation or cut footage, as your form requires), room tone, ambient sound and sound design (and a music bed only if you choose one).
2. Mix that section to the loudness targets if your editor can measure them; otherwise check on both headphones and a phone speaker.
3. Post the clip (as your release allows). Comment on at least three classmates’ clips by Thursday at noon, with one comment on motion and one on the mix (name timecodes).

**Assessment**
*M9 Rough Cut*
*Deadlines:* Post for critique (this is your D9 post): Saturday of Week 13 at noon, Discord #d9-rough-cut · Due: Sunday of Week 13 at noon (Canvas: the rough cut link, a copy of your narrator email and your critique record) · Hard close: Monday of Week 14 at noon (10% deduction between the deadline and the hard close; nothing accepted after). Send the rough cut to your narrator by Sunday at noon.
1. Apply the motion-test comments, then carry motion and sound through the rest of the story.
2. Adjust timing so cuts and moves follow the narration. Keep the story within 2 to 4 minutes.
3. Generate draft captions.
4. Export (720p or higher) and post the rough cut in D9 as an unlisted link or upload, only if your release allows class sharing (otherwise submit privately and answer the alternate prompt).
5. Send the rough cut to your narrator with the narrator review form [instructor to create] and a short review email from the template (100 to 150 words): what you are sending, what kind of feedback you need, and a response date of Wednesday of Week 14.
6. Add a row to your AI-use log and submit the Canvas record.

**Discussion · D9 Rough Cut Screening**
*Prompt:* Your post is your M9 rough cut. In your comments, review classmates’ rough cuts using the critique protocol: one comment on picture and timing, one on the sound mix, and whether the turn lands (name timecodes). *Alternate prompt (if your release does not allow class sharing):* post an annotated before-and-after frame pair from your edit with a 50 to 80 word note on the hardest editing decision you made, and review classmates’ cuts.
*Deadlines:* the same as M9 (post Saturday of Week 13 at noon; due Sunday at noon; hard close Monday of Week 14 at noon).
*Your post:* this is M9.
*Critique:* comments on at least three classmates’ rough cuts by Sunday at noon.
*Instructor role:* screens every rough cut and records individual video feedback by Wednesday of Week 14 at noon.

### Week 14 · Finishing: Titles, Color and Accessibility

> **Instructor notes (not student-facing)**
> **Competencies:** C9; C6; C3.
> **Recording plan:** Lecture 14.1, two segments of about 15 min: finishing (a basic color pass for consistency across sources; title sequence and end credits: type choice, hierarchy, timing, contrast and safe areas; credits with AI disclosure following the Archival Producers Alliance and the MLA guidance); accessibility: captions for prerecorded video are WCAG Level A; Level AA also asks for description of visual information the soundtrack does not carry, so key visuals are spoken in the narration or included in a descriptive transcript; on-screen type needs sufficient contrast; and the narrator review: honoring requests unless a change would make the story false (talk with the instructor). Demo (about 30 min): color pass with Lumetri Color and titles with the Essential Graphics panel in Premiere Pro (Adobe Express on the backup path); caption correction and export; writing a descriptive transcript; adding the final story and key art to the case-study page. Studio Session (30 min, live, recorded, required to watch): finishing clinic.
> **RSI cadence:** Monday-at-noon announcement. Instructor comments on every titles-and-captions post before Friday at noon. **Rough cut video feedback Wednesday at noon**; students review it (direct time). **M10 feedback by Wednesday of Week 15 at noon.**
> **Seat time:** Direct 2.5 (lecture 0.5, demo 0.5, feedback review 0.5, Studio Session 0.5, critique for the Titles and Captions Checkpoint 0.5). Outside 5.5 (W3C description page and WebAIM contrast checker 0.5; Titles and Captions Checkpoint 1.25: title sequence 0.5, caption correction 0.75; M10 3.0: apply changes and color pass 1.5, description 0.5, export and upload 0.5, narrator review record 0.25, log 0.25; case-study page 0.75).
> **Common pitfalls:** uncorrected captions (names especially); credits without AI disclosure; thin white type over busy images; narrators not responding (if no response by Wednesday, the student follows up once and documents it; the release stands).

[GCC red title box: Week 14 · Finishing: Titles, Color and Accessibility]

**Objectives**
By the end of this module, you will be able to:
1. Design a title sequence and end credits that are legible and credit sources, including AI use.
2. Finish and export a captioned digital story, with a consistent color pass, that meets accessibility requirements.
3. Incorporate your narrator’s review into the final cut.

**Overview**
This module addresses the course outcomes on editing and producing the digital story and on finalizing the story, and returns to language, image and power through credit and disclosure. A **color pass** adjusts images from different sources so they look like one film. This week has two deadlines: the Titles and Captions Checkpoint on Thursday at noon and M10 on Sunday at noon (M10 has no critique). Your rough cut feedback and your narrator’s response are due Wednesday.

**Watch**
- Lecture 14.1, Finishing. Segment 1: color, titles and credits, including AI disclosure. Segment 2: captions, description of visual information, contrast and the narrator review.
- Demonstration: color pass and titles; captions; the descriptive transcript; export; the case-study page.
- Studio Session: finishing clinic.
- Your instructor’s rough cut feedback video (Wednesday).

**Read/View**
- W3C Web Accessibility Initiative, [“Captions/Subtitles”](https://www.w3.org/WAI/media/av/captions/) and [“Description of Visual Information”](https://www.w3.org/WAI/media/av/description/).
- WebAIM, [Contrast Checker](https://webaim.org/resources/contrastchecker/) (use it on your title colors).
- Optional: Matthew Butterick, [*Practical Typography*](https://practicaltypography.com/) (free to read online), the sections on type size, line length and font choice; Art of the Title, [title sequence archive](https://www.artofthetitle.com/titles/).
- MLA Style Center, [“How do I cite generative AI in MLA style?”](https://style.mla.org/citing-generative-ai/) (for the credit line).

**Practice**
*Titles and Captions Checkpoint*
*Deadlines:* Post for critique: Wednesday of Week 14 at noon, Discord #titles-and-captions · Due: Thursday of Week 14 at noon (Canvas: the clip plus your critique record) · Hard close: Friday of Week 14 at noon (10% deduction between the deadline and the hard close; nothing accepted after).
1. Finish your title sequence (from the Week 12 title card). Check title contrast and keep type inside safe areas.
2. Correct captions for accuracy, names and timing on a 30-second section that includes on-screen type.
3. Post the title sequence and the captioned section. Comment on at least three classmates’ posts by Thursday at noon, with one comment on type and one on caption readability.

**Assessment**
*M10 Final Cut and Narrator Review*
*Deadlines:* No critique (the showcase is D10) · Due: Sunday of Week 14 at noon (Canvas) · Hard close: Monday of Week 15 at noon (10% deduction between the deadline and the hard close; nothing accepted after).
1. Record your narrator’s response on the review form (requested changes, or that they approved). If you have had no response by Wednesday, follow up once and note it.
2. Apply instructor, peer and narrator feedback. If a requested change would make the story untrue, talk with the instructor.
3. Do a color pass so images from different sources sit together.
4. Add end credits, including your AI credit line and music and image credits.
5. Correct the rest of the captions.
6. Make sure key visual information (who is in a photograph, a date, on-screen text) is spoken in the narration or included in a descriptive transcript.
7. Export an MP4 viewing master and upload an unlisted copy. Keep your editing project and original media in the archive. Add the final story and key art to your case-study page.
8. Submit the unlisted link, the caption file, the descriptive transcript (if used) and the narrator review form. Add a row to your AI-use log.

**Discussion**
No separate discussion this week. Critique happens in the Titles and Captions channel.

### Week 15 · The Living Archive Showcase

> **Instructor notes (not student-facing)**
> **Competencies:** C11 (present for peer review); C10; C9.
> **Recording plan:** Lecture 15.1, two segments of about 15 min: introducing your work to an audience (the maker’s statement: what it is, why it matters, one decision); the case study for employers (layout, image-to-text ratio, captions that explain decisions, alt text, short copy). Closing video (about 30 min): highlights from the showcase and next steps with the Living Archive (keeping the archive, retiring the Teller as the release says). Students review M10 feedback (direct time).
> **RSI cadence:** Monday-at-noon announcement. **M10 feedback Wednesday at noon**, before the Final. Instructor comments on every showcase post before Friday at noon. Final Project due Wednesday of finals week at noon, hard close Thursday of finals week at noon; graded during the final exam period. Week 15 is the last instructional week.
> **Seat time:** Direct 3.0 (lecture 0.5, closing video 0.5, M10 feedback review 0.5, D10 critique of three full pieces and case-study pages 1.5). Outside 5.0 (D10 post 0.25; Final Project 4.5: Teller v2 and illustrated critique 1.25, walkthrough 0.75, case-study copy and layout 1.25, maker’s statement 0.25, AI-use statement 0.5, end-of-semester Teller plan 0.5; course survey 0.25).
> **Common pitfalls:** public pages beyond the release; live Teller links on portfolio pages (use the screen recording); case-study pages that are walls of text; maker’s statements that list tools instead of decisions.

[GCC red title box: Week 15 · The Living Archive Showcase]

**Objectives**
By the end of this module, you will be able to:
1. Present your digital story and its key art for peer review.
2. Publish a designed case study, with a maker’s statement, that documents your process and your decisions about AI.
3. Evaluate peers’ work with specific, constructive written critique.

**Overview**
This module addresses the course outcome on presenting the digital story for peer review and completes the outcomes on archiving and producing the story. Your Living Archive is your final project and your portfolio piece. A **maker’s statement** is a short piece of writing, in your own voice, that tells a viewer what the work is, why you made it, and one decision that shaped it. This week has one deadline: D10 on Thursday at noon. The Final Project is due Wednesday of finals week at noon.

**Watch**
- Lecture 15.1, Presenting Your Work. Segment 1: introducing a story and the maker’s statement. Segment 2: the case study for employers.
- Closing video.
- Your instructor’s M10 feedback (Wednesday).

**Read/View**
- The Final Project assignment page and rubric.
- Your classmates’ showcase posts.
- Model case studies [instructor to select, with permission].

**Practice**
*Course survey*
*Deadlines:* No critique · Due: Thursday of Week 15 at noon (Canvas survey) · Hard close: Friday of Week 15 at noon. The survey shares the D10 deadline time; it is not a separate deadline.

**Assessment**
*Final Project: The Living Archive*
*Deadlines:* No critique (the showcase is D10) · Due: Wednesday of finals week at noon (Canvas) · Hard close: Thursday of finals week at noon (10% deduction between the deadline and the hard close; nothing accepted after).
The full scenario, components and rubric are on the assignment page (Section 3.4). This week you complete:
1. **Teller v2:** load the final script and storyboard, revise the instructions using Project 1 feedback, ask 5 new questions, and write the Teller critique (150 to 250 words, illustrated with two annotated screenshots, or included in your walkthrough): two places where the Teller misrepresents your narrator or the story, and why.
2. **The walkthrough:** a 3 to 5 minute screen recording of your archive folder, your Teller test log and your edit timeline, explaining three decisions in your own voice (or book a 10-minute live conversation).
3. **The maker’s statement:** 100 to 150 words, written first, because it is also your D10 post.
4. **The case-study page:** add the key art, three storyboard frames beside the matching final frames, the Teller screen recording, the walkthrough, the maker’s statement and the AI-use statement. Keep the copy to about 300 words beyond the statements, with a caption and alt text on every image.
5. **The end-of-semester plan:** carry out release item 10 (delete the Teller, or record when it will be deleted or transferred) and send the archive copy to your narrator if the release says so.
6. **The full AI-use statement.**
Submit the case-study page link, the Teller v2 log and critique, the walkthrough, the AI-use statement and confirmation of the end-of-semester plan.

**Discussion · D10 Living Archive Showcase**
*Prompt:* Post your key art, your final story (as your release allows), a link to your case-study page (it may still be in progress) and your maker’s statement. Add one sentence on something your Teller can tell a visitor that the film does not. Then review classmates’ projects in writing using the critique protocol, with at least one comment on design (composition, type or color).
*Deadlines:* Post for critique: Wednesday of Week 15 at noon, Discord #d10-showcase · Due: Thursday of Week 15 at noon (Canvas record) · Hard close: Friday of Week 15 at noon (10% deduction; nothing accepted after).
*Your post:* key art, story, page link and maker’s statement.
*Critique:* reviews of at least three classmates’ projects by Thursday at noon. Use their comments to finish your case-study page before the Final Project deadline.
*Instructor role:* comments on every showcase post before Friday at noon; the closing video names highlights from across the class.

## 6. RSI and teaching-presence plan; Getting Started module

### 6.1 Regular and Substantive Interaction

| Interaction | Frequency | Substantive content | Where |
|---|---|---|---|
| Module announcement (captioned video or text) | Weekly, Monday at noon | Week’s focus; the week’s two deadlines in plain words (post time, deadline, hard close); connections to last week’s student work | Canvas Announcements |
| Critique facilitation | Every critiqued deadline (24) | Instructor comments in the Discord channel before the hard close; follow-up questions; a short summary after the close in discussion weeks | Discord (Canvas discussions as fallback) |
| Studio Sessions | Weeks 5, 7, 10, 12 and 14 | Live demonstration and Q&A; recorded and captioned; required to watch | Google Meet or Zoom; days and times TBD |
| Individual feedback on milestones | 10 times, on the schedule in 1.3 | Rubric-based, with one next step for the final | SpeedGrader, audio or video comments |
| Project feedback | Week 9 and finals week | Criterion-level; Project 1 notes carried into Teller v2 and the script | SpeedGrader |
| Mid-semester check-in | Week 8 | Individual progress note and plan | Canvas message or video |
| Rough cut video feedback | Wednesday of Week 14 | Screen-recorded notes on every rough cut | SpeedGrader |
| Outreach for missing work | After each deadline, before the hard close | Personal reminder that the hard close is at noon the next day | Canvas “Message students who” |
| Optional weekly time log | Weekly, 2 minutes | Workload calibration for the next offering | Anonymous survey |

### 6.2 Communication cadence (student-facing)

- The instructor responds to messages within 24 hours on weekdays and 48 hours on weekends.
- Feedback arrives on the schedule in the syllabus table, before the next item that depends on it.
- Each module opens on Monday at noon with an announcement.
- Course questions go in the Course Questions channel in Discord (or the Course Questions discussion in Canvas); personal questions go to Canvas Inbox, never to Discord.

### 6.3 Getting Started module (Week 1)

Each page opens with a GCC red title box.

1. **Welcome and Start Here:** welcome video; the Living Archive in brief; the three story forms; how pages are organized (GCC red title box, Objectives, Overview, then Watch, Read/View, Practice, Assessment, Discussion); the content note.
2. **How Deadlines Work** (student-facing text):

   > This course runs on two noon deadlines a week. Nothing is ever due at midnight.
   >
   > **The midweek cycle.** Post your work for critique in the assignment’s Discord channel by **Wednesday at noon**. Your work and your critique comments are due together in Canvas by **Thursday at noon**. The assignment closes **Friday at noon**.
   >
   > **The weekend cycle.** Post your work for critique by **Saturday at noon**. Your work and your critique comments are due together in Canvas by **Sunday at noon**. The assignment closes **Monday at noon**.
   >
   > **The Final Project** is due Wednesday of finals week at noon and closes Thursday of finals week at noon. It has no critique.
   >
   > **Week 1** has one deadline: post by Wednesday at noon, due Thursday at noon, closing Friday at noon. From Week 2 on, every week has one midweek and one weekend deadline, and never more than two.
   >
   > **The late window.** Work submitted after the deadline and before the hard close (24 hours) is accepted with a 10% deduction. Nothing is accepted after the hard close. If an emergency affects a deadline, contact the instructor before the hard close.
   >
   > **Posting late for critique.** Work posted for critique after the posting time may receive less peer feedback, because classmates comment on the work that is there when they sit down to critique. You still owe your own three comments by the deadline.
   >
   > **Your critique.** For every critiqued assignment, comment on at least three classmates’ posts by the deadline, using the critique protocol (I noticed, I wondered, What if). Each comment is 40 to 80 words and names the frame, timecode or line it is about.
   >
   > **Returning on the late-window day.** Your required critique is three comments by the deadline. Some classmates will post during the late window, after most feedback has been given. If you can, return on the late-window day (Monday for the weekend cycle, Friday for the midweek cycle) and leave a comment on any work posted late. This is not graded and earns no points; it is what a good member of an online creative community does. At some point in the semester you may be the one waiting for feedback, and it is worth more to a late project than the points it lost.
   >
   > **What goes in Canvas.** One submission per assignment: your work (file or link), a link or screenshot of your Discord post, the feedback you received (screenshot or short summary), and links or screenshots of your three comments. Use the critique record template. Some assignments have no critique because they contain private material (consent forms, releases, recordings, transcripts, Teller links, the final archive). Those are submitted in Canvas only and never posted to Discord.
   >
   > **Feedback from the instructor** on each milestone arrives before the next milestone is due. The schedule is in the syllabus.

   The page ends with the full calendar table (Section 1.2) and the feedback table (Section 1.3), with day names and noon only, never dates.
3. **Syllabus:** course outcomes in plain language; assignment groups and weights; grading scale; the deadline rhythm and the 10% late window; revision policy; accessibility and accommodations ([Disability Resources and Services](https://www.gccaz.edu/) [link to confirm]); academic integrity; AI-use policy; required text and how to get it (purchase, library e-book or reserve [CONFIRM]); technology requirements.
4. **Software and Recording Setup Guide:** the district pays for Adobe Creative Cloud and Google Gemini, so those are the tools for everyone. Online GCC students have Creative Cloud at home with their GCC account.

| | Default: Adobe Creative Cloud and Gemini (Windows or Mac) | Backup: Chromebook or phone |
|---|---|---|
| Record | Phone voice memo or Audition | Phone voice memo |
| Clean and mix audio | Audition; Premiere Pro Enhance Speech and Essential Sound | Adobe Podcast Enhance Speech (browser); Audacity only if needed |
| Write | Google Docs, Sheets, Slides and Gmail, with Gemini where your account has it; the Gemini app | Same |
| Moodboards, style frames, key art | Photoshop, Illustrator, InDesign; Gemini image generation; Firefly if you have generative credits | Adobe Express and Photoshop on the web (CONFIRM); Gemini image generation; Canva free tier or Photopea only if no Adobe tool runs |
| Drawing and animation | Photoshop, Fresco, After Effects | Adobe Express animation |
| Edit | Premiere Pro | Adobe Express; Premiere on a phone (CONFIRM); Google Vids; Clipchamp only if none of these runs |
| Captions | Premiere Pro | YouTube Studio (unlisted) |
| Teller | Gemini Notebook | Same |
| Critique | Discord (desktop or browser) | Discord (browser or phone app) |
| Case study | Adobe Portfolio | Google Sites |

   Also on this page: how to sign in to Creative Cloud with the GCC account and check for generative credits; loaner laptops and campus computer labs [links to confirm]; GCC library scanners; recording tips (a closet full of clothes or a parked car is a good booth; phone 6 to 12 inches from the speaker; airplane mode; a test recording first); photography tips (window light, plain background, three angles); the archive folder template and naming convention; joining the course Discord server and the Canvas discussion fallback; where to get help (GCC IT Help Desk, library, tutoring) [links to confirm].
5. **AI in This Course:** why AI is used at every stage, and why Gemini and Adobe are the defaults; the creative-collaborator uses list (4.1); what you author; the three-field log and the full statement, with a completed sample; the narrator menu; consent and privacy rules; the rule against generating images, video or voices of real people.
6. **Netiquette and Critique Norms:** the critique protocol; the late-window paragraph above, in the same words; care with stories that belong to real people; never posting private material to Discord; no sharing classmates’ materials outside the course.
7. **Glossary:** archive; narrator; Teller; Gemini Notebook; Gemini skills (optional); Gemini Live; Deep Research; Audio and Video Overviews; generative credits; Content-Aware Fill; grounding; guardrail; metadata; alt text; release; slate; room tone; b-roll; logline; pitch; moodboard; style frame; beat; beat board; A/V script; treatment; paper edit; dramatic question; the turn; kishōtenketsu; storyboard; annotation; shot list; thumbnail; animatic; parallax; motion comic; key art; tagline; title card; assembly edit; rough cut; J and L cuts; music bed; sound design; LUFS; color pass; captions; description of visual information; Content Credentials; 3-2-1 backup; maker’s statement; case study; unlisted; deadline; hard close; late window. One plain sentence each.
8. **Accessibility Statement:** all instructor videos are captioned with transcripts; student videos must be captioned; images posted for critique carry alt text; how to request accommodations.

### 6.4 Accessibility and UDL checklist

- Instructor videos captioned, with transcripts; segments under 15 minutes; guided lab with a written checklist and pause points.
- Canvas pages: real headings, descriptive link text, alt text on images, sufficient contrast including the GCC red title box.
- Discord: channel names that describe the assignment; pinned posts with the deadline block and the critique protocol; students post alt text with images; Canvas discussions available as an equal fallback.
- Posts accept a visual with writing, or audio or video with captions or a transcript.
- Choice of narrator, story subject, story form (animation or motion comic, illustrated or photo documentary, live-action or archival video, each with narration and sound), Teller voice (with the required consent), device path, and the materials of the visual journal.
- Templates for moodboard, frame breakdown, beat board, A/V script, storyboard, shot list, metadata sheet, asset log, key art, title and credits, consent documents, narrator emails, narrator review form, AI-use log, critique record, editing project and case-study page.
- Every Adobe generative step names a no-credit path (Gemini image generation or a non-generative Photoshop tool).
- Deadlines at noon with a 24-hour late window, published as day names, so students who work evenings or weekends can plan.

## 7. ILO recommendations

Attach GCC Institutional Learning Outcomes late in the course, on the Final Project only, one ILO per rubric criterion. **Michelle to confirm GCC’s current ILO names and codes.**

| Final Project criterion | Recommended ILO | Rationale |
|---|---|---|
| Story craft and script | TE Creative thinking | Shaping raw material into an original structure with a turn |
| Visual design: composition, typography and color | VC Visual communication | Design principles applied to meaning |
| Motion, editing and timing | TE Creative thinking | Timing and motion decisions that shape meaning |
| Sound and narration | OC Oral communication | Narrated delivery |
| Archive and Teller v2, including the critique | I Information literacy | Organizing, describing and evaluating sources and AI output |
| Rights, consent, narrator review and AI disclosure | I Information literacy | Licensing, attribution and disclosure |
| Accessibility | None | WCAG requirement, not an outcome measure |
| Case study and written communication | W Career and workforce readiness | Professional presentation and writing for employers (use a written communication ILO instead if GCC lists one) |
| Showcase and written peer critique | OC Oral communication | Presenting and critiquing work |

If GCC limits the number of ILOs per assignment, keep VC, I and W.

# AVC150 Multimedia Storytelling · Course Design, Version 6

**Course:** AVC150 Multimedia Storytelling, Glendale Community College, Maricopa County Community College District
**Format:** 3 credits, Lec+Lab, 6 periods / 6 load hours, occupational. 15 instructional weeks plus finals week, Canvas.
**Gen ed:** Arts and Humanities [AHM]; Humanities, Arts and Design [HU]
**Term dated in this version:** Spring 2027, from the MCCCD 2026 to 2027 academic calendar. Semester begins Saturday 16 January 2027. College closed Monday 18 January (Martin Luther King Jr. Day) and Monday 15 February (Presidents’ Day). Spring break Monday 15 to Sunday 21 March. Last day of classes Sunday 9 May. Finals Monday 10 to Friday 14 May. All times are noon, Arizona time.
**Prepared for:** Michelle Blomberg, course owner and human SME reviewer
**Builds on:** Version 5 (Section “AVC150 Multimedia Storytelling · Course Design, Version 5” in `canvas/avc150/AVC150.md`). Anything this file does not restate stands as written in Version 5, with the label, date and title-box changes in Section 7.4 applied.
**Status:** Draft for human review. Items marked [verify] need a human check before teaching; items marked [instructor to create] do not exist yet.

---

## 0. Version 6 change log

Version 6 applies a faculty review and brings the build into line with the course build standard (including its 24 October 2026 additions). Hours are per student. A negative number is time returned; a positive number is time added. Every week still totals 8.0 hours, and the course still totals 40 direct plus 80 outside, 120 hours.

| Change | What changed | Hours impact |
|---|---|---|
| AI statement for the course notes (faculty review) | A 54-word statement for the class schedule listing tells students before they enroll that the course requires AI tools and that interviewees consent to AI processing (Section 1) | 0 |
| Strong consent (faculty review) | The consent sequence now has three required parts: a **Consent Affirmation** signed before the interview (Week 5), a **video-recorded consent** the narrator reads on camera at the start of the interview (Week 6), and a **Signed Agreement** after the interview (Week 7) that replaces the Version 5 release. All three state plainly that the story material will be uploaded to and processed by large language models in Google Gemini and Adobe tools, what that means, and that the narrator can withdraw. The Project Information Sheet gains a full section on AI processing (Section 2) | +0.75 outside (information sheet walk-through 0.25 in S5, video consent 0.25 in S6, agreement signing session 0.25 in S7), funded by the cuts below |
| Mini project first (faculty review) | Weeks 1 to 3 are now a complete mini version of the whole project, run on a synthetic Practice Archive the instructor gives everyone: collect (select, describe and add one item), build a practice Teller, and make a 30 to 45 second story. Students see the end product before they ask a real narrator for anything. **Project 1 is now the mini, due Sunday of Week 3** (Sections 3, 4 and 7) | 0 net; Weeks 1 to 3 rebuilt inside 8.0 hours each |
| Synthetic Practice Archive (faculty review) | A pack built around a fictional narrator: profile, five transcript excerpts with a machine-draft transcript, five documents and letters, a list of public-domain period photographs from real collections, sample consent documents, a question bank and an instructor key. Gemini may write the fictional text; no AI-generated images anywhere in the pack (Section 3) | 0 for students; instructor build time listed in Section 3.9 |
| The real project starts in Week 4 | The real pitch moves from Week 3 to Week 4. Weeks 4 to 8 carry what Version 5 spread across Weeks 3 to 8 (pitch, consent, interview, archive, Teller). Weeks 9 to 15 keep the Version 5 sequence | see the compression table below |
| Version 5 Project 1 retired | The Week 8 Teller and Micro-Story project is gone. Its Teller half becomes process step S8 (Teller Build and Test); its style frames move into S9 (Beat Board and Style Frames, Week 9); the micro-story is cut, because the mini already taught the micro-story pipeline | −2.0 outside (micro-script 0.75, narration 0.5, edit and captions 0.75) |
| Gemini writes; it does not design (standard, 24 Oct) | In Weeks 1 to 9, AI image generation is removed from moodboards, journal pages, studies and style frames. Gemini still brainstorms, drafts emails and metadata, rehearses interviews, and powers the Teller. D4 now analyzes instructor-provided, labeled AI images instead of asking students to generate one. Weeks 10 to 15 items that still use generative images are listed in Section 7.4 for a decision | 0 (moodboard capped at 10 to 16 images; time unchanged) |
| Grading groups (standard) | Process Work: 20 steps, 10 points each. Projects: Project 1 (the mini) and the Final, 30 points each. Exercises: discussions, practices, setup items, 5 to 10 points each. Weights 40 / 35 / 25. Replaces the Version 5 groups (Practice 10, Discussions 15, Milestones 20, Project 1 20, Final 35). Critique comments are graded inside each step’s rubric instead of a separate critique-record group | 0 |

Start Here carries the full list and an alignment map (Section 5.6) | 0 |

Week 4 and Week 8 weekend cycles shift one day earlier (post Friday, due Saturday, close Sunday) so no late window or hard close lands on Presidents’ Day or spring break. Week 5 opens Tuesday 16 February. No deadline, late window or hard close falls on a closed day (checked in Section 4.4) | 0 (two shifted weekend cycles lose one working day each; see 4.5) |

| Delivery mode | The design summary no longer states a delivery mode. The course can run either way; Studio Sessions are scheduled live sessions, recorded | 0 |
| Narrator withdrawal fallback | If a narrator withdraws after Week 7, the student finishes the semester with the Practice Archive (or a self-interview) at no grade penalty | 0 |

## 1. Course-notes AI statement (class schedule listing)

> This course requires generative AI. Students use Google Gemini and Adobe Creative Cloud AI tools through their Maricopa accounts to transcribe, organize, research and search a story archive, and they build an AI “Teller” from interview material. Every interviewee gives signed and video-recorded consent before their material is uploaded. All AI use is disclosed.

(54 words. If the schedule system does not accept curly quotation marks, use straight ones in that field only.)

---

## 2. Consent package

### 2.1 What changed and why

The faculty review asked that a narrator know, in plain words and before anything is uploaded, that their story will be processed by large language models, and that consent be given three ways: an affirmation, a video recording, and a signed agreement. Version 6 keeps the Oral History Association sequence from Version 5 (ask, inform, record, release, review) and builds the three parts into it. The narrator sees the AI language four times: in the student’s first conversation, on the Project Information Sheet, on camera, and in the Signed Agreement.

The student first meets every document in Weeks 1 to 3 as a completed sample for the fictional Practice Archive narrator (Section 3.6), so no student sends a narrator a form they have never read.

### 2.2 Consent sequence

| When (Spring 2027) | Document or step | How it is given | Where it is kept |
|---|---|---|---|
| Weeks 1 to 3 | Students read the sample package for the Practice Archive narrator and practice explaining AI processing in D2 | Course work | Canvas |
| Week 4, before S4 (by Saturday 13 February) | **Informal ask.** The student explains the project, including the one-minute AI explanation (2.3), and asks whether the narrator is willing to hear more | Conversation | S4 notes, Canvas only |
| Week 5, S5 (after S4 feedback on Tuesday 16 February; submitted by Sunday 21 February) | **Project Information Sheet** (2.4) sent with the student’s project email and the OHA “For Participants” page; the student offers a 15-minute walk-through by phone, video call or in person. **Consent Affirmation** (2.5) signed | E-signature (Google Form in the student’s district account), or a photograph of a signed page; guardian co-signs for a narrator under 18 | 06_Consent folder; Canvas only |
| Week 6, at the start of the interview (window Tuesday 23 February to Wednesday 3 March) | **Video-recorded consent** (2.6): the narrator reads the consent script on camera, then the interview begins | Phone or computer camera; for an interview by video call, the narrator’s camera on the video call (recorded with permission) or a phone video the narrator sends | 06_Consent folder; never uploaded to any AI tool; Canvas only |
| Week 7, S7 (by Sunday 7 March) | **Signed Agreement** (2.7), signed after the narrator knows what they said | E-signature or signed page; guardian signs for a narrator under 18; family member or estate holder signs Part D for a story about someone who has died | 06_Consent folder; Canvas only |
| Week 13, S18 (rough cut sent by Sunday 25 April) | **Narrator review** of the rough cut, with a reminder that the narrator may still withdraw or restrict | Narrator watches; the student records the response on the review form | 06_Consent folder |
| Week 15 and finals week | **End-of-semester Teller plan** carried out (Agreement item 12) and confirmed to the narrator in writing | Email or message | 06_Consent folder |

**Upload rule (stated on every page that touches narrator material):** nothing the narrator says, writes or owns goes into any AI tool until the Signed Agreement is complete with item 4 marked yes. The video consent recording itself never goes into any AI tool. A student who is waiting transcribes the excerpt by hand, and may keep working on scanning, the shoot log and non-AI tasks.

**If the narrator says no to AI processing (item 4):** the student may still make the story with the narrator’s material using non-AI tools (hand transcription, manual editing, no speech-to-text, no Enhance Speech), and builds the Teller only from material the student owns (journal pages, the student’s own photographs, the student’s own notes about the story), or switches to a self-interview. Neither path is graded lower.

**If the narrator withdraws:** see 2.8. The student finishes with the Practice Archive or a self-interview, at no penalty.

### 2.3 The one-minute AI explanation (student script for the informal ask, Week 4)

Students say this in their own words; these are the points it must cover:

1. “For my class I’m making a short video story, and I’d like it to be your story about [subject].”
2. “I’d record a 20 to 30 minute interview, and you could share photos or papers if you want to.”
3. “The class uses AI tools from Google and Adobe. If you agree, your recording, words and photos would be uploaded to those tools so they can type up the interview, help me organize things, and let me build a searchable version of the archive that answers questions with your words.”
4. “Nothing is uploaded until you sign a form that explains it. You can say no to the AI part, or to anything, and you can change your mind later.”
5. “Would you be willing to look at the information sheet?”

### 2.4 Project Information Sheet (student-facing; the narrator keeps a copy)

> **Project Information Sheet · AVC150 Multimedia Storytelling, Glendale Community College**
>
> **Student:** [student name] · **Contact:** [student district email] · **Instructor:** [instructor name], [instructor district email] · **Course:** AVC150 Multimedia Storytelling, Spring 2027
>
> **What this project is.** I am a student in a Glendale Community College course on digital storytelling. This semester I am making a short digital story, 2 to 4 minutes long, about [subject]. I would like to interview you about it.
>
> **What I am asking you to do.** Take part in one recorded interview of 20 to 30 minutes, at a time and place you choose. If you wish, share photographs, letters, documents or objects for me to scan or photograph. Near the end of the semester, watch a rough cut of the story and tell me about anything you want changed. Taking part is voluntary. You do not have to answer any question, and you can stop at any time.
>
> **Before the interview** you will sign a Consent Affirmation. **At the start of the interview** I will ask you to read a short consent statement while I record video of you saying it. **After the interview** you will receive a Signed Agreement that lists, item by item, how your material may and may not be used. You can say yes or no to each item.
>
> **How AI tools will be used with your material. Please read this section closely.**
>
> This course uses artificial intelligence (AI) tools at every stage. If you agree in the Signed Agreement, your interview recording, the transcript of your words, and any photographs and documents you share will be **uploaded to and processed by large language models**. A large language model is a computer program that has learned patterns from very large amounts of text and images, and uses those patterns to transcribe, summarize, describe and answer questions.
>
> The tools are:
> - **Google Gemini**, through my Maricopa district Google account: the Gemini app, Gemini Notebook (formerly NotebookLM), and Gemini in Google Docs, Sheets and Gmail.
> - **Adobe Creative Cloud**, through my Maricopa district Adobe account: Premiere Pro speech-to-text (which types up the recording), Enhance Speech in Premiere Pro, Audition and Adobe Podcast (which cleans up the sound), and Photoshop tools that repair scratches on old photographs.
>
> **What that means for you, in plain words:**
> 1. **Your material leaves my computer.** It is stored on Google’s and Adobe’s servers so the tools can work with it.
> 2. **The tools read and rework your words.** They will type your interview, describe your photographs, and summarize what you said. I will check everything they produce against your actual recording, and I will not let an AI tool change any words that appear as your quotations.
> 3. **I will build a “Teller.”** A Teller is a Gemini Notebook loaded with your interview and the material you approve. It answers questions about your story by quoting and citing that material. **A Teller can still get things wrong.** It can misstate a fact, mix up dates, or say something you never said. Part of my grade is finding and fixing those errors. The Teller is never made public. If you allow it, a short screen recording of it may appear on my class portfolio page.
> 4. **What the district accounts promise.** For school accounts, Google states that Gemini does not use this material to train its AI models and that it is not reviewed by people for that purpose, and Adobe states that it does not train its generative AI on customer content [verify both statements against the current Google Workspace for Education and Adobe for Education terms before teaching]. Like any internet service, these systems can have outages, errors or security incidents.
> 5. **Deleting is possible, but not instant or total.** If you withdraw, I will delete your material from the tools and my files, as described below. The services keep deleted data for a period set by their own policies before it is erased from their systems [verify current retention periods]. Anything classmates have already seen cannot be unseen.
> 6. **AI will never imitate you.** No one in this course will use AI to copy your voice, create a picture or video that looks like you, or make it appear you said something you did not. That is not allowed in this course, even with permission.
>
> **You can say no to the AI part and still take part.** If you agree to the interview but not to AI processing, I will work with your material by hand, without AI tools.
>
> **Who will see your material.** My instructor will see everything I submit. My classmates may see parts of it in the course’s private critique space (a Discord server for our class) only if you say yes to that item. The finished story is shared only in the ways you choose: class only, an unlisted link, or public.
>
> **What I will not ask about.** I will not ask about immigration or legal status, legal cases, health details or criminal history. If you bring them up, I will offer to stop recording, and I will cut them before anything is uploaded to any AI tool.
>
> **Changing your mind.** You may withdraw at any time, before or after the interview, before or after the semester ends, by telling me or my instructor in any way. I will remove your material from Google Drive, Gemini Notebook, my Gemini chat history, my Adobe cloud storage, the class critique space and any posted video, and I will confirm in writing within seven days. If you withdraw, my grade will not suffer; the course has another path for me.
>
> **Questions.** Contact me at [student email] or my instructor at [instructor email]. The Oral History Association’s page for participants, attached, explains your rights in any oral history interview.

**Google Form version:** the same six statements as required checkboxes, a typed full name as signature, the date, and a guardian section that appears when the narrator answers “under 18” [instructor to create from this text]. The form lives in the student’s district Google account, and the response is exported to the 06_Consent folder.

### 2.6 Video-recorded consent (Week 6, start of the interview)

**Instructions to the student (student-facing):**
1. Set up the camera so the narrator’s face and voice are clear. Record in the same session as the interview, before any interview question.
2. Begin with your slate: “This is [your name], interviewing [narrator’s name], at [place], on [day and date], for AVC150 at Glendale Community College.”
3. Hand the narrator the script below, printed in large type, or show it on a screen. The narrator reads it aloud. If reading is difficult for them, you read each sentence and the narrator answers “Yes, I agree” after each one. Both ways count.
4. Stop if the narrator hesitates or says no to anything. Ask what they would like to change. Do not argue or persuade.
5. Save the video as `YYYY-MM-DD_consent_lastname.mp4` in the 06_Consent folder and in a second backup location the same day. Submit it in Canvas only. **Never upload the consent video to Gemini, Adobe AI features, Discord or any other tool.**
6. Narrator under 18: the parent or guardian appears on camera too and says, “I am [name], [relationship], and I agree.”
7. A narrator who does not want their face recorded may hold the signed Consent Affirmation in front of the camera, with only their hands and the page in frame, and read the script aloud [Michelle to confirm this alternative].

**Script the narrator reads on camera:**

> My name is [narrator’s name]. Today is [day and date].
>
> I agree to be interviewed by [student’s name] for a class project in AVC150 Multimedia Storytelling at Glendale Community College, and I agree that this interview may be recorded.
>
> I have read the Project Information Sheet, and I signed the Consent Affirmation.
>
> I understand that after this interview I will receive a Signed Agreement. If I agree in it, my recording, my words, and my photographs and documents will be uploaded to and processed by AI tools called large language models, in Google Gemini and in Adobe software, through [student’s name]’s school accounts.
>
> I understand that these tools may make mistakes, and that no one will use AI to copy my voice or my face.
>
> I know I can skip any question, stop at any time, and withdraw from the project later, even after the class ends.
>
> I agree to begin.

(About 150 words; 60 to 75 seconds aloud.)

### 2.8 Withdrawal procedure (student-facing, on the AI in This Course page)

1. Stop all work with the narrator’s material the day you learn of the withdrawal.
2. Within seven days, delete the material from: Google Drive (and the Drive trash), Gemini Notebook (delete the notebook or the sources), Gemini Apps Activity (delete the chats that contain the material), Adobe cloud documents and libraries, the course Discord server (your posts), and any video host.
3. Email the narrator a confirmation listing what was deleted, and copy the instructor.
4. Message the instructor about your path for the rest of the semester: continue with the Practice Archive (Section 3), or switch to a self-interview. Work already graded keeps its grade; remaining steps are graded on the new material at the same standard.

### 2.9 Rules carried from Version 5 (unchanged)

The narrator menu (family member; community member the student knows; self-interview; a GCC-arranged volunteer if a pool exists [verify]; a public oral history with a confirmed license), sensitive-information rules, third parties, cultural protocols, grief, minors (as narrator and as student) and recording law stand as written in Version 5, Section 4.4. Two edits: “release” now reads “Signed Agreement” everywhere, and the item numbers follow 2.7 (first person for a living narrator is item 6; first person for someone who has died is Part D; end-of-semester plan is item 12). A self-interview student completes the Consent Affirmation, the video consent and the Signed Agreement for themselves; it takes about ten minutes and teaches the process.
