## 3. The Practice Archive (synthetic archive pack for Weeks 1 to 3)

### 3.1 What it is and the rules for it

The Practice Archive is a complete, small story archive for a **fictional** narrator. Every student gets the same pack in Week 1 and uses it to make the mini version of the whole project: collect, build a Teller, make a 30 to 45 second story. Because the narrator is fictional, students can upload everything to Gemini on day one, post anything to Discord, and make mistakes without risk to a real person.

- **Fictional, and labeled.** Every text file opens with: “Practice Archive. Fictional narrator and documents, written with AI assistance and edited by the instructor, for AVC150 coursework. Not a historical record. Do not cite.”
- **Gemini writes; it does not design.** The narrator profile, transcript, letters and documents may be drafted in Gemini and edited by the instructor. **No AI-generated images** are in the pack, and students may not add any. The pictures are real public-domain period photographs from public collections, used as **stand-ins**.
- **Stand-in images are labeled.** Because the narrator is fictional, no photograph shows her or her family. Every image is a period photograph of real people and places, and students must caption it that way on screen or in the credits: “Period photograph, [collection]. Not the narrator’s family.” This is the Archival Producers Alliance practice students will use again in Week 12.
- **No synthetic voice.** The narrator’s audio is read by a human volunteer [instructor to create], who signs the course’s Signed Agreement (Section 2.7) for the recording. AI voice is not used, even for a fictional person.
- **Built-in teaching problems.** The pack contains three machine-transcript errors, one date conflict between the interview and a letter, and one gap the archive cannot answer. They exist so students catch a Teller inventing, and so the fact-check habit starts in Week 2. The key is in 3.8 (instructor only).

**Pack folder (shared read-only from the instructor’s district Drive) [instructor to create]:**
`00_Read_Me` · `01_Interview` (audio excerpts, machine-draft transcript, corrected transcript for E1 only as a model) · `02_Images` (stand-in photographs with source files) · `03_Documents` (photographed handwritten or typed documents, plus typed text versions) · `05_Journal` (empty; for the student’s own item) · `06_Consent` (sample documents, marked SAMPLE) · `07_Production` (A/V script, frame and edit templates) · `Metadata_Starter` (Google Sheet, 3 of 14 rows filled as models)

### 3.2 The narrator (fictional)

**Ada Pearl Hensley Ruiz.** Born 1931 near Sallisaw, Oklahoma. In autumn 1940, when Ada was nine, her family drove west with a borrowed truck and settled at a federal farm workers’ camp near Glendale, Arizona, where her parents picked cotton. Her mother, Opal, bought a treadle sewing machine on installments from the camp store and sewed for other families. In 1950 Ada married Tomás Ruiz, a mechanic she met at a Saturday dance. From 1958 to 1994 Ada ran **Ruiz Alterations**, a one-room tailoring shop in downtown Glendale, with her mother’s sewing machine in the front window. She has one daughter, Marisol. In the fictional interview she is 95 and speaks with her granddaughter, the interviewer.

**The story’s moment:** the night in 1941 when her mother sewed her a dress from flour sacks for the camp dance, the shame Ada felt when another girl laughed, and what she understood years later, sewing her own daughter’s first-day dress in the shop.

**Voice notes for the volunteer reader:** plain, dry humor; Oklahoma vowels without imitation or caricature; pauses before the parts that still hurt. Read exactly what is written, including the false starts.

### 3.3 Interview transcript excerpts

Five excerpts, about 9 minutes of audio in all [instructor to record with the volunteer reader]. “I:” is the interviewer; “A:” is Ada. Timestamps are from the full fictional interview.

**E1 · [00:01:10] Leaving Oklahoma**
> I: Take me to the morning you left.
> A: It was cold, I remember that, because Mama put my brother’s coat on me over my own coat. The truck was my uncle Ray’s, and Daddy said we’d send it back with the first money, which we did, eventually. Everything we owned went in the back. The mattress, the skillet, a crate of jars, and Mama’s sewing machine, which wasn’t hers yet, I’ll tell you about that. No. Wait. That’s wrong. The sewing machine came later. In Oklahoma she sewed by hand. I keep putting that machine in the truck because that’s how I see it. But it came later.
> I: That’s all right.
> A: Memory does what it wants.

**E2 · [00:06:42] The camp**
> A: We got to Arizona the week before Christmas, 1940. I know because there was a little tree in the community hall, a creosote branch somebody had stood up in a bucket, with tin can lids for ornaments. We lived on a wooden platform with a tent over it first, and then they moved us into one of the metal shelters. Hot. Lord, those were hot. There was a water tower you could see from anywhere. That was how you found your way home.
> I: What did the camp sound like at night?
> A: Radios. Somebody always had a radio. And the canal. You could hear the water going by if everybody was quiet, which was never.

**E3 · [00:11:05] The flour-sack dress**
> A: There was a dance every Saturday in the hall. I was ten, almost eleven, and I wanted to go so bad. Mama said I could if I had something decent to wear, and I didn’t. So she took the flour sacks she’d been saving, the ones with the little blue flowers, and she made me a dress. On the treadle machine. She had it by then.
> A: I wore it to the dance, and a girl named Loretta, her daddy was a foreman, she looked at me and said, real loud, “I can still read the Pillsbury on you.” Which wasn’t even true. They were Gold Medal sacks. But everybody laughed, and I went outside and sat by the water tower until it was over.
> I: Did you tell your mother?
> A: No. I told her it was fun. I never told her.

**E4 · [00:15:30] The turn**
> A: I didn’t think about that dress for, oh, thirty years. Then Marisol was starting first grade, and I was making her a dress in the shop, after closing. Blue, with a white collar. And it was taking me three nights, because I kept ripping out the hem. I wanted it perfect. And I was sitting there at Mama’s machine, at midnight, and I thought, she did this. She did this for me, three nights, after picking cotton all day. And I went outside and sat on the step of the shop and cried like I hadn’t since that dance.
> I: What did you do with that?
> A: I finished the hem.

**E5 · [00:19:48] The shop**
> A: Ruiz Alterations, 1958 to 1994. Hems while you wait, that was our sign. Tomás painted it. I kept Mama’s machine in the front window. People thought it was for show. It worked fine. I used it for the buttonholes, because it made a better buttonhole than the electric.
> I: Where is it now?
> A: (laughs) Well. That’s a longer story.

**Machine-draft transcript.** The `01_Interview` folder also holds a speech-to-text first draft of all five excerpts, as Premiere Pro would produce it [instructor to create by running the volunteer recording through Premiere Pro speech-to-text, then checking that the three planted errors appear, adding them by hand if they do not]: “Glendale” as “Glenn Dale” (pack description), “treadle” as “tread all” (E3), and “flour sacks” as “flower sacks” (E3).

### 3.4 Documents and letters

Each document has a typed text version and a photographed version, handwritten or typed by the instructor on plain paper and photographed flat in window light [instructor to create]. No AI images, no artificial aging filters.

**DOC1 · Letter, Opal Hensley to her sister Wilma, 3 November 1940** (handwritten, pencil, one page)
> Dear Wilma, We are in Arizona and have been here three weeks now. The camp is clean and the government man is fair. Ray’s truck made it with one flat tire outside Lordsburg. There is cotton to pick until spring they say. Ada is going to the camp school and minds her teacher. The children here call her Okie but she gives it right back. There is a store that will sell a sewing machine for four dollars down and fifty cents a week. I have my eye on it. If I can sew for the other women I can pay it in a month of Saturdays. Write to me care of the camp office. Your loving sister, Opal

**DOC2 · Installment card from the camp store, 1941** (printed card with handwritten entries)
> CAMP CO-OP STORE · INSTALLMENT RECORD · Customer: Mrs. O. Hensley, Shelter 14 · Item: One treadle sewing machine, used · Price $19.50 · Down $4.00 · Weekly 50¢ · Payments stamped: Jan 11, Jan 18, Jan 25, Feb 1, Feb 8 [the card continues; last stamp Oct 4 1941, “PAID IN FULL”]

**DOC3 · Postcard, Ada (age 12) to her cousin June in Oklahoma, 1943** (picture side blank)
> Dear June, I am in the 6th grade. Mama sews for half the camp now and she made me a blue dress for Easter with a real collar. It is not a flour sack one!! Ha. We have a radio. Daddy says we will have a house by next year. Your cousin Ada

**DOC4 · Shop flyer, Ruiz Alterations, about 1961** (typed and hand-lettered, one page)
> RUIZ ALTERATIONS · Hems While You Wait · Zippers · Buttonholes · Wedding and Quinceañera Dresses Fitted · Mrs. Ada Ruiz, Proprietor · Open Tues to Sat, 9 to 6 · “Your clothes, made to fit you.”

**DOC5 · List, “Things Mama Said,” written by Ada, about 1985** (handwritten, lined paper)
> 1. Measure twice, it’s cheaper than cloth. 2. Nobody looks at a hem but the person who sewed it. 3. You can’t be proud and cold both. 4. A thing that works is pretty enough. 5. Wash it before you cut it.

### 3.5 Stand-in period images (public domain or no known restrictions)

Every image below comes from a public collection whose rights statement allows reuse. The specific item numbers must be chosen and checked by the instructor before the pack ships; each line is marked [verify] until then. Farm Security Administration and Office of War Information photographs at the Library of Congress are generally marked “No known restrictions on publication.” Students credit each image and label it as a stand-in.

| # | Image to find | Source and link | Use in the mini | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| IMG1 | Casa Grande Valley Farms, Pinal County, Arizona, 1940 to 1942 (Russell Lee, FSA cooperative and its members); choose one photograph of shelters or the water tower | Library of Congress, [lot record 2004677759](https://www.loc.gov/item/2004677759) (rights: no known restrictions, confirmed 24 Oct 2026); individual item to choose | The camp: shelters, a water tower | Lot verified; item [verify] |
| IMG2 | Migratory cotton picker, Eloy, Arizona, 1940 (Dorothea Lange, FSA) | Library of Congress FSA/OWI search: [Eloy, Arizona](https://www.loc.gov/pictures/search/?q=Eloy%20Arizona&co=fsa) | Cotton picking; parents’ work | [verify] |
| IMG3 | Farm Security Administration camp for migratory workers near Glendale (Agua Fria), Arizona, about 1940 | Library of Congress FSA/OWI search: [Agua Fria](https://www.loc.gov/pictures/search/?q=Agua%20Fria&co=fsa) | The camp, the community hall | [verify that photographs of this camp exist in the collection] |
| IMG4 | Migrant family and loaded truck on a highway, late 1930s | Library of Congress FSA/OWI search: [migrant family truck](https://www.loc.gov/pictures/search/?q=migrant%20family%20truck&co=fsa) | Leaving Oklahoma (E1) | [verify] |
| IMG5 | A woman at a treadle sewing machine in a camp or farm home, 1930s to 1940s | Library of Congress FSA/OWI search: [sewing machine](https://www.loc.gov/pictures/search/?q=sewing%20machine&co=fsa) | The sewing machine (E3, E4) | [verify] |
| IMG6 | Clothing or a dress made from printed feed or flour sacks, 1930s to 1940s | Library of Congress FSA/OWI search: [feed sack](https://www.loc.gov/pictures/search/?q=feed%20sack&co=fsa) | The flour-sack dress (E3) | [verify] |
| IMG7 | A Saturday dance or gathering in a camp community hall | Library of Congress FSA/OWI search: [camp dance](https://www.loc.gov/pictures/search/?q=dance%20camp&co=fsa) | The dance (E3) | [verify] |
| IMG8 | An irrigation canal in the Salt River Valley, 1930s to 1940s | Library of Congress FSA/OWI search: [irrigation canal Arizona](https://www.loc.gov/pictures/search/?q=irrigation%20canal%20Arizona&co=fsa) | The sound of the canal (E2) | [verify] |
| IMG9 | A small-town main street storefront in Arizona, 1940s to 1960s | National Archives Catalog: [Arizona main street search](https://catalog.archives.gov/search?q=Arizona%20main%20street) (federal works are public domain; check each item); or the Library of Congress [Free to Use and Reuse sets](https://www.loc.gov/free-to-use/) | The shop (E5) | [verify; rights vary by item] |
| IMG10 | Moving-image option: a short public-domain film about cotton farming or migrant workers, 1930s to 1940s | Internet Archive, [Prelinger Archives](https://archive.org/details/prelinger) (search “cotton”) | Optional 3 to 5 seconds of b-roll | [verify the item’s license] |

**Student’s own item:** each student adds one photograph they take themselves of an object from their own home that could stand in for something in the story (a spool of thread, a hem, a radio, a jar), as a composed still life with no people. This is the “collect” step of the mini, and it is the only item a student adds.

### 3.6 Sample consent documents (marked SAMPLE)

`06_Consent` holds completed versions of the Section 2 documents for the fictional Ada Ruiz, each watermarked “SAMPLE · FICTIONAL NARRATOR” in text: a Project Information Sheet, a Consent Affirmation with all lines initialed, a 70-second consent video read by the volunteer reader on camera [instructor to create], and a Signed Agreement with items 1 to 5, 7 (unlisted link, first name only), 8, 9 and 10 marked yes, item 6 marked no, item 11 restricting “the name of the girl at the dance,” and item 12 set to delete. Students read these in Week 1 and use the item 11 restriction in Week 3: the name “Loretta” may not appear in their mini story. This is the first time students honor a restriction.

### 3.7 Teller question bank (for S2 and D2)

Questions the archive **can** answer: Where did Ada’s family live when they arrived in Arizona? What did the water tower mean to her? What was the dress made from? Who laughed at the dance, and what did Ada do? What made Ada remember the dress thirty years later? What was the name of Ada’s shop, and what did the sign say? Why did Ada keep the machine in the window? How much did the sewing machine cost?

Questions the archive **cannot** answer (the Teller should say so): What happened to the sewing machine after 1994? What was Tomás like? What music played at the dance? Did Opal ever learn about the dance? What did Ada’s father think of Arizona?

Questions with a **conflict** in the archive: When did the family arrive in Arizona? (E2 says the week before Christmas 1940; DOC1, dated 3 November 1940, says three weeks.) Did the sewing machine come on the truck? (E1 corrects itself; DOC1 and DOC2 show it was bought in Arizona.)

### 3.8 Instructor key (not student-facing)

| Planted item | Where | What a good student catches | What a Teller tends to do |
|---|---|---|---|
| “Glenn Dale” | Machine draft | Corrects to Glendale against the audio | Repeats the error if the uncorrected draft is a source |
| “tread all” | Machine draft, E3 | Corrects to treadle | Explains “tread all” as if it were a real term |
| “flower sacks” | Machine draft, E3 | Corrects to flour sacks; DOC3 and IMG6 confirm | Describes a dress made of flowers |
| Arrival date conflict | E2 vs DOC1 | Notes both, trusts the dated letter for the date and the interview for the memory; says so in the metadata note | Picks one date without saying there is a conflict |
| Sewing machine on the truck | E1 | Notices Ada’s own correction | Says the machine came from Oklahoma |
| Where is the machine now? | E5 ends unanswered | Teller instruction: “say the archive does not say” | Invents a museum, a daughter’s house or a sale |
| “Pillsbury” vs “Gold Medal” | E3 | Uses Ada’s correction; brand names spoken in a quote are fine | Merges the two |
| Item 11 restriction (“Loretta”) | Sample Agreement | Leaves the name out of the story and the Teller instructions tell it not to name her | Names her |

### 3.9 Building the pack (instructor tasks and time)

| Task | Tool | Estimated time |
|---|---|---|
| Edit the profile, excerpts and documents in this section (or regenerate drafts in Gemini and edit) | Gemini app, Google Docs | 2 hours |
| Recruit a volunteer reader; reader signs the Signed Agreement; record E1 to E5 and the sample consent video | Phone or Audition; phone camera | 3 hours |
| Run the recording through Premiere Pro speech-to-text; confirm the planted errors | Premiere Pro | 0.5 hour |
| Handwrite or type and photograph DOC1 to DOC5 | Phone camera, window light | 1.5 hours |
| Choose, download and check rights for IMG1 to IMG10; write the credit lines | loc.gov, National Archives Catalog, Internet Archive | 2 hours |
| Fill 3 model rows of the metadata sheet; build the folder and share it read-only | Google Drive, Sheets | 1 hour |
| Build the instructor’s own model mini (30 to 45 seconds) for the Week 3 lecture | Premiere Pro | 3 hours |

Item counts: 20 process steps (S1 to S20), 2 projects, 9 discussions (D1 to D9), 2 practices (P3, P4), Setup Check, Syllabus and AI-Use Agreement, 2 ungraded surveys, 5 Studio Sessions (Weeks 6, 8, 10, 12 and 14; live, recorded and required to watch; Week 8’s is a full hour, the others 30 to 45 minutes; days and times TBD).

**Outline alignment:** Unit I (Introduction to Storytelling) = Weeks 1 to 5, with the mini in Weeks 1 to 3 as the overview of the whole process. Unit II (Crafting a Personal Story) = Weeks 6 to 10. Unit III (Creating the Digital Story) = Weeks 11 to 15. The mini touches Unit III skills (archiving, editing) early on purpose; the district note gives faculty authority over sequencing.

**Visual artifact trail:** mini story and its three frames (Week 3) → moodboard (Week 4) → visual field journal (Week 6) → still life in the archive inventory (Week 7) → beat board and style frames (Week 9) → visual treatment (Week 10) → storyboard and animatic (Week 11) → key art (Week 12) → motion test and rough cut (Week 13) → final cut with title sequence (Week 14) → case-study page (Week 15).

### 4.3 Deadline rhythm and calendar (Spring 2027)

**The rhythm.** Midweek cycle: post for critique Wednesday at noon, due Thursday at noon, hard close Friday at noon. Weekend cycle: post Saturday at noon, due Sunday at noon, hard close Monday at noon. Work submitted between the deadline and the hard close loses 10%; nothing is accepted after the hard close. In Canvas, Due = the deadline and Available until = the hard close. Week 1 has one deadline. Modules open Monday at noon, except Week 1 (opens Saturday 16 January) and Week 5 (opens Tuesday 16 February).

### 4.5 Weeks at risk (new or changed)

| Week | Risk | Mitigation |
|---|---|---|
| 1 to 3 | The mini feels like a lot of new tools at once | Every input is supplied; the story is 30 to 45 seconds from three frames; editing template provided; the Week 3 model mini shows the finish line |
| 4 | Thursday to Saturday is two days between deadlines | D3 is light (0.75); S4 work (informal ask, moodboard) can start Monday; Project 1 feedback returns Thursday 11 February at noon, before S4 is due |
| 5 to 6 | Consent now takes three steps, and a narrator may slow them | Information sheet and affirmation go out in Week 5 with a walk-through offer; the affirmation can be an e-signature; the video consent happens inside the interview session |
| 6 to 7 | Narrator cancels or reschedules | As Version 5: tell the instructor by Wednesday 3 March and S6 may be submitted by Monday 8 March at noon with no deduction; self-interview always available |
| 7 | Upload has to wait for the Signed Agreement | Signing session budgeted (0.5); hand transcription of the 10-minute excerpt allowed while waiting |
| 8 | A shifted Saturday deadline right before spring break | D5 is light (0.25 post); the shifted close (Sunday 14 March) keeps the break free |
| 9 | Style frames now share a week with the beat board and script draft | Thumbnails start in Week 8; three frames meet the spec; students already made frames in the mini |

## 5. Assessment plan

### 5.1 Grading groups and weights (Canvas, weighted)

| Group | Items | Points | Weight |
|---|---|---|---|
| Process Work | S1 to S20, every project step, each submitted with process evidence and (where critiqued) three critique comments | 10 each, 200 total | 40% |
| Projects | Project 1: The Mini Living Archive (Week 3); Final Project: The Living Archive (finals week) | 30 each, 60 total | 35% |
| Exercises, Quizzes and Reflections | Setup Check (5); Syllabus and AI-Use Agreement (5); P3 Interview Rehearsal (10); P4 Visual Field Journal (10); D1 to D9 (10 each) | 120 total | 25% |
| Surveys | Mid-semester and course surveys | Ungraded | |

**Late work:** every item has a 24-hour window after its deadline with a 10% deduction; nothing is accepted after the hard close. Exceptions (illness, emergencies, a narrator cancellation) go through the instructor before the hard close. **Revision:** any process step may be resubmitted once within one week of feedback. **One Canvas submission per critiqued item:** the work, a link or screenshot of the Discord post, the feedback received, and the three comments the student wrote (critique record template [instructor to create]). Private material (consent documents, the consent video, recordings, transcripts, Teller links, the archive) goes in Canvas only, never Discord. Every item from the Practice Archive may be posted, because it is fictional.

### 5.3 Process step rubric (S1 to S20; 10 points)

| Criterion | Complete | Partial | Not yet |
|---|---|---|---|
| Checklist items present (4 points; 5 for a step without critique). Evaluates the step’s module objective | 4 (5): every item on the step’s checklist, at the minimum viable spec | 2 (3): most items | 0 to 1: few items |
| Visual or production craft (2 points). Evaluates the step’s module objective | 2: deliberate choices appropriate to the stage (composition, type, color, recording quality, file organization) | 1: some deliberate choices | 0: little evidence of decisions |
| Writing and communication (2 points). Evaluates the step’s module objective | 2: the writing in the step (logline, email, captions, alt text, metadata, annotations, instructions) is clear, aimed at its reader, concise and correct | 1: clear with wordiness or errors | 0: unclear or off-audience |
| Critique (1 point; critiqued steps only) | 1: three comments of 40 to 80 words by the deadline, using I noticed, I wondered, What if, each naming a frame, timecode or line | 0.5: fewer than three, or protocol used loosely | 0: none |
| AI-use log (1 point) | 1: three-field entry complete (or “No AI used this step”) | 0.5: partial | 0: missing |

Step pages may replace the craft and writing descriptors with step-specific ones (for example, S8 grades “Testing and correction” in place of craft); the points and objective tags stay the same.

### 5.4 Project 1 rubric: The Mini Living Archive (30 points)

**Competencies evidenced:** Course Competency 1 (present an overview of storytelling, including elements of the story, the power of the story and a variety of ways to present a story); Course Competency 3 (describe the importance of effectively blending language, image and power in the creation and production of a personal story); Course Competency 8 (use computer hardware and software, tools, commands and procedures to manipulate graphics, video and sound in producing and editing the digital story); Course Competency 10 (archive the story on relevant technology).

| Criterion | Exemplary | Proficient | Developing | Beginning |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Story: one moment and a turn** (Module Objective 3.1; Course Competency 1) | 8: centers one moment from the archive; something changes by the end; every line earns its time; the A/V script’s picture column does work the words do not | 7: focused moment with a turn; minor slack | 5.5: a summary of events rather than a moment, or the turn is unclear | 4: no discernible moment |
| **Archive and Teller** (Module Objectives 2.1 and 2.2; Course Competency 10) | 8: items chosen with purpose; metadata accurate, with the arrival-date conflict noted; alt text specific; Teller instructions define role, voice, scope and the “no answer” response; testing caught at least two planted problems and fixes were retested | 7: complete and accurate; one planted problem caught | 5.5: metadata thin; testing uncritical | 4: archive or Teller missing |
| **Production** (Module Objective 3.3; Course Competency 8) | 8: 30 to 45 seconds; clean narration at a steady level; the narrator clip placed with purpose; motion on stills follows the voice; accurate captions; frames show controlled crop, type and contrast | 7: clear audio and readable frames; minor timing or caption issues | 5.5: noisy audio, arbitrary motion, or uncorrected captions | 4: unusable audio, no captions, or outside the length |
| **Language, image and power** (Module Objective 3.4; Course Competency 3) | 6: every stand-in image labeled on screen or in credits; the Agreement restriction honored (no name of the girl at the dance); AI-use statement specific; the 100 to 150 word reflection names two things the student will do differently with a real narrator | 5: labels, restriction and statement complete; reflection general | 4: a label or disclosure missing | 3: images unlabeled, restriction ignored, or AI use undisclosed |

The Teller is never graded on how fluent it sounds; it is graded on what the student wrote, tested and fixed.

### 5.5 Final Project rubric: The Living Archive (30 points; one criterion per competency)

The Final Project components, scenario and minimum viable spec stand as in Version 5, Section 3.4, with two edits: “release” reads “Signed Agreement,” and the case-study page may include the mini as a short “practice run” section (optional, not graded). The rubric is rebuilt so each competency the Final evidences has one criterion.

| Criterion | Exemplary | Proficient | Developing | Beginning | ILO (GCC to confirm) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Course Competency 2:** voice, place and culture | 3: the narrator’s own words, dialect and place are specific and honored; no visual stereotypes | 2.5 | 2 | 1.5 | TE Creative thinking |
| **Course Competency 3:** language, image and power; rights, consent and AI disclosure | 3: every asset owned, licensed or covered by the Signed Agreement; narrator review documented and honored; stand-ins and any AI-assisted image work labeled; AI use disclosed in credits and statement | 2.5 | 2 | 1.5 | I Information literacy |
| **Course Competency 4:** story craft | 4: a clear question and a turn; the audience understands something differently by the end | 3.5 | 3 | 2 | TE Creative thinking |
| **Course Competency 6:** script edited and finalized | 3: concise script written for the ear; fact check resolved; quotations unaltered | 2.5 | 2 | 1.5 | W Written communication or career readiness |
| **Course Competency 7:** design principles from storyboard to final frames | 4: composition, hierarchy, type and color carried from the style frames; final frames show development from the storyboard; key art legible and well set | 3.5 | 3 | 2 | VC Visual communication |
| **Course Competency 8:** tools for graphics, video and sound | 4: motion, editing and sound mix controlled; loudness on target; accurate captions and description of key visual information; contrast meets WCAG 2.1 AA | 3.5 | 3 | 2 | TE Creative thinking |
| **Course Competency 9:** the finished short digital story | 3: 2 to 4 minutes; titles and end credits designed; the piece holds together as a finished work | 2.5 | 2 | 1.5 | VC Visual communication |
| **Course Competency 10:** archive and Teller v2 | 3: archive organized, described, preserved, backed up (3-2-1); Teller v2 revised and retested; critique names two specific misrepresentations with annotated evidence | 2.5 | 2 | 1.5 | I Information literacy |
| **Course Competency 11:** presentation for peer review | 3: case-study page, maker’s statement and walkthrough explain decisions to an employer audience; the D9 showcase post frames the work | 2.5 | 2 | 1.5 | OC Oral communication |

Total 30. (Exemplary, Proficient, Developing and Beginning are 100, 85, 70 and 50 percent of each criterion, rounded to the half point.)

### 5.6 Competencies and alignment map (for Start Here and the syllabus)

**Official course competencies (MCCCD), as students will see them:**
1. Present an overview of storytelling, including elements of the story, the power of the story and a variety of ways to present a story.
2. Explain the impact of voice, place and culture in storytelling.
3. Describe the importance of effectively blending language, image and power in the creation and production of a personal story.
4. Identify, describe and apply the basic principles, methods and techniques used to craft a personal story.
5. Identify, develop and present the elements of the personal story for peer review.
6. Edit and finalize the story in preparation for the creation of a digital story.
7. Use basic design principles to lay out and produce a storyboard for the personal story.
8. Use computer hardware and software, tools, commands and procedures to manipulate graphics, video and sound in producing and editing the digital story.
9. Edit and produce a short digital personal story.
10. Archive the story on relevant technology.
11. Present the digital story for peer review.

**Alignment map** (process steps that build toward each project in bold):

| Course Competency | Modules | Formative evidence | Summative evidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1, 3 | D1, Syllabus and AI-Use Agreement, **S3** | Project 1 |
| 2 | 4 | **S4** | Final |
| 3 | 2, 3, 5, 8 | D2, D4 | Project 1, Final |
| 4 | 5, 6, 7, 9 | **S5**, **S6**, P3, P4, **S10** | Final |
| 5 | 4, 8, 9, 11 | D3, D5, D6, D7 | (peer review is assessed formatively) |
| 6 | 10, 14 | **S11**, **S12** | Final |
| 7 | 9, 11 | **S9**, **S13**, **S14** | Final |
| 8 | 1, 3, 6, 12, 13 | Setup Check, **S15**, **S17** | Project 1, Final |
| 9 | 13, 14 | **S18**, **S19**, **S20** | Final |
| 10 | 2, 7, 8, 12, 15 | **S1**, **S2**, **S7**, **S8**, **S16** | Project 1, Final |
| 11 | 13, 15 | D8, D9 | Final |

Orphan check: every competency has assessed evidence, and every graded item maps to one competency.

### 5.7 Discussion rubric (D1 to D9; 10 points) and practice rubric (P3, P4; 10 points)

Discussions keep the Version 5 page format (Prompt, Deadlines, Your post, Critique, Canvas record, Instructor role) and the Version 5 criteria, rescaled to 10 points: Post substance and visual evidence 4 · Critique comments 4 · Design and story vocabulary 1 · Clarity and mechanics 1. In workshop weeks (D6, D7, D8) the post is graded as the step, so the discussion grade covers critique, vocabulary and clarity, rescaled to 10. Practices P3 and P4: Checklist items present 5 · Craft 2 · Writing 1 · Critique 1 · AI-use log 1. Setup Check and the Syllabus and AI-Use Agreement: complete (5) or incomplete (0), with feedback.

From Week 4 you make the full version with a real narrator.”
- **How Deadlines Work** keeps the Version 5 text, adds the Spring 2027 calendar (4.3) with dates, and adds: “Two weekend deadlines move one day earlier so that nothing is due or closing on a day the college is closed: Week 4 (due Saturday, February 13) and Week 8 (due Saturday, March 13).” The late-window paragraph ends “what a good member of a creative community does.”
- **AI in This Course** adds the course-notes statement (Section 1), the plain explanation of what large language models do with a narrator’s material (from 2.4), the consent sequence table (2.2), the upload rule, the withdrawal procedure (2.8), and the rule “Gemini writes; it does not design”: Gemini may brainstorm, draft and tighten writing, draft metadata and alt text, rehearse interviews, transcribe, and power the Teller; students make every visual (frames, moodboards, journal pages, storyboards, key art) from archive material and their own drawing, photography and footage. No AI image generation for design work.
- **Practice Archive Read Me** is a new Getting Started page that links the pack (Section 3) and the sample consent documents.
- **Glossary** adds: Practice Archive; stand-in image; large language model; Consent Affirmation; video-recorded consent; Signed Agreement; withdrawal; process step. It drops: generative credits, Content-Aware Fill (moved to Week 12 pages), Audio and Video Overviews.

### 7.2 Weeks 1 to 3 (full Canvas-ready text)

---

#### Week 1 · Instructor notes (unpublished page)

- **Competencies:** 1 (overview of storytelling); 8 (tools and setup).
- **Recording plan:** Welcome and course tour (6 to 8 minutes): the course path (the mini on the Practice Archive in Weeks 1 to 3, then the full story with a real narrator); a direct statement that this course requires AI and that disclosed use inside the rules is expected; the consent sequence in one minute (affirmation, video, signed agreement), so students know what they will ask a narrator in Week 5; How Deadlines Work. Lecture 1.1 (three segments of 12 to 15 minutes): a story versus a topic; who tells and who listens; forms of presenting a story, closing with *Artificial* and a two-minute preview of the Practice Archive. Setup demonstration (four segments of about 15 minutes): Creative Cloud at home; Gemini, Gemini Notebook and Gemini in Docs, Sheets and Gmail with the district account; opening the Practice Archive folder and adding a shortcut to your Drive; the Chromebook and phone backup path; joining the Discord server. Setup FAQ video (about 15 minutes) [instructor to create].
- **RSI cadence:** Saturday 16 January at noon: welcome announcement (captioned video). Comment on every D1 post by Thursday 21 January at noon. Thursday afternoon: message anyone without the Setup Check, naming the Friday 22 January noon hard close. Friday summary announcement. No announcement on Monday 18 January (college closed); the welcome covers the week.
- **Seat time:** Direct 3.0 (welcome 0.5, lecture 0.75, setup demo 1.0, FAQ video 0.25, D1 critique 0.5). Outside 5.0 (Getting Started pages including the sample consent package 1.5; *Artificial* block 1 1.5; *Cookbook* Ch. 1 with one story 0.5; Practice Archive Read Me and profile 0.5; Setup Check with the D1 post 1.0).
- **Common pitfalls:** personal Google accounts instead of district accounts; students who cannot join Discord (route them to the Canvas discussion fallback without comment); students who think the Practice Archive narrator is real (the Read Me says otherwise; repeat it in the welcome); alt text that repeats the caption. *Artificial* page blocks must be set with the book in hand.

---

**[Title box] Week 1 · Getting Started: What Is a Story?**

**Objectives**

Module 1 objectives. By the end of this module, you will be able to:
- 1.1 Set up your district Google account, Adobe Creative Cloud, a recording method and Discord for the semester (Course Competency 8)
- 1.2 Distinguish a story from a topic, and identify what makes a told story memorable (Course Competency 1)
- 1.3 Describe the semester project, its three story forms, the deadline rhythm, and how AI and consent work in this course (Course Competency 1)

**Overview**

This course is about finding a story that is personal to you, though not necessarily about you, and telling it with pictures, sound and words. You will also build an AI **Teller**: a tool that answers questions about a story using only the material you give it.

You will make the whole project twice. In Weeks 1 to 3 you make a complete small version, called the **Mini Living Archive**, from the **Practice Archive**: a set of interview recordings, letters, documents and period photographs about a fictional narrator named Ada Ruiz. Everyone in the class uses the same Practice Archive, so you can try every tool, post everything for critique, and see the finished product before you ask a real person for their story. From Week 4 you make the full version: a 2 to 4 minute story from a real interview, in one of three forms (animation or motion comic; illustrated or photo documentary; live-action or archival video).

This course requires AI tools from Google Gemini and Adobe Creative Cloud, used through your Maricopa accounts. You will disclose your AI use in a short log. When you work with a real narrator, they will give consent three ways (a signed affirmation, a short video statement, and a signed agreement) before anything of theirs goes into an AI tool. You will read sample versions of those documents this week.

This week has one deadline: Thursday, January 21, at noon. Read How Deadlines Work before anything else.

*Content note:* this course includes stories about family, migration, death and grief, and a book about an AI built from the writing of someone who has died. Support is available through [GCC Counseling](https://www.gccaz.edu/) [link to confirm] and the [Dean of Students](https://www.gccaz.edu/) [link to confirm]. You may always choose a different story.

**Watch**
- Welcome and Course Tour: the course path, the Practice Archive, how deadlines and critique work, how AI and consent work in this course, and where to find help.
- Lecture 1.1, What Is a Story? Segment 1: a story versus a topic (“my grandmother” is a topic; “the summer she taught me to drive her truck” is a story). Segment 2: who tells, who listens, and why stories last. Segment 3: ways to present a story, from spoken and drawn to video and AI-assisted, and an introduction to *Artificial: A Love Story* and the Practice Archive.
- Setup Demonstration: installing Adobe Creative Cloud; signing in to Gemini and Gemini Notebook with your district account; opening the Practice Archive; the Chromebook and phone backup path; joining the course Discord server; recording clean audio on a phone; writing a caption and alt text.
- Setup FAQ video.

**Read**
- How Deadlines Work; AI in This Course; Practice Archive Read Me (Getting Started).
- Sample consent documents for the Practice Archive narrator (Getting Started, AI in This Course page). Read them now; you will use the real versions in Weeks 5 to 7.
- Amy Kurzweil, *Artificial: A Love Story* (Catapult, 2023), block 1 [pages to be set by the instructor]. Book page: [amykurzweil.com/artificial-a-love-story](https://amykurzweil.com/artificial-a-love-story/).
- Joe Lambert et al., [*Digital Storytelling Cookbook*](https://www.storycenter.org/s/cookbook.pdf), Chapter 1, “Stories in Our Lives,” pages 1 to 8 (free; CC BY-NC-SA 2.5), and StoryCenter, [“Fences” by Emily Love](https://www.storycenter.org/stories/v/fences-by-emily-love).
- Optional: NPR, [“Using AI, cartoonist Amy Kurzweil connects with deceased grandfather in *Artificial*”](https://www.npr.org/2023/10/19/1205898776/new-yorker-cartoonist-amy-kurzweils-graphic-memoir-artificial-a-love-story) (about 5 minutes, with transcript).

**Practice**

*Setup Check* (5 points). This assignment measures Module Objective 1.1, which supports Course Competency 8.
1. Install Adobe Creative Cloud on your computer with your GCC account, or, if you work on a Chromebook or phone, choose the backup path in the Software and Recording Setup Guide.
2. Join the course Discord server, or tell the instructor that you need the Canvas discussion option.
3. Sign in to Gemini and Gemini Notebook with your district Google account. Report which of these open for you: the Gemini app, Gemini Notebook, Gemini Live, and Gemini in Docs, Sheets and Gmail. Whatever opens, the report is complete; it helps the instructor support you.
4. Open the Practice Archive folder from the Read Me page and add a shortcut to it in your Google Drive. Confirm that you can play one audio excerpt and open one document.
5. Record a 30-second audio clip in the quietest space available to you (a closet full of clothes or a parked car works well), introducing yourself and naming one place that matters to you.
6. Photograph or draw that place. Write a one-sentence caption and a one-sentence alt text. Alt text describes what is in the image for someone who cannot see it; a caption tells the viewer what it means. This image is also your D1 post.
7. Submit your tool path, the story form you are leaning toward (you may change it until Week 4), your feature report, the audio file, and the image with its caption and alt text, together with your D1 critique record.

**Assessment**

*Syllabus and AI-Use Agreement* (5 points; no critique). This assignment measures Module Objective 1.3, which supports Course Competency 1.
1. Read the syllabus, How Deadlines Work, AI in This Course, the Practice Archive Read Me, the sample consent documents and the glossary.
2. Complete the syllabus check.
3. Sign the AI-Use Agreement, which confirms that you will keep a short AI-use log, disclose AI use in your projects, follow the upload rule for any real narrator’s material, and never use AI to imitate a real person’s voice or likeness.

**Critique / Discussion**

*D1 Introductions: A Story Someone Told You* (10 points). This assignment measures Module Objective 1.2, which supports Course Competency 1.
- *Prompt:* Post your Setup Check image of a place that matters to you. Introduce yourself: your name, your program, and one thing you hope to make this semester. Then share a story someone once told you that you still remember. What made it stay with you: the voice, the place, a detail, or the way it was told?
- *Your post:* the image with 100 to 200 words, or a 2-minute audio or video post with captions or a transcript, in Discord #d1-introductions.
- *Critique:* comment on at least three classmates’ posts by Thursday at noon, naming one thing that made the story memorable and one thing the image shows.
- *Instructor role:* comments on every post by Thursday at noon; posts a Friday summary naming the story elements that appeared across the class.

#### Week 2 · Instructor notes (unpublished page)

- **Competencies:** 10 (archive the story); 1; 3 (through D2).
- **Recording plan:** Lecture 2.1 (three segments of about 15 minutes): what an archive is (preserved files, descriptions and consent records) and how a Teller differs (one way to search it); Lambert’s Seven Steps, with the Practice Archive as the example; metadata, file naming, alt text, and why a machine transcript is a first draft (play the E3 audio against the machine draft and find “flower sacks”). Guided lab (three segments of about 15 minutes, with a written checklist and pause points): creating a Gemini Notebook; loading sources; the instruction scaffold, placed in the notebook’s custom instruction setting or loaded as a “Teller Instructions” source [verify which student accounts support]; rating answers; fixing one problem and asking again.
- **RSI cadence:** Monday 25 January at noon: announcement naming both deadlines. Comment in the S1 channel by Thursday at noon. **S1 feedback by Sunday 31 January at noon; S2 feedback by Wednesday 3 February at noon.** Ask two held-out questions of five practice Tellers in the D2 channel.
- **Seat time:** Direct 2.5 (lecture 0.75, guided lab 0.75, critique for S1 and D2 1.0). Outside 5.5 (*Artificial* block 2 1.5; *Cookbook* Ch. 2 0.75; Library of Congress handouts and WebAIM 0.5; S1 1.25; S2 1.25; D2 post 0.25).
- **Common pitfalls:** treating the Practice Archive as real history; loading the uncorrected machine draft without a label; dates invented for undated items; alt text that starts “image of”; one-line Teller instructions; treating fluent answers as accurate. The key to the planted problems is in Section 3.8. Confirm the *Artificial* passage used in D2 against the book before publishing.

**[Title box] Week 2 · The Practice Archive: Collect and Build a Teller**

**Objectives**

Module 2 objectives. By the end of this module, you will be able to:
- 2.1 Organize and describe archive items with accurate metadata, specific alt text and a corrected transcript excerpt (Course Competency 10)
- 2.2 Build a Teller in Gemini Notebook from an archive, write its instructions, and test and correct its answers (Course Competency 10)
- 2.3 Compare how Fredbot in *Artificial* and a Gemini Notebook answer from an archive, and what each gets wrong (Course Competency 3)

**Overview**

This week you do the first two parts of the project on the Practice Archive: **collect** and **build a Teller**.

An **archive** is the preserved set of files, descriptions and consent records behind a story. **Metadata** is information about an item: what it is, who made it, when, where it came from, and who may use it. **Alt text** is a short description of an image for people who use screen readers; it says what matters in the image for this story. A **machine transcript** is the text a speech-to-text tool produces from a recording; it is always a first draft, and you correct it against the audio before you trust it.

Your **Teller** is a Gemini Notebook (formerly NotebookLM) that answers questions using only the sources you load, with citations, and follows instructions you write. A grounded tool like this can still invent an answer when the archive has a gap. Part of your job is to catch that and fix it. The Practice Archive has a few problems built in on purpose. See how many you find.

You will also read Joe Lambert’s Seven Steps of digital storytelling, the method this course builds on. This week has two deadlines: S1 on Thursday, January 28, and S2 with D2 on Sunday, January 31, both at noon.

**Watch**
- Lecture 2.1, What an Archive Is. Segment 1: the archive and the Teller. Segment 2: the Seven Steps, with Ada Ruiz’s story as the example. Segment 3: metadata, naming, alt text, and checking a machine transcript.
- Guided Lab, Building the Practice Teller (follow along with the written checklist): creating a notebook; loading sources; writing the instruction scaffold; asking and rating questions; fixing a problem.

**Read**
- *Artificial*, block 2 [pages to be set by the instructor]. D2 asks about a passage where Fredbot answers a question.
- [*Digital Storytelling Cookbook*](https://www.storycenter.org/s/cookbook.pdf), Chapter 2, “Seven Steps of Digital Storytelling,” pages 9 to 24.
- Library of Congress, Personal Digital Archiving: [“Scanning Your Personal Collections”](https://digitalpreservation.gov/personalarchiving/documents/scanning_collections.pdf) and the video [“Adding Descriptions to Digital Photos”](https://digitalpreservation.gov/multimedia/videos/personalarchiving-photometadata.html).
- WebAIM, [“Alternative Text”](https://webaim.org/techniques/alttext/), the sections on context and on writing effective alt text.
- Reference: Google, [“Learn about Gemini Notebook”](https://support.google.com/notebooklm/answer/16164461) [verify the page title and address].

**Practice**

No separate practice this week; S1 and S2 are your practice on the Practice Archive.

**Assessment**

*S1 Archive Selection and Metadata* (10 points, Process Work). This assignment measures Module Objective 2.1, which supports Course Competency 10.
1. Open the Practice Archive and read the Read Me page again, including the stand-in image rule.
2. Choose nine items you think could carry a 30 to 45 second story: at least two transcript excerpts, three documents and four images.
3. Collect one item of your own: photograph an object from your home that could stand in for something in Ada’s story (a spool of thread, a hem, a radio, a jar of buttons) as a still life. Use window light and a plain background, fill the frame with what matters, and include no people. Name the file `2027-01-28_still-life_lastname.jpg`.
4. Make a copy of the `Metadata_Starter` sheet into your Drive, and create a folder named `AVC150_Practice_Lastname` with the subfolders 01_Interview, 02_Images, 03_Documents, 05_Journal and 07_Production. Save copies or shortcuts of your ten items there.
5. Correct the machine-draft transcript of excerpt E3 against its audio. Keep Ada’s exact words, including false starts. Mark each change in suggestion mode or in a short list.
6. Complete a metadata row for each of your ten items: title, date (mark a guess as “ca.” and note any conflict you find between items), creator, description (one or two sentences), source and credit line, and, for period photographs, the stand-in label “Period photograph, [collection]. Not the narrator’s family.”
7. Write alt text of one or two sentences for five images, including your still life.
8. You may ask Gemini in Sheets, or the Gemini app with your list pasted in, to draft descriptions and alt text. Check every name and date against the items, and revise the alt text so it says what matters for this story.
9. Post your still life with its caption and alt text, and one metadata row, in Discord #s1-practice-archive by Wednesday at noon. Comment on at least three classmates’ posts by Thursday at noon, with one comment on light or framing.
10. Add a row to your AI-use log and submit your folder link (shared with the instructor), the metadata sheet, the corrected E3 and your critique record.

*Minimum viable spec:* ten items described, one conflict noted if you found one, five alt texts, E3 corrected.

*S2 Practice Teller* (10 points, Process Work). This assignment measures Module Objective 2.2, which supports Course Competency 10.
1. Create a Gemini Notebook named “Practice Teller · Ada Ruiz · [your name].”
2. Load at least eight sources: your corrected E3; the other transcript excerpts you chose (title any uncorrected one “machine draft, uncorrected”); the typed versions of your documents; and your metadata sheet. Do not load the sample consent video; consent recordings never go into an AI tool, even sample ones.
3. Write the instruction scaffold in your own words: Who are you? Whose archive do you hold? What voice do you use? (Use the archivist voice, third person: the sample Signed Agreement marks first person “no.”) What must you never do? (Include the sample Agreement’s restriction on naming the girl at the dance.) What do you say when the archive does not contain the answer? Place it in the notebook’s instruction setting, or load it as a source titled “Teller Instructions.” You may ask Gemini to tighten your wording; record that in your log.
4. Ask ten questions from the Practice Archive question bank (on the Read Me page): five the archive can answer, three it cannot, and two where the archive disagrees with itself.
5. Rate each answer accurate, partly accurate, or invented, in the test log template, and note whether it cited a source.
6. Change your instructions or sources to fix at least two problems, then ask those questions again and log the results.
7. Make a screen recording of about one minute of your Teller answering one question it can answer and one it cannot. You will use it in D2 and Project 1.
8. Share the notebook with the instructor [verify named-person sharing]. Because the Practice Archive is fictional, you may also share it with classmates for D2.
9. Add a row to your AI-use log and submit the share link, your scaffold, the test log, the screen recording and your D2 critique record.

*Minimum viable spec:* eight sources, a complete scaffold, ten questions rated, two fixes retested, a one-minute screen recording.

**Critique / Discussion**

*D2 Fredbot and the Practice Teller* (10 points). This assignment measures Module Objective 2.3, which supports Course Competency 3.
- *Prompt:* Post your one-minute screen recording and a photograph of one panel or page from *Artificial* (with the page number) where Fredbot answers a question [instructor to confirm a passage in blocks 1 or 2]. In 100 to 200 words: how does Fredbot’s answer work differently from your Teller’s? (Fredbot, as Kurzweil describes it, selects real sentences from her grandfather’s writing; a Gemini Notebook paraphrases and cites.) Which approach would you trust with a real person’s story, and what one instruction would you add to your Teller because of it?
- *Your post:* the recording and photograph with 100 to 200 words, in Discord #d2-fredbot-and-the-teller.
- *Critique:* by Sunday at noon, ask at least three classmates’ Tellers one question each from the “cannot answer” or “conflict” list (open the shared notebook, or ask the owner to run it) and report what happened in 40 to 80 words using I noticed, I wondered, What if.
- *Instructor role:* asks two held-out questions of at least five Tellers in the channel; Monday summary of the most common invention and the instruction that fixed it.

**Due**

[Date box] S1 · Post for critique: Wednesday, January 27, 2027, at noon, Discord #s1-practice-archive · Due: Thursday, January 28, 2027, at noon, in Canvas · Hard close: Friday, January 29, 2027, at noon (10% deduction; nothing accepted after).

[Date box] S2 and D2 · Post for critique: Saturday, January 30, 2027, at noon, Discord #d2-fredbot-and-the-teller · Due: Sunday, January 31, 2027, at noon, in Canvas · Hard close: Monday, February 1, 2027, at noon (10% deduction; nothing accepted after).

#### Week 3 · Instructor notes (unpublished page)

- **Competencies:** 1; 8; 3. Project 1 evidences 1, 3, 8 and 10.
- **Recording plan:** Lecture 3.1 (three segments of about 15 minutes, including the model mini): the micro-story (one item, one moment, one turn); the two-column A/V script (words on the left, picture on the right; the picture column does work the words do not); frames from archive images (crop, composition, type on a frame, a move on a still), and how to label a stand-in image on screen or in credits. Show the instructor’s 30 to 45 second model mini from the Practice Archive [instructor to create] and its A/V script. Demonstration (three segments of about 15 minutes): recording narration in Audition or a phone voice memo; Enhance Speech; Premiere Pro: stills on a timeline, a scale or position move on each, the narrator clip, auto captions and correcting them, a credits card, exporting MP4; a short Adobe Express clip for the Chromebook path.
- **RSI cadence:** Monday 1 February at noon: announcement with the minimum viable spec for Project 1. **S2 feedback by Wednesday 3 February at noon.** Comment on every S3 post before the Friday 5 February close. **Project 1 feedback by Thursday 11 February at noon** (four days), with one sentence on what to carry into the real project.
- **Seat time:** Direct 2.5 (lecture 0.75, demo 0.75, critique for S3 and Project 1 1.0). Outside 5.5 (*Artificial* block 3 1.5; StoryCorps and Pixar in a Box 0.5; S3 1.25; Project 1 2.25).
- **Common pitfalls:** summarizing Ada’s whole life; a narrator clip longer than ten seconds; frames without the stand-in label; using the name the sample Agreement restricts; overbuilding motion (one move per frame is the spec); uncorrected captions. Remind students that no AI-generated or AI-extended images are allowed in the mini.

**[Title box] Week 3 · The Mini Story**

**Objectives**

Module 3 objectives. By the end of this module, you will be able to:
- 3.1 Write a 60 to 90 word micro-story script in two-column A/V format, centered on one moment and one turn (Course Competency 1)
- 3.2 Prepare three story frames from archive images with deliberate crop, composition and on-screen type (Course Competency 8)
- 3.3 Produce a captioned 30 to 45 second story with narration, a narrator clip and motion on stills (Course Competency 8)
- 3.4 Label stand-in images, honor a narrator’s restriction, and disclose AI use accurately (Course Competency 3)

**Overview**

This week you finish the mini version of the semester project: a very short story from the Practice Archive, plus your Teller. Together they are **Project 1: The Mini Living Archive**, a small version of the Living Archive you will make from a real interview by the end of the semester.

A **micro-story** centers on one item and one moment, and something changes by the end (the **turn**). An **A/V script** has two columns: audio on the left (narration, the narrator’s recorded voice, sound) and picture on the right (which image, how it is framed, how it moves, any words on screen). A **frame** is a finished still that shows exactly how a moment in the story will look.

Because Ada Ruiz is fictional, no photograph in the Practice Archive shows her. Every period photograph stands in for her world, and you label it that way. This is the same honesty you will practice with real archival images later in the semester. This week has two deadlines: S3 on Thursday, February 4, and Project 1 on Sunday, February 7, both at noon.

**Watch**
- Lecture 3.1, The Micro-Story and the Frame, with the instructor’s model mini. Segment 1: one item, one moment, one turn. Segment 2: writing in A/V format. Segment 3: frames from archive images, and labeling a stand-in.
- Demonstration: recording narration; Enhance Speech; moving a still on a timeline; placing a narrator clip; captions; a credits card; exporting.

**Read**
- *Artificial*, block 3 [pages to be set by the instructor].
- StoryCorps, [“Sundays at Rocco’s”](https://storycorps.org/animation/sundays-at-roccos/), first minute: notice what the picture does while the voice talks.
- Pixar in a Box, [Storytelling unit](https://www.khanacademy.org/computing/pixar/storytelling), “Visual Language” lesson [verify lesson name].
- The Project 1 assignment page and rubric.

**Practice**

No separate practice this week.

**Assessment**

*S3 Mini Script and Frames* (10 points, Process Work). This assignment measures Module Objective 3.1, which supports Course Competency 1.
1. Choose one moment from the Practice Archive. Strong choices: the dance (E3), the hem at midnight (E4), the water tower (E2), the machine in the window (E5).
2. Write 60 to 90 words of narration in the A/V script template. Write it in your own voice, as the person telling Ada’s story. In the left column, place one clip of Ada’s recorded voice, 5 to 10 seconds long, with its timestamp. In the right column, name the image, the crop, the move and any on-screen words for each line.
3. Build three frames at 1920 x 1080 pixels in Photoshop (or Adobe Express on the backup path) from the period photographs, the photographed documents and your own still life. Make deliberate choices about crop and composition. Put on-screen type on at least one frame (a title, a date or a short quotation) at a size and contrast that reads on a phone.
4. Plan the stand-in label for each period photograph: on screen in a lower corner, or in the credits.
5. Honor the sample Agreement: do not name the girl at the dance.
6. Use no AI-generated or AI-extended images. You may ask Gemini to suggest cuts to your narration; the wording you keep is yours, and Ada’s quotations are never changed.
7. Post the script and the three frames in Discord #s3-mini-script by Wednesday at noon. Comment on at least three classmates’ posts by Thursday at noon, with one comment on the picture column.
8. Add a row to your AI-use log and submit the script, the frames as PNG files and your critique record.

*Minimum viable spec:* a 60 to 90 word A/V script with one timestamped narrator clip; three frames, one with type.

*Project 1: The Mini Living Archive* (30 points, Projects). This assignment measures Module Objectives 3.1 to 3.4, which support Course Competencies 1, 3, 8 and 10.

*Scenario:* A community history group in the Phoenix area wants to invite families to turn their photographs and stories into short videos and searchable AI Tellers. Before it asks a single family, it is testing the process on a fictional archive. Your Mini Living Archive is that test: a short story, a working Teller, and an honest account of what went wrong and what you would do differently with a real person.

*Steps:*
1. Revise your script and frames using your S3 critique and the instructor’s comments.
2. Record your narration in the quietest space available to you. Clean it with Enhance Speech if needed.
3. Edit a 30 to 45 second story in Premiere Pro (or Adobe Express on the backup path): your three frames, one move on each (a slow push, pull or pan), your narration, Ada’s 5 to 10 second clip, and, if you like, a sound you record yourself (a sewing machine, running water, a radio). Add captions and correct them against the audio. End on a credits card: your name, “Narrator’s voice: Practice Archive volunteer reader,” the credit and stand-in label for each period photograph, and an AI line (for example, “Transcript drafted with Adobe Premiere Pro speech-to-text and corrected by the author”). Export as MP4.
4. Add your final script to your Teller as a source. Ask it two new questions, one it can answer and one it cannot, and make a 60 to 90 second screen recording.
5. Write a 100 to 150 word reflection: name two things you will do differently when the archive belongs to a real person, using evidence from your Teller test log or your edit.
6. Complete the full AI-use statement (log summary; where AI helped or got it wrong and how you could tell; what is yours; credit line).
7. Post the story in Discord #project-1-mini by Saturday at noon. Comment on at least three classmates’ stories by Sunday at noon, with one comment on timing and one on a frame.

*Submit:* the MP4; the A/V script; the three frames; the metadata sheet with any updates; the Teller share link, the S2 test log with any new entries, and the screen recording; the reflection; the AI-use statement; your critique record.

*Minimum viable spec:* a 30 to 45 second captioned MP4 with narration, one narrator clip, three frames with a move each, labeled stand-ins and a credits card; a Teller with two new logged questions and a screen recording; the reflection; the statement.

*Rubric:* Section 5.4 (four criteria, 30 points).

**Critique / Discussion**

Critique this week happens inside S3 and Project 1. There is no separate discussion. Use the Course Questions channel for questions.

**Due**

[Date box] S3 · Post for critique: Wednesday, February 3, 2027, at noon, Discord #s3-mini-script · Due: Thursday, February 4, 2027, at noon, in Canvas · Hard close: Friday, February 5, 2027, at noon (10% deduction; nothing accepted after).

[Date box] Project 1 · Post for critique: Saturday, February 6, 2027, at noon, Discord #project-1-mini · Due: Sunday, February 7, 2027, at noon, in Canvas · Hard close: Monday, February 8, 2027, at noon (10% deduction; nothing accepted after).

#### Week 4 · Instructor notes (from Version 5 Week 3)

- **Competencies:** 2 (voice, place and culture); 5 (peer review, through the D3 Story Circle).
- **Recording plan:** Version 5 Lecture 3.1 (voice; place as a character; culture and language, including a narrator’s own words, dialect and Spanish; the danger of a single story), plus a short segment on pitching and the one-minute AI explanation (2.3), with a model conversation. Demonstration (about 30 minutes): building a moodboard from the board template in Adobe Express or InDesign from your own photographs and archive and licensed images; captioning it; tightening a pitch with Gemini without turning it into someone else’s story; recording ambient sound on a phone. No AI image generation.
- **RSI cadence:** Monday 8 February at noon: announcement naming the shifted Saturday deadline. **Project 1 feedback by Thursday 11 February at noon.** Comment on every D3 logline by Thursday at noon. **S4 feedback by Tuesday 16 February at noon** (Monday 15 February is Presidents’ Day): approve or redirect the story, form and narrator. Private follow-up on any pitch involving a minor, a recent death, a community-held story or sensitive material.
- **Seat time:** Direct 2.5. Outside 5.5 (see 4.2).
- **Common pitfalls:** stories too large; narrators not yet asked; skipping the AI explanation in the informal ask; moodboards with no point of view; people lists posted to Discord (Canvas only).

**[Title box] Week 4 · Voice, Place and Culture: The Pitch**

**Objectives** · Module 4 objectives. By the end of this module, you will be able to:
- 4.1 Present a story idea to peers for feedback, and respond to theirs in writing (Course Competency 5)
- 4.2 Explain how voice, place and culture shape your story in a pitch package: logline, pitch, form, moodboard, narrator and consent plan (Course Competency 2)

**Overview** · Now the real project begins. You have made a complete small story; this week you choose the real one. You will listen to how real people talk about real places, share three loglines in a Story Circle, ask your narrator informally whether they are willing to take part (including the one-minute explanation of how AI tools will be used), and propose your story in a pitch package. A **logline** is one sentence that makes someone want to see a story: whose it is, the moment, and what is at stake. A **moodboard** is a curated set of images that shows the look and feeling of a piece before it is made. This week has two deadlines: D3 on Thursday, February 11, and S4 on **Saturday**, February 13, both at noon. S4 is a day early because the college is closed Monday, February 15.

**Watch** · Lecture 4.1, Voice, Place and Culture (four segments) and the pitching segment · Demonstration: moodboard, pitch, ambient sound.

**Read** · *Artificial*, block 4 (you finish the book this week) · StudioBinder, [“What Is a Mood Board”](https://www.studiobinder.com/blog/what-is-a-mood-board/) · Pixar in a Box, [Storytelling unit](https://www.khanacademy.org/computing/pixar/storytelling), “Pitching” lesson · Chimamanda Ngozi Adichie, [“The Danger of a Single Story”](https://www.ted.com/talks/chimamanda_ngozi_adichie_the_danger_of_a_single_story) · StoryCorps animations [“Mi Abuela Panchita”](https://storycorps.org/animation/mi-abuela-panchita/) and [“Facundo the Great”](https://storycorps.org/animation/facundo-the-great/).

**Practice** · No separate practice; the Story Circle (D3) is your practice.

**Assessment** · *S4 Pitch Package* (10 points, Process Work). This assignment measures Module Objective 4.2, which supports Course Competency 2.
1. Choose your narrator from the narrator menu on the AI in This Course page. You do not need to give a reason for your choice.
2. Have a short, informal conversation with your narrator. Explain the project and cover the five points of the one-minute AI explanation (AI in This Course page). Ask whether they are willing to read an information sheet. Write three or four sentences about how it went, including any question they asked about AI. (Self-interview: write a short paragraph about the place, object or memory you will explore.)
3. Write a logline (25 words or fewer) and a pitch of 100 to 120 words: whose story it is, the one moment it will center on, and why it matters to you. Revise using at least one comment from the Story Circle.
4. Choose your story form: animation or motion comic; illustrated or photo documentary; or live-action or archival video. If this is your first time editing video, the illustrated or photo documentary form is recommended.
5. Build a moodboard of 10 to 16 images from the board template: your own photographs, images you already have from your narrator (shown to the class only if your narrator agrees), and licensed or public-domain images, each with its source. No AI-generated images.
6. Write a 50 to 80 word caption for the moodboard naming the palette, the type direction, the light and the feeling you are after.
7. In Canvas only: list every person whose voice, image or story will appear; name one risk (access, time, sensitive material) and your plan for it; and note your narrator’s answer about AI tools.
8. Post the logline, pitch and moodboard in Discord #s4-pitch by Friday at noon. Comment on at least three classmates’ packages by Saturday at noon.
9. Add a row to your AI-use log and submit the Canvas record.

**Critique / Discussion** · *D3 Story Circle: Whose Story, Which Moment?* (10 points). This assignment measures Module Objective 4.1, which supports Course Competency 5.
- *Prompt:* Post three loglines for three possible stories, each with one quick study: a thumbnail sketch or a phone photograph (no AI images). Mark the one you are leaning toward. In your comments, use I noticed, I wondered, What if to tell classmates what you most want to know and which logline fits 2 to 4 minutes best.
- *Your post:* three loglines and studies, with up to 100 words of context, in Discord #d3-story-circle.
- *Critique:* comments on at least three classmates’ posts by Thursday at noon, so each maker can use them in S4.
- *Instructor role:* comments on every post by Thursday at noon on scale and focus.

**Due** · [Date box] D3 · Post: Wednesday, February 10, 2027, at noon · Due: Thursday, February 11, at noon · Hard close: Friday, February 12, at noon. · [Date box] S4 · Post: **Friday**, February 12, 2027, at noon, Discord #s4-pitch · Due: **Saturday**, February 13, at noon · Hard close: **Sunday**, February 14, at noon (10% deduction; nothing accepted after).

#### Week 5 · Instructor notes (from Version 5 Week 4)

- **Competencies:** 3 (language, image and power); 4 (methods: the interview guide).
- **Recording plan:** Lecture 5.1 (four segments): McCloud’s word and picture combinations; power: whose story it is and who holds the camera or the stylus; consent as part of the craft; **what a large language model does with a narrator’s material**, in plain terms, walking through the AI section of the Project Information Sheet line by line, including what the district accounts promise and what deletion can and cannot do [verify current terms before recording]. Demonstration (about 30 minutes): reading Content Credentials on the instructor’s labeled AI images; writing the narrator email from the template; walking a narrator through the information sheet (a model role-play); sending the Consent Affirmation as a Google Form.

**[Title box] Week 5 · Language, Image and Power: Consent and AI**

**Objectives** · Module 5 objectives. By the end of this module, you will be able to:
- 5.1 Compare an AI-generated image with an archival photograph in what each claims and whose view it represents (Course Competency 3)
- 5.2 Prepare an interview guide of open, scene-seeking questions (Course Competency 4)
- 5.3 Explain in plain language how AI tools will process a narrator’s material, and obtain the narrator’s signed Consent Affirmation (Course Competency 3)

**Overview** · This module treats consent as part of the craft of telling a real person’s story. You will treat your narrator the way a professional treats a client: with a clear explanation of what you will make, how it will be shown, and what will happen to their words and photographs, including that they will be uploaded to and processed by large language models in Google Gemini and Adobe tools. Your narrator signs a **Consent Affirmation** this week, before the interview. On interview day they will read a short consent statement on video, and after the interview they will sign the **Signed Agreement**, which lists each use for a yes or no. Nothing of theirs goes into an AI tool until that agreement is signed. This week opens Tuesday, February 16, and has two deadlines: D4 on Thursday, February 18, and S5 on Sunday, February 21, both at noon.

**Watch** · Lecture 5.1 (four segments) · Demonstration: Content Credentials; the narrator email; the information sheet walk-through; sending the Consent Affirmation.

**Read** · Scott McCloud, *Understanding Comics*, Chapter 6, “Show and Tell” (library e-reserve [link to confirm]) · Oral History Association, [“For Participants in Oral History Interviews”](https://oralhistory.org/wp-content/uploads/2025/12/2025-OHA-PrinciplesBP_For-Participants-in-OH-Interviews.pdf) (you will send it to your narrator) · Smithsonian, [*Folklife and Oral History Interviewing Guide*](https://folklife.si.edu/the-smithsonian-folklife-and-oral-history-interviewing-guide/smithsonian), sample forms, for reference · Helen Rosner, [“The Ethics of a Deepfake Anthony Bourdain Voice in ‘Roadrunner’”](https://www.newyorker.com/culture/annals-of-gastronomy/the-ethics-of-a-deepfake-anthony-bourdain-voice) (may be metered) · Interview readings moved from Version 5 Week 5: Nate Rott, [“Feign ignorance, demystify the mic and other audio interview tips”](https://www.npr.org/sections/npr-training/2025/05/28/g-s1-64689/feign-ignorance-demystify-the-mic-and-other-audio-interview-tips); Dana Lynn Driscoll, [“Introduction to Primary Research”](https://pressbooks.gvsu.edu/writingspaces2/chapter/introduction-to-primary-research-observations-surveys-and-interviews/), interview sections; Global Center for Journalism & Trauma, [“Interviewing in the aftermath of trauma”](https://gcjt.org/resources/interviewing-aftermath-trauma) · Model interviews: StoryCorps [“Q & A”](https://storycorps.org/animation/q-and-a/) and [“No More Questions!”](https://storycorps.org/animation/no-more-questions/).

**Practice** · No separate practice; the image analysis for D4 is your practice.

**Assessment** · *S5 Interview Guide and Consent Affirmation* (10 points, Process Work). This assignment measures Module Objective 5.2, which supports Course Competency 4.
1. Use StoryCorps [“Great Questions”](https://storycorps.org/participate/great-questions/) and your S4 pitch to ask Gemini for 20 candidate questions. Cut them to 10 to 12 open questions that ask for scenes (“Take me to the day…”), ordered from easy to deeper, with one planned follow-up each. Include two questions that ask your narrator to describe what something looked like, because those answers become pictures. End with “Is there anything I didn’t ask that I should have?”
2. Check the guide against the interview rules: no questions about immigration or legal status, legal cases or health details.
3. After your S4 feedback (Tuesday, February 16), write a project email or message to your narrator of 150 to 200 words from the template: what you are making, what you will record, how long it will take, how the finished piece will be shown, that AI tools will be used with their material if they agree, and that they may say no to anything. Gemini may help you tighten the message; it must still sound like you.
4. Send the email with the Project Information Sheet (complete every bracketed field; do not remove or shorten any section) and the OHA “For Participants” page.
5. Offer a 15-minute walk-through of the information sheet by phone, video call or in person. Read the AI section together. Write down any question your narrator asks and your answer. If you cannot answer a question, ask the instructor before the interview.
6. Collect the signed **Consent Affirmation** (Google Form from the template, or a photograph of a signed page; a guardian co-signs for a narrator under 18). Self-interview: complete it for yourself.
7. Schedule the interview for 20 to 30 minutes, in the quietest place available, between Tuesday, February 23, at noon and Wednesday, March 3. Record the planned day.
8. Post the guide only (no names or private details) in Discord #s5-interview-guide by Saturday at noon. Comment on at least three classmates’ guides by Sunday at noon, with at least one suggested follow-up question.
9. Add a row to your AI-use log and submit in Canvas: the guide, a copy of your email, your walk-through notes, the Consent Affirmation, the interview day, and your critique record.

**Critique / Discussion** · *D4 Image and Power: The AI Image and the Real Photograph* (10 points). This assignment measures Module Objective 5.1, which supports Course Competency 3.
- *Prompt:* From the instructor’s set of labeled AI-generated images, choose one and pair it with a real archival photograph of a similar subject (from the Practice Archive or a public-domain collection). Inspect the AI image’s Content Credentials. Give each image a one-line caption and a one-line alt text, and give the AI image a disclosure line naming its tool. What does each image claim? Whose view does each represent? Then respond to the Rosner article: where should a storyteller draw the line between illustration and fabrication?
- *Your post:* the two images with 100 to 200 words, in Discord #d4-image-and-power.
- *Critique:* comments on at least three classmates’ posts by Thursday at noon.
- *Instructor role:* mid-cycle post on the Archival Producers Alliance guidelines; Friday summary.

**Due** · [Date box] D4 · Post: Wednesday, February 17, 2027, at noon · Due: Thursday, February 18, at noon · Hard close: Friday, February 19, at noon. · [Date box] S5 · Post (guide only): Saturday, February 20, 2027, at noon, Discord #s5-interview-guide · Due: Sunday, February 21, at noon · Hard close: Monday, February 22, at noon (10% deduction; nothing accepted after).
